# P1b plan: unityrs-batch, the engine

**Status: design round 38, the fold of round 37's review (no HIGH, 1 MEDIUM folded as wording under the convergence rule); the design ready for its PR; before round 22, a reviewed design: 21 rounds, the last with no HIGH or MEDIUM (2026-11-10).** P1b is 2D batch extraction (`docs/DESIGN.md` section 5; called P2 until 2026-11-09, when the phase table gave the 2D work its own phase). This file is the engine's plan: staging, admission, the attempt, the hand-off to sinks, the stop path and refusal. The rest of the phase is `docs/plans/extract.md`, whose requirements rely on sections 1 and 2 here. The exact contract (the spec's fields, keys, identity and order, the spool, the manifest, the zip bytes, the exit codes) is `docs/BATCH_SPEC.md`. The P1b texture build's reviews (2026-11-11) add 2.7's row for `unityrs_texture::DecodeError`, with the detail codes and limit kind it maps to (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3 and 9.3, added to manifest version 1's closed sets).

**What it builds on.** The core API and what it guarantees any batch engine: `docs/plans/core.md` section 4.1, a list of properties each checked by a P1 test. This plan relies on those properties, on the public API named where it is used, and on nothing else the core does. A need the list does not cover is a request to the core (section 6), not an assumption. Each request says what the engine does until the core grants it.

**Citations.**
- P1's review findings are cited by the P1 review round that found them, as `core.md` cites its own. Each problem in section 3 restates what was found, so no review document is needed to read it.
- Design rounds' findings are cited by what they say.
- "core 4.1, *property*" names a row of `docs/plans/core.md` section 4.1 by its first column. "core 3.13" is a section of that plan. A source path without a crate is under `crates/unityrs-core/src/`.
- Corpus figures are aggregates from the private corpus, measured on the P1 core.

---

## 1. Goal

DESIGN.md section 4's `unityrs-batch` row: a JSON job spec of inputs, outputs and resource budgets, run in parallel with bounded memory, streaming outputs to sinks, with the run marked `incomplete` when an input fails.

`docs/plans/extract.md` adds what the engine must give the rest of P1b:
- inputs that each declare their dependencies by id, the spec's whole graph (2.3);
- the own-bundle-first rule for same-named CABs under `EnvOptions::batch()` (3.9);
- a failed dependency failing exactly the inputs that need it (3.10);
- sinks that may hold output across inputs, for the `prefer` winners (3.5).

**Each input decodes its own dependencies.** Each attempt opens the bundles of its view in a dependency cache of its own, so a dependency that N inputs read is opened up to N times and decoded up to N times in what they reach (2.2, its cost). Within one attempt, each bundle of its view is opened at most once per residency in that attempt's cache, and the manifest counts its evictions. DESIGN section 4 says this is version 1's behaviour, and that a shared pre-decoded cache, which keeps D, is planned (section 8).

**Two properties the design keeps.** Every mechanism in section 2 serves one of them or a requirement it names.
- **D, determinism.** An input's records are a function of its staged bytes, the staged bytes of the bundles of its view (2.3), the spec's entries for those bundles, and the spec's other fields. Its records are its outputs and their bytes, its skips, and its failure. They do not depend on:
  - `threads`, or the order inputs run or finish in;
  - what else is in flight;
  - which bundles outside its view failed, or which exist.

  2.10 shows why, against every counterexample earlier reviews built, and names the test. D has three limits, the third until CR2 lands:
  - a failure of the run as a whole stops it instead of deciding a record: a host failure (an I/O error, an allocation the allocator refuses), the spool's cap, or an engine bug (2.7, 2.11);
  - a bundle that could not be staged (2.5: a download that failed after its retries, a local file that is missing) fails the inputs whose view holds it, and that record depends on the network or the host, not on bytes. Its reasons form the `transport` class (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.3), which a caller retries;
  - liblzma allocates through C's `malloc`, which the engine's counting allocator (2.7) does not see; the core reports its refusal as a `Budget` refusal of the meter it decodes for (`codec/lzma.rs`, `failure`). Two such refusals go unseen and can decide a record by the host's memory (A13): (a) one on a dependency's meter, which `with_room` takes for a pool refusal, evicts for and retries; (b) one on any meter, outside `with_room` too (a direct read on a dependency's handle), after a budget refusal absorbed earlier in the same core call (by 2.7's absorbing paths, among them), so the budget's refusal count moved and it reads as a budget refusal. A peek (2.6 step 4) meets neither: its budget's limit is its meter's bound, which the meter checks first (`limits/meter.rs`, `reserve`), so its budget never refuses, and a liblzma refusal there is the allocator's, `host_memory` (2.7).
- **B, the memory bound.** The process's live heap is at most `max_inflight_uncompressed_bytes` plus constants fixed by the spec (section 5). Every byte that grows with an input or an output is charged to a budget before it is allocated.

---

## 2. The design

### 2.1 Phases

1. **Validate** the spec (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 1), on the main thread (2.11's *Threads*). No thread starts and nothing is written. A bad spec writes the `exit` line and exits 78.
2. **Preflight** (E63), in this order. The main thread's steps 3 to 7 (2.11's *Threads*), the deadline's check, the header variables and 2.7's host checks among them; then, on the controller: build the staging client (2.5), read the credentials, and build the controller's and the finisher's store clients (object store, 2.9); start the spool (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.2: dedication, its lock, old journals, cleanup, free space); check the kernel's release, probe `openat2` on the output root, open `_unityrs`, take the root's lock and open `_unityrs/<run_id>` (directory sink, 2.9, `extract.md` E62), or prove the bucket exists (object store); read the stored head (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2), which fences this attempt. A failure exits 69, a stored head that supersedes this attempt exits 7.
3. **Manifest `running`** (E38), fenced. No output is written before it.
4. **Stage** every bundle into the spool (2.5), at most `download_concurrency` at once; at its end, commit the failure record of every input whose view did not stage whole (2.3, rule 1).
5. **Extract**: first the **peek pass**, one peek of each bundle of a view that staged whole (2.6 step 4's table, run as 2.4 says), then the failure records of the inputs whose view holds a peek failure (2.3, rule 1), then every input whose view staged whole and peeked clean (2.4 to 2.8).
6. **Winners** (2.9): a pure function of the committed records.
7. **Publish** (2.9), from the spool, at most `download_concurrency` transfers at once; then, in an object store, verify what was published.
8. **Finish** (2.11): the finisher writes the final manifest and the `exit` line, then exits.

Once the controller is spawned, every end goes through the finisher (2.11): a normal end, a stop, and a 69 or 7 in preflight or at the `running` write, or a 74 at the spool's cleanup or at a directory sink's `running` write, for which it writes no head (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.4). A stop (a signal, the deadline less the grace, the parent's death, stdin's EOF, or a run-level failure) can come in any phase from 2 on.

### 2.2 Sealed attempts

An **attempt** is one run of one input's extraction. Each attempt is sealed: everything that holds memory or counts work during it belongs to it alone.
- **Its budget.** The attempt has one `MemoryBudget` object of its own, built by the engine from atomics. It never blocks, never calls the core and takes no lock (the budget contract, core 4.1; `budget/mod.rs`). It counts its own refusals (2.7). It routes each reservation by its `Reservation`, with one `PermitHome` per `Reservation`, since a release carries no reservation and permits merge by home:

  | `Reservation` | limit | what books with it |
  |---|---|---|
  | `Input` | `M` | the attempt's meter `M_X`: its input's bytes, runs, entries, values, and the engine's buffers (2.6) |
  | `Dependency` | `D` | every meter of the attempt's private Environment: its opens, runs, entries, registry slots and keys |
  | `Provider` | refused | version 1 configures no typetree provider (A8), so nothing books here |

  Every attempt registers its own bundle in its private Environment (2.6 step 4), so a pointer between two containers of X's bundle resolves as UnityPy resolves it, and every attempt's Dependency limit is `D`.
- **Its Environment.** The attempt builds a private `Environment` with `EnvOptions::batch(ctx_X)`, `dep_cache_bytes` set to `D`, and registers its view in it (2.6). No other attempt reads through it. It is dropped when the attempt returns.
- **Its context.** `ctx_X` is `FileContext::unitypy()` (`options.rs`: an empty provider chain, stateless codecs, the AssetBundle read at load) with the attempt's budget and the limits below.
- **Limits.** The engine sets, for every meter of the attempt, and for a peek's (2.6 step 4): `max_input_memory` = `M`, `max_input_bytes` = `M`, and `max_decode_work` = `4 M`, the core's default ratio. The other fields keep `Limits::default()` (`limits/mod.rs`).
- **Admission by reservation.** Every attempt's **slot** is `S = M + D`. The controller runs at most `min(threads, floor(I / S))` attempts at once (2.4). The budget's limits are its slot, so the attempts in flight together can never hold more than `I`, and no attempt's reservation is ever refused for what another holds. A peek (2.6 step 4) takes a slot as an attempt does, and reserves `M` of it; the peek pass ends before the first attempt is dispatched (2.4), so peeks and attempts together never hold more than `I` either.
- **The rest of the run.** Phases 6 and 7 run when no attempt does, under one **phase budget** of limit `I` (2.9).
- **Leak check.** When the attempt's function returns, every local of it is dropped: the bundle, the private Environment, every file, value and pin. Its worker then reads the budget's `Input` and `Dependency` use. Both must be zero. A non-zero use is reported as a run failure (2.4), and the slot stays reserved.
- **Validation** (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.1) refuses a spec unless `M <= D`, `M + D <= I` and `I >= 64 MiB`. The first lets one dependency open at its cap fit the attempt's Dependency budget, its view's registry aside (5.6 sizes for that). The second lets one slot fit. The third gives phases 6 and 7 their working set.

**Library boundary (provisional until B5–B7).**
`unityrs_batch::attempt::run_attempt(AttemptArgs<'_>) -> AttemptEnd` borrows an
ordered staged-path view, compiled export options and member templates, a
successful immutable `ngui::PeekBits` implementation (`PeekTable` retains its
sorted slice adapter, validated once by `PeekTable::new` outside attempts), a
prepared `spool::Directory` (with its shared counter and filesystem facts), limits, the canonical `Control` stop query and hooks. `Prepared::from_spec`
compiles the templates once outside extraction. It drives own open, own metadata,
ordered private registration, `ngui::cut`, disk copy-out and seal. Internal hooks
always count budget refusals and export work. After all charged locals drop it
audits both accounts and the thread's allocator refusals; the fixed-size end
contains state (`Done`, `Failed`, `Stopped`, `RunFailure`), checked reason/detail
codes, a dependency's view index, bounded entry detail, figures and claims, with
no permits or output buffers. A leak stops `internal` and leaves the slot
unreleasable; allocator refusals stop `host_memory`, subject to code precedence.
Run failures call `request_stop` before return. Only `Done` is commit-eligible;
the directory and every partial claim remain the caller's to clean up. Successful
`ms` is sampled at the final seal boundary and preserved in the summary/end;
failed/stopped attempts sample after unwinding and audit. No call commits.

**Preparation and peeks (provisional until B7).** `phase1::prepare`
constructs compiled templates, index tables, atomic peek words, received-fact
slots, clear uncommitted bits, queue capacities and controller walk/active
scratch inside `alloc::measured`, including for `--check`. It retains no input's
whole view and duplicates no bundle-id/path table. All retained structures are
measured; 5.3's additional 256 bytes per bundle remain conservative until B12.
`peek::run_peek` uses an explicit Input-only budget (other reservations,
zero-byte requests included, are refused), writes an individually owned peek
file only for marked rows, drops charged locals and audits before publishing
its word. Run words encode pending, all-clear N, disk N or a fixed failure code
explicitly; received figures remain separate. Both the run-word `PeekBits` and
B4's `PeekTable` use one checked disk loader. The provisional `m_end` is 8 KiB,
with a structural lower bound for the actual end union plus 256 bytes of
channel overhead; B12 owns its measurement.

**Cost.** This is what sealing pays for D.
- **Memory.** Each slot is reserved whole for the attempt's life, used or not. Parallelism is the largest number of slots that fit `I`, at most `threads`. For the corpus example (5.6), 8 slots of `M` = `D` = 256 MiB need `I` = 4 GiB.
- **Throughput.** No dependency is shared between attempts. An attempt reads every other bundle of its view from the spool, whole (`load_dependency` reads a `Source::Path` whole, `env/mod.rs`, `read_path`), and decodes again every block it reaches. Measured on the corpus (A5), on both full builds, build A and build B, and, where marked, on the 551/545-input sample of each:
  - In the 551/545 sample, most inputs have no dependency: the mean view is about 2 bundles, the largest 22. Dependencies concentrate on a few bundles, a handful of which sit in hundreds to thousands of views. On the preview diff below, 1,830 of the 1,869 inputs have dependencies.
  - Version 1 crosses a bundle on one read only: the NGUI cutter's step 1, the name of the material's `_MainTex` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 5). Sprite sheets and `m_Script` stay inside their bundle on the corpus, and no atlas's material is in another bundle: the read that crosses is the `_MainTex` texture's `m_Name`. Measured on views by `m_Dependencies` (A5), 694 atlases in 690 inputs on build A, 693 in 689 on build B and 660 in 656 on the preview diff each decode one dependency block again to read that one name, two distinct blocks a run, so a run decodes +47.6 GB more on build A, +46.2 GB on build B and +45.3 GB on the preview diff: 9.1 and 10.8 times the decode-once work below, and 17.7 times the diff inputs' own 2.55 GB. The larger block decodes at 168 to 170 MB/s on one core and the 2.1 MB one at 331 MB/s, so the reads, each block at its own rate, cost 266 to 280 s of CPU, 33 to 35 s at 8 workers. Every one of them ends in `sheet_not_found`: the name matches none of the input's own textures, and step 2's try of the atlas's name fails too, so on this corpus they buy no output. With the peek table below, this is the only per-attempt decoding left on the corpus (the interim's peeks, 2.6 step 4, find nothing to read there, A5), and what the planned shared cache, CR6, not in version 1, would remove (section 8); CR9, a per-load opt-in decode of only about as much of a dependency member's block as a read needs, its contract its own core PR's (section 6), would cut it to about 0.12 to 0.18 GB a build, from the larger crossed block's CAB entry, its first 0.18 to 0.26 MB (the 2.1 MB block's was not measured), changing, by estimate, no outcome on the corpus; its figures change: `dependency_peak`, the `inputs` rows' `ms` and the head's `vm_hwm`. For each of the 686 inputs (685 on build B) crossing the larger block, the crossed member's share of its `dependency_peak` falls from about 145 MB to about 9 MB (its 8.1 MB file, read whole, plus under 1 MB of prefix and transient, a cut-sized dictionary assumed), and its figure from about its view's staged member bytes (a median 13.5 MB, at most 17.3 MB) plus 137 MB (135 MB) to about those bytes plus 1 MB, the four a build crossing only the 2.1 MB block not measured for room; the head's figure falls from about 154 MB (152 MB) to about 18 MB by `m_Dependencies` views, or to 25 to 31 MB by the manifests' dependency lists, all estimates (section 6).
  - **Registration's peek** (2.6 step 4) asks of each top-level entry whether its first 21 bytes are a bundle's, and on a member of one block it decodes the member whole. 23,060 of the corpus's 23,061 bundles are one block, so the peek, asked in every attempt, decoded every view member whole. Per full build, against a run that decodes each bundle once, it added +93.4 GB (17.9 times) on build A by direct views, and +103.6 GB (24.1 times) on build B by direct views and +255.8 GB (59.6 times) with the closure, each view taken by `m_Dependencies` ("direct": the bundle and the ones its `m_Dependencies` names; the builds' manifest dependency lists give at most 2 % more). On a first consumer's preview diff (build B to build A: 1,869 inputs, its production task count; views by `m_Dependencies`, direct and closure alike) it added 71.5 GB, 28 times the inputs' own decoding: about 113 to 135 s of wall time at 8 workers, at a single-core LZMA rate of 66 to 79 MB/s, where the whole run was expected to take 30 to 60 s.
  - So each bundle's peek is computed once per run, and the attempts read its table (2.6 step 4). On the corpus, whose bundles are one block, that costs about the decode-once work: about 5.2 GB a full build on build A and 4.3 GB on build B, and on the preview diff the inputs' own 2.55 GB and their dependencies' bytes once. That is an estimate for this corpus, not a bound: a peek is bounded by its own `4 M` of decode work, and can decode a block twice (2.6 step 4). Until CR7 the core still peeks in an attempt at a row a later registration's key drops, in the interim's three cases (2.6 step 4), each a decode of its member's block in every attempt whose view holds it; on the corpus none occurs (A5).
  - All of a build's distinct dependencies decode to 262 to 276 MB (measured), which is what the planned shared cache would hold (section 8).
  - The view members' file reads come from the page cache, and 2.5 keeps staged files cached.
- **What it removes.** The shared dependency cache, the Shared and Alone attempts, the Alone retry, the registration phase before extraction, the key table and flagging.

### 2.3 Views

**Views.** `view(X)` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.2) is X and the closure of `deps` through the spec's graph. Every id in a `deps` list names a bundle the spec declares (validation, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.1). `extract.md` E13's "needs" means "is in the view".

**No discovery.** A job declares its complete graph. The engine reads X's AssetBundle object's `m_Dependencies` only to report: a name that is not the id of a bundle of `view(X)` is a diagnostic row (`undeclared_dependency`: the input and the name, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3) and a run count. It is never fetched or opened, and a read that reaches a CAB only it would hold is the object-level skip of rule 2.

The view rules:
1. **A failed member fails X.** A bundle of `view(X)` that could not be staged (2.5), whose peek failed (2.6 step 4), or whose load in the attempt is refused, fails on its bytes, or gives a file that is not a bundle, fails X with `dependency_failed(<id>, <reason>)` (3.10), where the inner reason is the member's own (`not_a_bundle` for the last). The exit code follows the inner reason (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.2). A failure at staging or at a peek fails X before it is dispatched, by this precedence. X's own staging failure gives X's own reason. Otherwise, when `view(X)` did not stage whole, the record names its member with the least id, in bytewise order, among those that failed staging. Either way the record is committed when staging ends (2.4), before any peek, and no peek bears on it: the peek queue holds only the views that staged whole (2.4), so whether another view had X's members peeked cannot change X's record (D). Otherwise `view(X)` staged whole, and X waits for the peek pass: X's own peek failure gives X's own reason, and else the record names the member with the least id among those whose peek failed, committed when the peek pass ends (2.4). So each record is a function of X's own view and the spec. So an attempt never meets a failed peek, and neither X's own steps nor the eviction cap can come first. In an attempt, a load that fails names its member, the least id first, the one registration meets first. An I/O error reading a member's staged file is not a member's failure: it is a host failure and stops the run (2.7).
2. **Reads stay in the view.** The private Environment holds only `view(X)`, so every lookup resolves within it. A read whose CAB no bundle of the view holds is an object-level skip, `outside_view(<cab>)`.
3. **Same-named CABs** resolve by `BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.3: the reading file's own bundle first, then X, then the greatest id. This is a Tier C rule of `EnvOptions::batch()` only.
4. **Size.** Validation refuses a spec in which a view holds more than `max_view_bundles` bundles. A view's walk stops at `max_view_bundles + 1`, and no `deps` list repeats an id or holds more than `max_view_bundles` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.1), so the check makes at most `inputs x (max_view_bundles + 1)` visits, each reading one list. That product, up to about 2^42 ids on a 64 MiB spec of hub-shaped views, is no bound a run can wait for, and no near-linear exact check is known, so the check has a work budget in the contract: it reads at most 2^24 ids, the running sum over the inputs in document order of the `deps` lengths of each view member, and the input at which the sum passes it is `view_size` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.1). The sum is a function of the graph alone, so a caller can compute it, and the walk stops at the first limit passed, so phase 1 reads at most 2^24 + `max_view_bundles` ids. Each id read is a load from the bundles' tables at a place the graph chooses, so the budget, not the document's size, bounds the walk's time. Review round 2 lowered it from 2^30 to 2^28 for a view of 65,535 members scattered among 2,000,000 bundles (16 to 22 s at 2^30). Review round 3 lowered it to 2^26 for the same walk over the 4.7 million id-only bundles a 64 MiB spec holds, a ring whose members each list the next in ascending order of place: 16.9 s at 2^28, 5.7 s at 2^26, about 55 ns an id, an order the hardware's prefetching partly hides. Review round 4 lowered it to 2^24 for rings whose members list one another in a pseudo-random order, which no prefetch follows: 34 disjoint rings of 65,535 over all 2.26 million bundles of a 64 MiB spec, the inputs cycling among them, at 220 to 255 ns an id, 16.7 to 19.0 s at 2^26, 9.7 to 10.3 s at 2^25, 5.8 to 6.0 s at 2^24 (section 8). 2^24 still accepts what the corpus needs: its largest view holds 22 bundles, and a full build's 11,556 bundles, each an input whose 22-bundle view fills every list, read about 5.3 million ids, about 32 % of the budget. 100,000 such inputs read about 46 million, which no longer fits one spec (about 36,000 do). Measured (section 8): a dense spec at the budget's most validates in 0.9 s, and the shape that took 840 s before the budget is refused in 1.4 s. An attempt's own walk of its view keeps at most `max_view_bundles + 1` entries (`C_env`, 5.3). The rule bounds the uncharged heap a private Environment holds per registration (5.3).
5. **Members are bundles.** Every bundle is staged under a spool name (2.5), and the core keys a top-level raw or serialized dependency by its file name (`env/load.rs`, `register_file`), so such a member could never be found by its own name. Version 1 refuses it by rule 1.

### 2.4 The controller

**Local library drive (provisional until B7/B9).** `controller::Controller`
consumes phase-1 state and a trusted `PreparedSpool`; `execution::Execution`
is constructed separately before staging and outlives the drive and handover.
`drive_local` returns `ReadyForWinners` only after every input has a committed
success or failure artifact and every extraction reservation is clear. It
returns `HandOver` on a stop or controller panic, preserving received facts,
uncommitted bits, active owners and the pending commit. Neither boundary
publishes or writes a final head. No drop joins workers or blocking tasks;
cooperative hosts/tests quiesce explicitly. B9 owns process parking and stop
arbitration through the canonical, synchronous `control::Control` interface.

The local controller uses a current-thread Tokio runtime; transport enables the approved `rt`, `net`, `time` and `sync` features, without `full` or a multithread runtime, and uses
`download_concurrency + 1` prewarmed blocking threads with 2 MiB stacks, bounded
worker channels, and end/stop wake descriptors. A completed pool handle is
loaded with `block_on` only after readiness; a held pool call is waited under
the wake descriptors instead. Admission refuses a zero-slot configuration.
Ordinary commits follow receipt order; barrier failures follow bytewise input
id order. Failure writes use the failed slot's released room and prevent refill
until handling finishes; their peak is retained separately as controller work.
Typed hooks observe dispatch/receipt/rename and the
two commit fault points; they do not implement the official trace protocol.


**Workers.** `threads` OS threads, each with an explicit stack of twice `Limits::stack_needed` plus core 3.15's per-level term, `CONTAINER_LEVEL_STACK x (max_container_depth + 1)`, as `unityrs-dump`'s `worker_stack` sizes it. The atlas JSON writer walks a value with a heap stack, not by recursion. **Test:** `the_deepest_value_and_nest_fit_a_worker_stack`, a value at `max_value_depth` and the deepest nest on a thread of `worker_stack`'s size, in debug and in release. Each runs one attempt, or one peek (2.6 step 4), from start to end, on that one thread, inside `catch_unwind`.

**Order.** The ready queue holds every input whose view staged whole and whose view's peeks all succeeded (2.3, rule 1), sorted by the input's own staged file length, largest first, then by bytewise id; an input whose view did not stage whole was committed failed when staging ended, and one whose view holds a peek failure is when the peek pass ends. Largest first shortens the tail. Order changes no record (D). The **peek queue** holds the union of the views of the inputs whose view staged whole, each bundle once, in the same order by that bundle's own staged file length and id: a bundle no such view holds is never opened, as no attempt would open it.

**The end message is total.** Every dispatched attempt, and every dispatched peek (2.6 step 4), sends exactly one end message, whatever happens in it. Its outcome is one of:
- `Done`: its directory is ready to commit;
- `Failed(reason)`: an input failure;
- `Stopped`: the attempt saw the stop flag (2.6 step 11) and returned with nothing to commit;
- `RunFailure(reason)`: a run-level failure (2.7), whose stop the worker requests (`request_stop(reason, culprit)`, 2.11) before it writes its stderr line and sends the message: a host I/O error, an allocator refusal (seen by the counting allocator or by the refusal count), a refused spool claim, `ContainerDropped`, an error `classify` does not know, a panic outside a decoder's `catch_unwind` (caught by the worker's), or a leak found by the leak check.

A peek's message names its bundle, and its outcome is `Done` (what it found is in the table, a peek failure included, with its reason), `Stopped` or `RunFailure`, whose stop's culprit is the bundle (2.11). It carries the peek's peak and its decode work, which the hand-over's `bundles` rows take as `peek_peak` and `peek_work`, with a peek failure's reason, which the message carries too, as `peek`, so all three come from the message alone, and its `run` figures as their maxima, `peek_peak` and `peek_work_peak` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3); an attempt's figures leave them as they are. The message is fixed-size: the input or a peek's bundle, its outcome, its reason code and its detail code (`spool_full`'s, 2.11, or a failed input's error code, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3), for an `entry_error` the entry's path in a field of 1 KiB, cut at a code point boundary, with its cut flag, its counters and its peaks; `m_end` counts it whole (5.3). Its records are in the spool, never in the message (2.8).

**The loop.** State: `ready` (sorted), `in_flight` (a count), `slots` = `min(threads, floor(I / S))`, and `uncommitted`, the inputs dispatched, or failed before dispatch, and not committed, one bit per input (built at phase 1, 2.11 step 2): a dispatch sets its input's bit, as staging's end and the peek pass's end do for each input that fails before dispatch, each end setting every such bit before its first commit, and the controller clears it after it renames the input's directory to `done/<input>/`, on its own thread. Only the controller publishes a commit, a `Done` attempt's or a failure record's (2.8, below), so `uncommitted` and `done/` change only on its thread, and never after its hand-over. **The hand-over** gives the finisher `uncommitted`, the phase reached, the bundles rows, the figures of every end message received, the dispatch instant of every dispatched uncommitted input (at most `slots`), the `running` write's tries (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.4's first two rows), the `run` figures, `verified`, `verified_by_size` and the counts of `resume_list_failed`, `verify_list_failed`, `etag_not_md5` and `abort_failed`, all held outside the closure the controller's `catch_unwind` runs; the stop word holds the kept stop (2.11). The finisher writes each uncommitted input `not_run` with the reason `stopped`, each input that is neither uncommitted nor committed `not_run` with no reason (it was never dispatched, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3), the stop's culprit (2.11) with its reason as `detail` on its `inputs` row, or, when the culprit's kind is a bundle, a peek's, on that bundle's `bundles` row (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3), and an input whose `done/<input>/` exists as committed. **The finisher's snapshot:** it takes `uncommitted` once, at its take, with the **hand-over slot**, an atomic naming the input whose commit was under way, or none (the commit order, below). When the slot names an input, the finisher makes one stat, of `done/<input>/`, which exists only when the controller stopped between that rename and the slot's clear, after a controller panic or a failed rename (one that reported an error, 2.4's commit order): the input is then committed, whatever its bit, since its rename came before the hand-over, and the finisher appends `commit found at take: <id>` to the `trace` log. A stat error other than `ENOENT` counts the input as uncommitted, written `stopped`, the conservative answer, since resume applies to published outputs in either sink and every later run stages and extracts its inputs again, without reusing spool commits: the finisher appends `commit unknown at take: <id> <errno>` to the `trace` log, a line that overrides any rename line for the input, as the reconcile line does, and writes a stderr warning naming it, after the finisher's step 3 (2.11), its last head write, and after step 4's wait, before step 5, a hold of it bounded by `T_hard`, which is armed, since a slot still set means a stop (2.11 step 4, *A held stdout*, W13). The `handover` hook's report and every `inputs` row are written from that snapshot. No commit lands after the hand-over, since only the controller renames into `done/` and it parks once it has handed over (2.11), so the set of `done/<input>/` that exist is fixed at the hand-over, and the hook, the `trace` log, with its reconcile line, and the manifest agree; the one exception is a stat error, where the hook and the manifest say `stopped` and the `trace` log shows the rename, if it was logged, followed by the `commit unknown` line. A re-run's resume (E43) checks previously published outputs by the sink's procedure: whole-file MD5 for a directory, LIST/ETag for a store, including a PUT the server completes after the hand-over (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2); its spool starts clean (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.2), so the cut decides only this run's rows.

**The peek pass** comes first in phase 5. It is the loop below over the peek queue in place of `ready`: each peek takes a slot; it sets no `uncommitted` bit and no dispatch instant, which are an input's; its `Done` commits nothing; and its `Stopped` and `RunFailure` are an attempt's. Its `trace` line is `peek <bundle id>`. It ends when the peek queue is empty and no peek is in flight. Then the controller marks every input whose view staged whole and holds a peek failure (2.3, rule 1), setting each one's bit as a dispatch does, all before the first commit, then commits their records, the stop word loaded before each rename (below), and builds `ready` from the rest; only then is the first attempt dispatched. So every attempt reads a whole table that holds no failure for its view, and the peeks' work is bounded as an attempt's is (2.6 step 4). During the pass the progress line's `inputs.running` is 0, `inputs.queued` counts every input whose view staged whole, none yet dispatched, and `memory.slots_reserved` and `memory.attempts_used` count the peeks' slots and budgets (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 10).

```
queue = the peek queue
loop:
  if stopped(): hand over to the finisher (2.11) and park
  while in_flight < slots and queue is not empty:
      x = queue.pop(); dispatch(x); in_flight += 1
  if in_flight == 0 and queue is empty:
      if queue is the peek queue:
          mark every peek failure's holders, then commit their records (2.3, rule 1); queue = ready; continue
      go to phase 6
  poll the end eventfd and the wake eventfd, for at most the 1 s tick
  whatever ended the poll: read the end eventfd (EAGAIN counts as 0), then
  try_recv until Empty, handling each end message before taking the next:
    on each end message (input or a peek's bundle, outcome):
      in_flight -= 1
      match outcome:
          Done:            commit the attempt's directory (2.8, in the commit order below); a peek's, nothing
          Failed(r):       unless stopped(): delete the directory (2.11's checks); the pool writes its failure record, the controller renames it (E60, below)
          Stopped:         delete nothing, commit nothing (the input is not_run, reason stopped; the next start clears the spool)
          RunFailure(r):   hand over and park (its worker requested the stop, 2.11; a leaked slot is never freed)
```

- A failure record is committed as a `Done` attempt is, by the controller's rename: a records file of one line, in a directory of its own renamed to `done/<input>/`. The controller reads `stopped()` before it spawns each record's write, and spawns none once it is set; a blocking task on the controller's spare permit (2.11) creates a temporary directory, `fail/<input>/` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.1), reads the stop flag and, when it is clear, writes the file there, and the controller awaits it in `block_on` under the stop (W1, W2). Then it commits the directory to `done/<input>/` in the commit order. **The commit order**, for every commit, a `Done` attempt's or a failure record's, on the controller's own thread: (1) it records the input in the hand-over slot; (2) it loads the stop word, and when that holds a reason it clears the slot and hands over, the input uncommitted; (3) it renames; (4) it writes the rename's `trace` line; (5) it clears the input's bit; (6) it clears the slot. A rename that fails is a run failure classified by 2.7, `spool_full` on `ENOSPC` or `EDQUOT`, otherwise `host_io`: the controller requests that stop (2.11), writes `rename failed: <id> <errno>`, `<id>` the input's id, as a `RunFailure`'s line names it (below), to the `trace` log and hands over with the slot still set, its stderr line, the same `rename failed: <id> <errno>`, masked as every stderr line is (2.11), written after the hand-over, the stop naming no culprit, so the finisher's stat decides, `ENOENT` uncommitted and an existing directory committed, with its reconcile line. No `point` sits between the rename and its trace line; the commit's points sit between steps 1 and 2, after the slot is set, and between the trace line and the clear (2.11). So a panic anywhere between the rename and the slot's clear leaves the slot naming the input, which the finisher's one stat finds committed (above). A rename is a metadata call, which dirty-page throttling does not hold (its journal wait is named in 2.11's *What cannot be bounded*). A stop while it awaits the write, or at the load, leaves the directory unrenamed: the controller hands over, the finisher reads no `fail/` directory, and the next start's cleanup removes it (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.2 step 5). This holds for every failure record: staging's end's, the peek pass's end's and phase 5's `Failed(r)`'s. The `trace` log has a line per rename, the controller's, then the controller's `handover` line, which it writes before it releases the finisher, in its `catch_unwind` handler too, after which no rename is logged (2.11). The failure writer takes the failed attempt's own measured figures from its end message (`export_work`, `spooled_part`, peaks, evictions and elapsed time), with null figures where the input never dispatched. It preserves them, except for zeroing `skips` and `unresolved_scripts`, and adds only the failure record's own flush to the supplied `spooled_part` (2.6 step 10); it never changes `export_work`. Its returned summary has exactly the figures written on disk. An input that fails before dispatch (2.3, rule 1) commits its record so, the stop word loaded before each rename: when staging ends, if its view did not stage whole; else when the peek pass ends. Each end sets every such input's bit before its first commit, so a stop during its commits leaves each record not yet committed `not_run` `stopped`, with a null `ms`. A stop during the peek pass, before its end, leaves every input whose view staged whole `not_run` with no reason and a null `ms`: none was dispatched or marked (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3); the staging failures keep their records.
- `stopped()` reads the stop flag, which `request_stop` sets before it returns (2.11). So the controller's own request is a stop at once: it hands over in the same turn, and no later step of its loop or of phase 6 or 7 starts; a phase-6 task already in flight on the pool ends at its next stop check (2.9).
- Every attempt and every peek returns (its work is bounded, 2.6 steps 4, 7 and 10), and one slot always fits `I`, so each queue empties.
- The dispatch channel has capacity `threads`, and the end-message channel `threads` too: at most `threads` attempts and peeks run, so no send blocks. Each sender writes the controller's **end eventfd** after its send, and the controller reads the channel only by `try_recv`: after every return of its `poll` of that eventfd and its wake eventfd (2.11), for at most the 1 s tick, whatever ended it, it first reads the end eventfd, `EAGAIN` counting as 0 and never as a host error, then calls `try_recv` until `Empty`, handling each message before it takes the next. The read comes before the drain, so a send after the drain writes the eventfd again and ends the next `poll`; a message still in the channel at the hand-over is not received, and its figures are left out of the `run` sums and peaks (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3). So it never blocks inside the channel, with one qualification: std's `try_recv` can spin briefly while a sender that has claimed a slot finishes writing it, so that rests on the senders' progress, the workers' and the peeks' code between the claim and the write, which neither blocks nor allocates (unverified against the pinned std). The lifecycle thread sends nothing on it: its wake is the stop word, an atomic the controller loads after the wake, and one write of the controller's wake eventfd (2.11).
- The controller's own loop runs inside `catch_unwind`; its handler requests the stop `internal`, writes the `trace` log's `handover` line, hands over, then writes its panic's stderr line (2.11), and parks.
- Every `RunFailure` writes one stderr line (2.11), from the worker that sends it, after its `request_stop` returns: the input's id, or a peek's bundle's, the reason and, for a panic, its location and payload. So a held stderr write delays neither the stop nor the hand-over (2.11, *A held stdout*).

### 2.5 Staging

**Local library seam (provisional until B7).** The driver operates on an exclusively
prepared spool supplied by its caller. `stage::require_local` rejects any URL,
an unused dependency's included, as a capability error before writes; validation
still accepts URLs. `stage::local::open` checks/opens on the caller, then returns
a movable `LocalCopy` job for the controller's blocking pool. `LocalCopy::run` only
copies and hashes; its `LocalFinish` result returns the pinned source to the
controller. The controller always calls `LocalFinish::finish`, including after
a failed or stopped copy, to re-stat, check the size and SHA-256, and seal via
`fchmod` on the original open destination descriptor before closing it. No
reopen is needed, so sealing is independent of the process umask. Ordinary
data-failure cleanup also runs during finalisation; a run failure or stop
retains the owner and claim for handover. Early EOF still re-stats before it
becomes `host_io`, preserving `source_changed` precedence.
The controller stages in spec order, commits all staging failures before the
peek queue, and commits all peek failures before attempts. Startup belongs to B7/B9.

Every bundle the spec declares, input or dependency, is staged once, in phase 4, in the spec's order, its `inputs` and then its `dependencies`, into the spool file `in/i<n>` or `in/d<n>` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.1). Every later read of the bundle, by any attempt, reads that file, never the source.
- **The HTTP service** makes every request the engine sends, staging's and the store clients' (2.9), each on a connection of its own: no pool, no proxy, HTTP/1.1 alone. A request resolves its host in a `spawn_blocking` task of its own (std's `ToSocketAddrs`, which runs `getaddrinfo` as tokio's `lookup_host` does; W17). A request keeps its permit, or its runtime's spare permit (2.11), for all its tries, and each of these permits has one **lookup token**: a try takes its permit's token before its timers start, and each redirect hop's lookup retakes it, free by then; the lookup's closure (a store's reached through 2.9's task-local slot) holds it, with its host's `String` and `CString` copies, and gives it back when the lookup returns, the copies ending with the closure. So a timer that drops a try leaves the token with its lookup, the next try under that permit starts once the lookup returns, and a permit whose token is out goes back to its pool when the lookup's closure returns, not inside the request. It connects to the addresses `BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4 allows, one at a time, in order, each through a tokio `TcpSocket` (`new_v4` or `new_v6`) on which it sets zero linger (`TcpSocket::set_zero_linger`, never the deprecated `set_linger`) before `connect`; a failure to make the socket or set its linger moves on to the next address, as a `connect` error does, and the address list is dropped once a `connect` succeeds. A connection carries one request, so any close, at `_exit`, a kill or a panic's unwind included, is an RST that discards what TCP still holds. Each socket is in the socket table (2.11, *Every exit*) from before its connect to its drop; a try that loads the closing flag set fails as `connect` without connecting. For `https` it runs the TLS handshake through tokio-rustls with step 4's `ClientConfig` (2.11), whose ALPN is `http/1.1`; for `http` it skips tokio-rustls, its IO an enum of the two streams, so one hyper `Connection` type serves both. A connection's resolve, connects and handshake run under its own `connect_timeout_ms` timer (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4). Then it runs hyper's `http1::Builder::new().writev(true).max_buf_size(128 * 1024).max_header_size(128 * 1024).handshake(io)`, `io` the stream in hyper-util's `TokioIo`, and sends its one request in origin form, with `Host` from its URL. It never sets `max_headers`, so hyper's default of 100 fields holds (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4's caps). The connection is never spawned: the request's future owns hyper's `Connection` and polls it beside the send, and once the head arrives the response body owns it, polls it before each read and drops it at its end. So a try's socket, TLS state, hyper buffers and request body end with its future: at its end, at its timer, or at `_exit` after a stop, which never drops a try's future (2.11). A request body reaches hyper in chunks of at most 64 KiB, each a slice of the payload's `Bytes`, with its exact size, so hyper writes `Content-Length`. Every request carries the user agent `unityrs/<version>` and `Accept: */*`, unless the bundle's `headers` names `User-Agent` or `Accept` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.2), whose own then takes its place; store requests keep the engine values; X7 and X9 check both. In tests only, the hooks `resolve` and `extra_roots` apply (2.11's *Test hooks*). `reqwest` stays in the dependency tree through `object_store`'s `cloud` feature, but the engine builds no `reqwest` client, so none makes a request.

  **Transport boundary.** `http::Service` serves one request; its response owns the unspawned hyper driver. B9 supplies the socket table and closing flag through `SocketTable`; the existing runtime supplies the blocking pool. Standalone transport tests use adapters. URL execution remains refused until controller/lifetime integration is accepted. The sole immutable `Arc<ClientConfig>` uses an explicit ring provider, compiled `webpki-roots` only, TLS 1.2/1.3 and `http/1.1` ALPN, with resumption, early data, certificate decompression and client identity disabled. Extra roots and resolver replacement exist only under the test hook. Socket registration uses the pinned Tokio release's supported descriptor interfaces; no descriptor is cleared until its owner closes.

  **B10a round-2 gates.** `sys::resolve` owns `getaddrinfo`/`freeaddrinfo` and copies only the first sixteen linked-list entries into fixed Rust storage, with the lookup token and 512 KiB charge held by the blocking closure. A synthetic 200,000-answer hook exercises this same walker under the allocator. Socket shutdown closes the zero-linger descriptor directly, before table removal, so neither TLS `close_notify` nor TCP FIN can precede its reset. `http_alloc` checks the 3.5 MiB library reservation at every allocation, subtracting only DATA pieces already returned to the caller, and also checks the 4 MiB total. Its accepted TLS scenes include near-cap informational/final heads and trailers, minimal TLS 1.2/1.3 certificate entries, an authority vector at capacity 32,768, a combined near-cap TLS 1.2 chain/OCSP/authority flight, and 32 × 16 KiB request values plus an exact 16 KiB transport URI. The TLS peer aligns handshake-message record boundaries for that combined flight; the original unaligned refusal remains tested. The production `Execution` runtime still needs B10c to enable both IO and time drivers before polling the service.

  Direct pins (defaults off) and registry publication dates, checked against the 2026-10-30 age cutoff:

  | crate | version | published | features |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | tokio | 1.53.3 | 2026-10-13 | rt, net, time, sync |
  | hyper | 1.12.0 | 2026-10-06 | client, http1, server |
  | hyper-util | 0.1.21 | 2026-09-24 | tokio |
  | http-body-util (tests only) | 0.1.5 | 2026-08-12 | none |
  | rustls | 0.23.46 | 2026-10-14 | ring, std, tls12 |
  | tokio-rustls | 0.26.6 | 2026-09-28 | ring, tls12 |
  | webpki-roots | 1.0.11 | 2026-10-23 | none |

  Keep `bytes` 1.12.1, ring 0.17.14, rustls-pki-types 1.15.1, rustls-webpki 0.103.16 and http 1.5.0 in the resolved lock. hyper's `server` feature exposes its typed size classifier. `http-body-util` collects fixture bodies as a dev-dependency; B10a uses neither tokio macros nor direct `futures-util` or `httpdate` dependencies. B10b adds direct `httpdate =1.0.3` for Retry-After; its only lockfile change is that dependency edge. Add the others only when their controller and store call sites land. X15 runs `cargo deny --all-features --config deny-bans.toml check bans` over the unfiltered graph, including platform-exclusive and dev dependencies; the supported-platform licence checks and notices keep matching filters in `deny.toml` and `about.toml`. The config checker holds the ban policies equal and refuses graph filters in `deny-bans.toml`. The exact pin and boundary fixtures are part of the contract: complete heads permit 128 KiB equality, unfinished heads refuse it, and trailers refuse equality. Ambiguous malformed/oversized inputs keep whichever typed error hyper encounters first. Registry dates are available from the [published hyper release](https://docs.rs/crate/hyper/1.12.0), [utilities](https://docs.rs/crate/hyper-util/0.1.21), [body utilities](https://docs.rs/crate/http-body-util/0.1.5), [TLS adapter](https://docs.rs/crate/tokio-rustls/0.26.6) and [roots release list](https://docs.rs/crate/webpki-roots/latest).

  **B10b staging ledger (2026-11-13).** `stage/{url,headers,retry,timers}.rs` drives one retained response and at most one pending positional write. The pending completion retains its original descriptor and DATA even when a stop wins receipt. The join-result adapter stores a completed owner inline before checking stop, so there is no filesystem mutex or separately allocated completion holder. A cutoff during a network wait parks that future, including a ready-but-unreceived head, for the outer driver's handover; it does not return by dropping a connecting socket. The lookup token is acquired by staging before its cutoff check and body-clock start, then transferred into B10a's resolver closure. A retry drops the response, waits for all writes, truncates the same descriptor to zero, releases its entire claim only on success, waits its backoff, and reserves again. No second file or disk counter is used. Supplied-size reservations and cumulative rounded growth share `OwnedFile`; failed writes, failed reset (including perform-then-error), and stop retain their claims. Every run failure signals `Culprit::None` before diagnostics. Download detail is B10a's fixed enum inside the stage's data-failure payload, mapped to both failure records and bundle rows; peer strings cannot enter it.

  Staging's additional active-URL/redirect and completion-storage reservation is 128 KiB per permit. It covers the current parsed URL, a bounded join and replacement URL (each serialized URL at most 16 KiB, a join's bounded transient at most their sum), fixed timer/hash state, and pending task bookkeeping. Request header maps/values and origin-form URI construction overlap the 3.5 MiB library envelope already checked with maximum requests; no maps or parsed URLs are retained per bundle. DATA is independently copied into at most `heap_cost(65536)` and keeps its full charge while moved into a write; hyper's retained backing remains in its envelope. During lookup, the 3 MiB library reservation, independent 512 KiB lookup charge and 128 KiB staging reservation total 3,801,088 bytes. After lookup quiesces, the library reservation grows to 3.5 MiB; with staging's 128 KiB and `heap_cost(65536)` for one DATA piece, the TCP/TLS phase totals 3,866,640 bytes. Each phase stays below `c_net = 4 MiB` (4,194,304 bytes); the lookup charge and full library reservation never overlap. The source analysis of hyper/rustls/DNS from B10a still applies: no new body collector, connection task, TLS configuration or resolver storage is introduced. Environment values remain shared in `C_hdr`; the caller resolves them at preflight. Runtime/pool setup remains in `C_base`, and phase-1 tables in measured `C_spec`.

  Round-2 full-staging stress combines the exact 16 KiB URL, 32 resolved 16 KiB header values, the aggregate 64 KiB header-name cap, near-cap informational/final heads and trailers, and the strongest aligned TLS 1.2 flight (near-64 KiB one-byte certificate list, 21,700 authority entries and 65,400-byte OCSP staple). This exposes the former 3 MiB library envelope as insufficient. The reservation is now 3.5 MiB, within the unchanged 4 MiB allowance. During resolution the service reserves 3 MiB and the blocking closure owns its independent 512 KiB; only after that closure returns does the library reservation grow to 3.5 MiB before TCP/TLS. Cancellation of a held lookup leaves its independent charge/token intact. With staging's 128 KiB and one charged 64 KiB DATA piece the live charge remains below 4 MiB; the lookup and TLS maxima never overlap. `url_alloc::maximal_staging_request_and_strongest_tls_with_ocsp_fit_both_envelopes` counts full staging, runtime and pool allocations without subtracting any caller-held DATA, so its library comparison is conservative as well as checking the total. Native aarch64 macOS release measured 3,565,872 bytes (104,144 below the 3,670,016-byte library envelope and 628,432 below 4 MiB); Linux remeasurement remains a gate.

  `url_alloc` counts runtime setup, the polling thread and pool threads in release with the same `heap_cost` allocator as B10a. On native aarch64 macOS: a 64 MiB streamed body peaked at 507,616 bytes, combined near-cap informational/final heads and trailers at 768,704, 32 resolved header values of 16 KiB at 1,664,896, a near-limit redirect at 183,584, and 1,000 sequential hostnames at 124,272. Each scene asserts the 4 MiB ceiling and zero remaining permit charges after quiescence. Peer backing and the validated spec are outside the network window; peer observations are conservatively counted and discarded between sequential hosts. Preparing 1,000 URL bundles retained and peaked at 345,776 bytes, including 88-byte `StageEnd` and 112-byte `BundleState` entries; typed detail needs no additional row allocation. These are focused staging measurements, not B12's full calibration; Linux gates remain required before enabling URLs. Round-1 remeasurement after DATA coalescing kept every scene below 4 MiB; the existing TLS stress maximum remained 2,937,328 bytes. Immediately Ready DATA frames fill the charged 64 KiB piece before a positional write, stopping at Pending, EOF or an error; an already received prefix is persisted before reporting a later parser fault. Every write checks available free space even inside an upfront reservation; staging alone skips its pool task's duplicate after checking and reserving the complete piece before dispatch. Content-Length checks follow hyper's framing (Transfer-Encoding overrides it), including equal digit-only comma lists and repeated fields. Retry-After dates stay absolute until retry scheduling. Allocation-refusal windows cover each stage's synchronous poll only. The transport adapter gates head and body delivery through scheduled futures; the shared driver advances to the earliest event, and two-stage tests check ordering and refusal isolation. A drop-observable transport owner proves cutoff retention through explicit retirement. The expanded fault matrix covers raw informational/head/trailer boundaries, oversized trailers, resets mid-chunk, empty DATA, zero backoff and cancellation during held lookup, connect, TLS handshake, head, body, write, retry wait, token wait and before descriptor sealing. The heavy tests join CI's scale command. `url_stage`, `url_faults` and `url_ledger` use an injected clock and in-memory byte streams; `url_transport` uses B10a's loopback TLS hooks. Tests pin expiry winning a DATA/EOF tie, a satisfied rate window rolling before crediting its boundary DATA, and pause ending at task completion rather than controller receipt. URL CLI execution, unused URL dependencies included, remains refused with 78 usage pending B10c's B9/B7c integration.

- **A URL** is downloaded by streaming to the file (E7), with `Accept-Encoding: identity`, so every byte counted is a byte of the bundle, and no body is decompressed. Staging follows redirects itself, by `BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4's `redirects`: it refuses a `Location` over 16 KiB before parsing it, joins one to the current URL with the `url` crate, and drops the 3xx response, and its connection, before the next request connects. So no third-party layer parses a `Location`, and hyper's 128 KiB head cap bounds the head that carries it (2.5, A1). It sets no whole-request timeout: the idle timeout and the rate floor are the engine's own, over a try's redirect hops (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4). Each body chunk is written to the spool file with `pwrite` (`FileExt::write_at`) at the try's own offset, on the runtime's blocking pool, the chunk moved into the task, and the next is read once that write returns; no `tokio::fs::File` is used, whose own buffer (up to 2 MiB, tokio's `src/io/blocking.rs`) no term counts.
- **Redirect/retry details.** Refuse userinfo before connecting to a joined URL; enforce the same URL form and size checks on each hop. Multiple Location fields give no usable Location and an non-retried final status. Of Retry-After fields, use the largest valid nonnegative seconds or HTTP date, malformed values ignored and overflow capped; never comma-split dates or carry redirect-only values forward. Check cutoff before/after waits, after token acquisition and before starting a try. Body timers exclude the stage's own outstanding spool write, preserving the window remainder; expiry wins an exact injected-clock tie.
- **A try's class** (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4) is read from the layer that failed, by the first row that matches. A store's try (2.9) reads its transport errors the same way, for `BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.6's table and section 10's detail.

  | where the try ended | class |
  |---|---|
  | resolving its host, setting up or connecting its socket, or at `connect_timeout_ms` | connect |
  | in the TLS handshake, on a `rustls::Error` (tokio-rustls returns it inside an `io::Error`, `src/common/mod.rs`) | connect, `tls` |
  | in the TLS handshake, on any other I/O error or an EOF | connect |
  | at the engine's idle or rate timer, firing first (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4), the connect still running included | timeout |
  | reading its head, on `Error::is_parse_too_large` (a head hyper refuses for its size, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4; `src/proto/h1/role.rs`, `src/body/length.rs`) | head too large; a store's try, no response (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.6) |
  | sending the request or reading its head, an unparsable head among them | no response |
  | reading the body, a malformed chunk among them | truncated |
  | a response head | its status's row; a redirect 1.4's rule refuses is `redirect_refused` |
- **A local path** is opened `O_RDONLY | O_NONBLOCK`, so a FIFO or a device cannot hold the open; `fstat` must show a regular file, or the bundle fails `source_not_regular`; then `O_NONBLOCK` is cleared and the file is copied to the spool file through a 64 KiB buffer, on the runtime's blocking pool, checking the stop flag per buffer. It copies exactly the size `fstat` gave at the open. After the copy, or on early EOF, re-stat: size, modification time, change time (both at full timestamp precision), device and inode must equal those at the open, or the bundle fails `source_changed` (a transport reason: the source was being written). Unchanged early EOF is `host_io`. After a complete unchanged copy, check expected size before SHA-256; only staged bytes or a whole-body integrity mismatch supply size/digest facts. A local source is on a local filesystem (A11).
- **Size.** A bundle is at most `M` bytes: every meter's `max_input_bytes` is `M`, and the core reads a file whole. The checks run in this order, before any claim, and the first that fails names the reason: a stated size (`size`, the local `fstat` size) above `M` (`limit(input_bytes, M)`: the record its attempt would give); a `Content-Length` other than a given `size` (`integrity_mismatch`); a `Content-Length` above `M` (`limit`). For a URL, while it streams, passing `M` is `limit(input_bytes, M)` before a given `size` is checked for `integrity_mismatch(size)`; reject before the violating claim or write. Check supplied size against `M` then reserve it at each try's start; Content-Length alone never reserves. Both integrity and input-byte failures are permanent.
- **While it streams**, the engine counts its bytes and computes its SHA-256. At the end, a given `size` or `sha256` that does not match fails the bundle (`integrity_mismatch`), its row recording what arrived (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3). The digest is recorded for the manifest's `bundles` rows.
- **Then** the file is made read-only (mode 0400), and nothing writes it again. The spool is the engine's own directory under its lock (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.2), so the staged bytes are pinned for the run (A11).
- **Failures.** A download that failed after its retries fails the bundle (`download_failed`, or `redirect_refused`); a local path that does not exist or cannot be opened fails it (`source_missing`), one that is not a regular file fails it (`source_not_regular`), and one written during its copy fails it (`source_changed`). These are the transport reasons of D's second limit (section 1). An I/O error while reading an opened local file, or writing the spool, stops the run (2.7).
- **Space.** For a local copy, check both supplied size and initial `fstat` size against `M` before claims, and claim/copy the initial `fstat` length, never the caller's larger expectation. Individually owned staged/peek files share the spool counter, rounding and error rules; drop releases nothing, and only successful explicit deletion or a retry's successful truncate-to-zero after every write completes returns their claims. Truncate failure retains the full claim. Positional writes and reset use this same owner, descriptor, counter and injectable filesystem seam. Each staged file's bytes are claimed from the spool counter before they are written (2.8's rule): a stated size (Size above; a `Content-Length` is not one) at the start; without one, the claim grows as the body arrives, to its size so far rounded up to `b`. A try that fails waits for its write in flight, so none lands later, then truncates the file and returns its claim before the next try claims. The truncate, the spool file's open and its `fchmod` to 0400, and a local source's open, `fstat`s and `fcntl` are calls on the controller's thread (W5), not blocking tasks, so a permit's blocking tasks stay 2.11's (each bounded by A11; its time is every other try's, D's second limit). A refused claim stops the run (`spool_full`); since no bundle claims more than `M` rounded up to `b`, that is the spool's sizing, not one bundle's doing.
- **Concurrency.** At most `download_concurrency` bundles stage at once. Extraction starts when every bundle has staged or failed.

**Why a copy.** A source file can change during a run without changing its size, modification time or inode, and the core reads a dependency's file again after each eviction. A hash of the first read cannot pin a reread the engine does not see. The copy pins every read, and makes a change of the source after its copy harmless. It costs the spool the build's bundle bytes (1.3 to 1.8 GB on the corpus) and one sequential copy, which a filesystem may serve from its page cache.

### 2.6 An attempt

1. **Budget and meter.** Build the attempt's budget (2.2) and `M_X = Meter::for_input(&limits, budget, Reservation::Input)`.
2. **Its own open.** `bytes = read_path(staged file, &limits, &M_X)` (`env/mod.rs`). Then `check_file_type_with_reader(&bytes, &limits, ReaderKind::Stream)` (`detect.rs`) and `parse_file_with_reader(name, bytes, ty, None, &M_X, &ctx_X, ReaderKind::Stream)` (`files/mod.rs`): the rules the core uses for a `Source::Path`. A file that is not a bundle fails X, `not_a_bundle`. A bundle is one file in version 1: no `.splitN` merge.
3. **The AssetBundle object.** Open every own top-level row in directory order
   first, including shadowed and unsupported rows, using the same traversal as
   selection. Then read the first AssetBundle of each selected own SerializedFile
   (the scope of `SerializedFile::container()`), through checked typetrees and
   cleaned attributes, and its container through that API. Concatenate
   `m_Dependencies` in own-file/list order, preserving repeated undeclared names,
   charged to `M_X`; compare exact bytes against view ids and never widen the view.
   No Environment container pass runs.
4. **The private Environment.** Register `view(X)` less X with `load_dependency(Source::Path(staged file))`, one at a time in bytewise order of id, then X last, always, with `load_dependency(Source::Bytes { name: its spool name, data: the bytes of step 2 })` (`env/load.rs`). Every member's peek succeeded, since an input whose view holds a peek failure fails before dispatch (2.3, rule 1), so the **peek table** (below) holds no failure for the view. X's slot shares its own handle's allocation, so X is never read twice, and the core's own-handle redirect serves X's resources through it (core 4.1). X's slot reads X's header, blocks-info and directory by the memory reader's rules, and X's own handle by the stream reader's (`open_source`, `env/registry/dependency.rs`). Of the two readers' differences (core 3.3), one alone lets both reads of a header succeed with different results: a header string with no NUL in its first 32,767 bytes, which the memory reader returns whole and the stream reader one byte shorter. Every other either raises on the stream side, where step 2 has already failed X, or leaves both readers past the end of the bytes, where they read the same nothing. So the engine compares the slot's header and directory with its own handle's, and a difference fails X (`container_error`). Add each returned `File`'s top-level directory names to the view's **name table**, charged to `M_X`, from which the `same_named_cab` rows are written once the view is registered (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3). Then, before the next load, and X's too, after its load, **reach its nested containers**: for each top-level entry the table marks as a container, in directory order, but a dropped row (below), call `get_cab(entry name, &M_X)` on the private Environment; nothing is read for the rest. Under `Lazy` a nested container is a key whose slot answers nothing until a lookup reaches it, and reaching registers its whole subtree at the ranks an eager load gives (core's Lazy invariant, `env/registry/mod.rs`; `PARITY.md`, Lazy difference (b)). The reached mark lives on the slot, which outlives an eviction (`env/registry/slot.rs`), so a reopened bundle's nested CABs stay registered. Without this, a pointer by name alone from X's top level into a nested container, X's or a dependency's, would miss where UnityPy's eager load finds it. Then drop the `File`, so the private LRU holds the only strong handle. Reaching before the next load keeps every drop of an unreached container slot to rows of one bundle. The engine reaches no row whose key (`simplify_name`) a later row of the same bundle takes, which it tells by one sorted pass over that member's names in the view's name table: each row's key computed once into a buffer, and a permutation of the member's rows (a `u32` each) sorted in place by `sort_unstable_by` on (key, index), a total order, so the sort takes no uncharged scratch as std's stable sort would, both charged to `M_X` and dropped after the member's reaches, a row being dropped when the next in that order has its key: that is `BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.3's dropped row, which the core reaches at the load, but for 4.3's gaps, and the later row gets its own reach. The table answers a dropped row too, for CR7 (section 6).
   - **The peek table.** Each bundle of the peek queue (2.4) is peeked once per run, in phase 5's peek pass, before any attempt (2.1, 2.4). It runs on a worker, sealed and alone, as an attempt does: on a budget of its own whose `Input` limit is `M`, its other reservations refused, which admission counts as a slot (2.2), and on a fresh meter with the attempt's `Limits` (2.2), its own `4 M` of decode work among them. It opens the staged file by step 2's rules, a `Source::Path`'s, so it reads the directory the attempts register (X's own once the comparison above passes). Then, for each top-level row in directory order, a dropped row included, it reads the row's first 21 bytes (`entry_range`, the bytes the core's detection reads) and notes whether they are a bundle's; answering every row, it keeps no table of keys. It keeps only the outcome, a function of the bundle's bytes, `M` and the spec alone: a bit per top-level row, set for a container, or the bundle's **peek failure**. That is its open's failure, what step 2 gives (a parse failure's code, a WebFile's `unsupported` among them, `files/web.rs`, or `not_a_bundle` for a file that parses as another kind, a SerializedFile), or a `Limit` of its meter at a row. Each is a fixed-size code: a bundle's open never wraps its error in `Error::Entry`, which only an entry's parse or read builds (`files/bundle/mod.rs`, `entry_error`), and a row's read error other than a `Limit` is "not a container" (below), so the table keeps no entry path. Its bits are built in a buffer charged on that meter, the peek's one account, and no second `Meter::for_input` is made for it, so the meter's bound, checked first, covers every charge the peek makes. Its uncharged heap is within the worker's `C_env` share (5.3), and the worker's checks after an attempt (2.2's leak check, 2.7's counts) apply to it. It checks the stop flag between entries; a stopped peek ends `Stopped`, and no attempt is dispatched after it (2.11).
   - **The peek's rule.** A read error that is not transient and not a `Limit` means "not a container", and that entry's own reads fail later with it, as any read of it does. A `Limit` is the bundle's peek failure, and an I/O error is a host failure (2.7), which stops the run. A refusal (a `Budget` error of the peek's budget) cannot come first: that budget's limit is its meter's bound, which the meter checks first (`limits/meter.rs`, `reserve`), so a `Budget` error in a peek is liblzma's refused allocation, the allocator's (`host_memory`, 2.7). This is the core's rule (`is_bundle_entry`, `env/registry/reach.rs`) but for a `Limit`, which the core reads as "not a container", dropping a nested container unnamed (CR7 takes the table's answers instead). So a block before a `.resS` whose decode fails (`Decompress`) fails no registration (3.10; under CR9, an opted-in member's read that ends before the failure can succeed; the table keeps the whole-block answer, section 6). Since the peek runs once, before any attempt, a bundle's peek failure fails every input whose view holds it, before dispatch, the same way whatever runs beside it and at any `threads`: its record names the bundle by 2.3's rule 1, which says when another failure comes first. A peek failure leaves the bundle's `bundles` row `staged` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3). When each attempt peeked on the member's own meter, before the table, that held only usually. Below depth 1, at a dropped container row, and at a top-level container row a nested key of one drops, the core decides, until CR7 (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.3's gaps).
   - **Where it is kept.** Each bundle has a **peek word**, a 64-bit atomic built at phase 1 (2.11 step 2), so `C_spec` counts it, which the peek's worker sets before it sends its end message: the bundle's top-level row count `N`, a `u32`, with no entry a container or some, their bits in the bundle's peek file; or the peek failure's reason, a fixed-size code as an end message's (2.4). `N` always fits: the core reads the directory's count as an `i32`, a negative one as 0 (`files/bundle/open.rs`, `read_blocks_info`), so no directory has more than 2^31 - 1 rows, and no reason is needed for one past `u32::MAX`. `N` and the bits are what CR7's hint takes (section 6). The **peek file**, `in/pi<n>` or `in/pd<n>` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.1), holds a bit per top-level row in directory order; it is written only for a bundle with a container entry, claimed by 2.8's rule, then made read-only (mode 0400). The bits are on disk because a bundle's top-level rows grow with its bytes, not with the spec, so a heap term for them would not be one of 5.1's constants. An attempt reads a member's peek file at that member's reach, into a buffer charged to `M_X` (step 10) and dropped after it: a refusal of that charge is X's own reason, as any charge to `M_X` is; the word's `N` must be the registered directory's row count and the file's length `ceil(N / 8)`, and any other is a run failure `internal`; an I/O error reading it is a host failure, as a staged file's is (2.3, rule 1). The table is written before the first attempt is dispatched and never after, and it holds no decoded data, so sealing and D hold (2.10).
   - **Its cost.** To find where a read starts, the core measures, so decodes, every earlier block whose length it does not yet know (`first_block`, `files/bundle/runs.rs`). So a peek decodes its bundle up to the furthest first bytes, by offset, among its top-level rows, or whole for a row whose size is negative, or whose offset is negative and size non-zero (`entry_span` measures every block): a bundle of one block, as nearly every corpus bundle is, whole (2.2, A5). On the corpus, one peek per bundle per run is about the decode-once work, an estimate (2.2). In general a peek is bounded by its own `4 M` of decode work, measuring and repeated decodes included: `measure_all` keeps no run of what it decodes, so a row whose offset is negative has its block decoded to measure it, then again to read it (`files/bundle/runs.rs`). In an attempt, only the reaches of marked entries decode, on the member's meter (5.6), but for the interim's peeks. Until CR7, the core's `settle` lists and peeks every unreached lazy row whose key a later registration takes (`env/registry/register.rs`, where a registration lists the slot it drops; `settle_list`, `env/registry/reach.rs`), so three per-attempt peeks remain: a `BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.3 dropped row, at its load; a member's unmarked row that a later member's row of the same key drops, at that load, or at the reach (4.3); and an unmarked row that a nested CAB of a marked row of the same member drops, at the engine's reach. Each is a decode of that member's block in every attempt whose view holds it, within the attempt's own decode work. A refused `settle` leaves its slot listed and retries it at later lookups (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.3), and each retry is counted within the attempt's decode work too. On the corpus none occurs: no bundle repeats a key in its directory, no direct view holds one key in two members, and no bundle has a nested container (A5). **Test:** `a_bundles_peek_runs_once_and_its_limit_fails_every_input_that_holds_it` (`extract.md` X11), at `threads` 1, 2 and 8: the `trace` log's `peek <bundle id>` lines show one peek per bundle of a view that staged whole and none of any other bundle; a dependency whose peek meets a `Limit` fails, before dispatch, every input whose view holds it, with the same record at every `threads`, two holders whose views put different pressure on `D` among them; an input's own bundle that fails its peek gives the input's own reason; a bundle that is both an input and another input's member fails both; a view that did not stage whole names its staging failure, committed at staging's end, though a member of lesser id failed its peek through another view, and a whole view names its least-id peek failure, each as its solo run does (2.3, rule 1); a member that fits `M` alone, which the per-attempt peek refused on a `D` its view's other members filled, is read; and, at `threads` 1, in a scene with no dropped row and no cross-member same-key row, step 4's registrations read no top-level row of any member but the reaches of marked entries, so no member did decode work in step 4 after its open's blocks-info but those reaches. The test counts entry accesses, not meter work, and covers step 4 only: the `point` hook's `count` action appends CR8's counts to the `trace` log at a named input's registration's start and at its end (2.11), and the test asserts their difference, so step 3's own parse of X's AssetBundle row falls outside. CR8's counters, behind its test feature, count each span read once, in `read_span_pinned`, which every one reaches (`parse_own`, so `entry()`, directly; `entry_range` and `entry_bytes` through `read_span`; `files/bundle/runs.rs`, `files/bundle/mod.rs`), by the row its caller names; a cached `entry()` hit or a twin's kept failure reads nothing and counts nothing, and the decoding of blocks-info at an open, metadata work, is no span read. A count is keyed by a **load id**, which the core draws under the test feature from one process-wide counter once per load: in `load_dep` and in `load_resolved` before `with_room`, so a retry keeps it, and in a caller's `Environment::load`, which has no retry; and which `Dependency` keeps and `Dependency::handle` passes to every reopen, as CR9's opt-in is carried, plus the row's index path: `parse_own` records on each nested `BundleFile` it opens its load's id, which a nested bundle inherits, and the index path from `dir_index`, equal to `Rank::path` for a registered row. An open outside a load (X's own handle, step 2, and every peek's open) counts under a `bare` key, which the `count` action skips, and `load_all`'s `read_span(0, u64::MAX)` under a `whole` key, which the test asserts absent. Two tables hold the counts: a **load-name table** of `(load id, open name)`, written once as each load draws its id, the open name being the one the load gives the core (a member's staged path, X's spool name), 4,096 entries of at most 4,096 bytes (`PATH_MAX`) each; and a **span-read table** of `(load id, path, count)` slots, 16,384 of them, each path of a width fixed when the table is reserved (17 indices for `Limits::default()`, `max_container_depth + 1`). The `count` action reads both through the core's test-feature accessor `unityrs_core::testing::span_reads`, whose reader sees them under their lock without a copy, so the action allocates nothing in the core, and maps each slot through the load-name table to the engine's bundle id, summing each (bundle id, path) over its load ids; a count it cannot map is written `unmapped`, which fails the test. The main thread's step 1 (2.11 *Threads*) preallocates both under `test-hooks` when a `count` action is armed (`unityrs_core::testing::reserve_span_reads(names, slots, width)`), outside any attempt, so X12's tally and the leak checks never see them grow; step 2's `C_spec` and phase 1's peak `p` are both measured from step 2's start (5.3), so the tables fall outside both, and the call returns nothing. With no table reserved, the core records and allocates nothing. 5.1's bound is the release build's: the tables sit outside it, and no `count` scene checks the heap against `memory_bound`. A test build's meter charges can differ from a release build's by the test fields (the load id and index path a `BundleFile` keeps), and no scene compares the two.
