A full table, a longer name or a deeper path sets a sticky overflow flag that fails the test and never drops a count; X11's scenes, synthetic, are built to use at most a quarter of each table over a run. Parses must be counted, since the engine's reach reads through `entry()`, not `entry_range`. Until CR7, each of the interim's three peeks has a scene of its own, in which the counts show that row's read and nothing else but the reaches of marked entries (a marked row's reach counts its own read and its nested bundle's row peeks); with CR7, the scene with a dropped row shows no read of a row its hint leaves unmarked. CR8 is this test's prerequisite (section 6).
   - **Until CR7** the engine itself reaches each marked entry that no later row of its bundle drops, by `get_cab` on the private Environment, as now: an Environment call, whose errors step 7 classifies. With CR7, `load_dependency` takes the table's row count and its answer for every top-level row as a hint, marks each unmarked row reached without reading it, and reaches every marked row itself, a dropped one included, so landing CR7 removes the interim's three peeks above (section 6).
   - A load or a reach that fails, or a load that returns a file that is not a bundle, fails X (2.3, rule 1; for X's own, as an entry or container failure of X), and the attempt makes no read through that Environment after it. CR1 has landed: under `batch()`'s `Lazy`, a failed `load_dependency` leaves the registry's keys as they were (core 4.1, *a failed dependency load publishes nothing*), so after a failed load this rule is a defence only. The engine's Environment is `EnvOptions::batch()`'s (2.2); only 3.9's Eager test variant runs an attempt under `Eager`, where a failed load keeps the CABs it registered before it failed (UnityPy's partial registration), so there the rule is load-bearing, not a defence. The stop flag is checked between registrations.
5. **Select and plan** (`BATCH_SPEC.md` sections 3 and 4.4). Walk the entries and objects in object order, filter by `class_id()` from the object table (no value read), and read typetrees only for the selected objects. Then plan, before any export: for every output the selection makes, in (kind, object order), expand its name and key, the key and each archive member path stopping at 1,025 bytes as `too_long` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 2.3), and decide `duplicate_name`, `duplicate_key` and every skip that needs no pixels. The plan table (one key per planned output) is charged to `M_X`. A planned output keeps its key even when its export later fails, so no export result moves a key.
6. **Export one sheet group at a time.**
   - Sprites are grouped by their sheet: the texture's file, known by its row chain from the view (each level the directory row it was opened from, the core's `ParentKind::Bundle`'s `dir_index`), never by its CAB name, and path id, and the same of its alpha texture or none (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.4); each file's identity is interned once per attempt, charged to `M_X`, in work linear in its chain, and a container gone before its file is `internal`. The groups' order is a counting sort of the Sprites by group, counted as decode work. For each group, decode the sheet (and its alpha texture) from the pointers the plan recorded for it, render every sprite of the group, then drop the sheet.
   - The atlases that cut one sheet are one group, cut one at a time (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 5). The sheet's own texture output comes first, then each atlas in object order, its JSON before its crops, so a unit's key is fixed before its crops are recorded (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.5). Any other texture is its own group.
   - This replaces UnityPy's per-file texture cache with a cache of size one, so the export buffers are bounded by the largest sheet (5.5). The output is the same: the cache changes when a sheet is decoded, not what it decodes to.
7. **Reads into other bundles.** A pointer is dereferenced with `Environment::deref` on the private Environment, from the file that holds it (`env/deref.rs`). Before its next Environment call, the attempt takes the target's top-level `File` from its `Parent` and holds it while it uses the target (core 4.1, *a dropped container is an error, never a guess*). What is read is copied into the attempt's charged buffers, and every handle into another bundle is dropped when that read ends. After each Environment call from step 4 on, the attempt first classifies every run-level class of 2.7 the call returned (70, `host_io` and `host_memory`); then it reads the Environment's `stats().dependencies_evicted`, and past `max_attempt_evictions` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.1) it fails X, `limit(attempt_evictions, <value>)`, after a registration call too; only then does it classify the rest. A reopen restarts its meter's work count (core 4.1). Every open of a member after its registration follows an eviction. The core does not mark which `Budget(Dependency)` refusal is a reopen's, so every such refusal the core returns after registration fails X (2.7; until CR7, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.3's gaps aside), and a refused reopen is never tried again at the next access, uncounted. So the attempt makes at most `|view| + max_attempt_evictions` opens, each a read of at most `M` bytes and within `4 M` of decode work.
8. **Resources** (own bundle first, core Q12). `BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.3's own-chain procedure, over the candidates of `resource_candidates(path)` (`env/resource.rs`): a container's rows are its `BundleFile::directory`, the chain is followed by `parent`, a row is opened with `entry(i)` and a raw one read with `entry_range` through that handle (`files/bundle/mod.rs`), and a SerializedFile fails as the core's `not_a_reader` (`decode_error`, `malformed`). Beside `PARITY.md` section 19.2's Tier C divergences, it differs under `batch()` from the core's `resource_try` (`env/resource_data.rs`) in two ways: Lazy difference (b), under which a container row holds its key, and a passed-over match, which moves there to the next candidate. The fallback is `Environment::resource(from, path, offset, size)` on the private Environment, which covers a candidate in another bundle of the view. The engine's copy of the candidate rule is checked by `own_first_resources_equal_environment_resource_without_same_named_cabs` (3.9) until CR3 moves the rule into the core.
9. **The container** comes from X's own `SerializedFile::container()` (`files/serialized/mod.rs`), never from `Environment::container()`.
10. **Engine buffers** (`extract.md` E59). Every buffer whose size an input chooses is charged to `M_X` at `heap_cost` before it is allocated: RGBA and alpha sheets, the texture's source data, a sprite's mesh arrays (vertices, indices, triangles) and the affine path's buffers, sprite canvases, crops, masks, PNG rows and output (the output allocated once, at zlib's `deflateBound` of its filtered bytes, plus its chunks' framing), atlas JSON, the sprite-group table, the record buffer, a member's peek bits (step 4). zlib's deflate state, the sum of its allocations' `heap_cost` at Pillow's settings (about 398 KiB, 5.5), is charged as a constant before `deflateInit2`, and zlib allocates through `zalloc` and `zfree` functions that call Rust's global allocator, as flate2 1.1.10's C backend does (its `src/ffi/c.rs`), so the counting allocator sees its refusals (2.7). The charges go through `Meter::charge` or `DecodeBuf` (`limits/meter.rs`, `budget/mod.rs`): the core's rule of 3.15, applied to engine code. **Export work**, in bytes, sums every pixel byte the attempt writes (a decoded sheet, an alpha merge, an atlas crop, a Sprite's image, at its mode's bytes per pixel), unrounded; every file it spools (each PNG, each atlas JSON, and its records file per flush of its 64 KiB buffer, flushed only when full and at the end; a failing attempt flushes nothing after its failure and drops its partial buffer uncounted), each at `max(4 KiB, its size rounded up to 4 KiB)`, whatever the spool's `st_blksize`; and each member record (2.8) at the largest size its `archive_members` row can take, its `cut` included, over the values of `included` and `reason`, which holds the archive's key. Past `max_export_work` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.1) it fails X, `limit(export_work, <value>)`: a pixel increment is counted and checked when its buffer is charged, before the decode or render that fills it, and a file's before its claim. The one exception is a Sprite's render (E17), one call whose intermediates (its crop, canvases and masks) are bounded by `M_X`'s charges inside it and by its decode work (E59), its loops counted there, not by export work: its export work is its image's pixel bytes, counted when the render returns the image, after section 3's answer 11 (an image the PNG encoder refuses is the Sprite's skip, never counted) and before its PNG; its group's sheet is counted before it is decoded, and the alpha merge once its sizes match, before it is made. So the limit is a function of the bytes and the spec, and D holds across hosts. Its **spooled part** is the same sum without the pixel bytes, plus a failed input's failure record (2.4), a failure before dispatch included, as a records file of one flush, which it adds to `S` (5.6); the head's `spooled_parts` is the sum of every input's spooled part (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3). The spool counter still claims in blocks of `b`, the spool directory's `st_blksize`, which preflight reads (2.8), so with `k_blk = max(1, ceil(b / 4 KiB))`, or 2 when `b` is below 4 KiB and does not divide it, an input's share of it is at most `max_export_work x k_blk`, whatever its number of objects or crops and however small each is, a bound known before the run (5.6). A spool that fills anyway is `spool_full`, a run-level stop (D's first limit).
11. **Copy-out** (2.8). The attempt checks the stop flag between registrations, planned objects, sheet groups and outputs, before sealing, at each final records claim/write and before the final flush; a stopped attempt returns at once, and its end message says `Stopped`.
12. **End.** The commit point is the stop check immediately before the seal’s final flush starts: a stop seen at or before it ends `Stopped`, and an attempt that passes it and completes the flush is `Done`. A flush already under way is not interrupted; a stop requested after that check, during the flush included, belongs to the controller (B5–B7). The function returns, which drops everything above. The worker reads the budget (2.2), then sends the end message.

### 2.7 Outcomes

**Until CR10 (section 6), an allocator refusal at its inventoried core and
export sites aborts the process.** The existing head remains `running`, with
no normal finisher or `exit` line. Their meter charges bound accounted memory;
they do not reserve the owners' heap storage. These allocations are charged
within `M`, so refusal requires host overcommit, not input. B7c makes batch and CLI sites
fallible or reserves them before the running head. The release controller
refusal sweep names each CR10 exclusion and checks that it still allocates
infallibly; an abort outside that inventory fails the gate.

Every error is classified once, by one engine function, `classify` (CR2 would make it the core's). Its classes, and the reason each records (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.3):

| what failed | outcome |
|---|---|
| `Budget(Input)` or `Budget(Dependency)` refused by the attempt's budget; `Limit` of kind `InputMemory` (a CAB or resource name's lowering among them, `pystr.rs`), `InputBytes` or `DecodeWork`; the engine's `attempt_evictions` and `export_work` | the budget class (exit 5), recorded where it surfaced: an object-level skip inside an object's read or export; an input failure while opening X or an entry of X; `dependency_failed(<id>, <reason>)` while loading or reaching a view member. The engine's `attempt_evictions` and `export_work` are outside this site rule: each always fails X with its own reason (2.6 steps 7 and 10). A `Budget(Dependency)` after registration fails X wherever it surfaces: the core does not say whether a refusal is a reopen's, and a refused reopen would otherwise be tried again at every access (2.6 step 7). So `budget(dependency)` is never an object's skip: a dependency read that does not fit in `D` once every other dependency is evicted fails the input, and its fix is a larger `D`. Reasons: `budget(input)`, `budget(dependency)`, `limit(<kind>, <configured value>)` |
| any other `Limit` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.3's data kinds: a depth, a count, `BlockOutput`, `Inflate`) | a data reason (exit 3 or 4), at the same sites: `limit(<kind>, <configured value>)` |
| an error the bytes decide: `Detect`, `OutOfBounds`, `Malformed`, `Unsupported`, `Decompress`, `Version`, `TypeIndex`, `Tree`, `AssetBundle`, `Entry`, `NotFound`, a decoder panic (E18), E25's bytes | DESIGN section 4's failure model: a container or entry failure fails X (`container_error`, `entry_error`), an object's failure skips it (`decode_error`, `decoder_panic`). An `Entry` error met inside a dereference or a resource read is classified as its innermost error; only selection's open of X's top-level rows, or the reach of X's containers at registration, makes it X's failure (`entry_error`). The detail field takes `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3's form: the error's code, its innermost variant's, and for an `entry_error` the entry's path after it, from the end message (2.4); a `decoder_panic`'s is the panic's payload (2.11) |
| a texture decode's `unityrs_texture::DecodeError` (E15, E18, E59), in `BATCH_SPEC.md` section 3's order | `NoImageData`, `NegativeSize`, `PillowMemoryError`, `NotEnoughData` and `DecompressionBomb`: the texture's skip, `decode_error`, its detail `no_image_data`, `negative_size`, `pillow_memory_error`, `not_enough_data` or `decompression_bomb` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3). `TooLarge`: the texture's skip, `limit(texture_pixels, 268435456)`, a data reason (exit 3), whatever the data's length once it is not empty (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.3). `Charge(Limit(_))`: as the core's `LimitError` it holds, by its kind (the input's meter's `InputMemory`: `limit(input_memory, <configured value>)`, the budget class). `Charge(Budget(_))`: by the budget rows' rules, `budget(input)` where the attempt's budget refused, else the allocator's refusal, `host_memory`. `Panicked`: `decoder_panic`, its detail the payload's `message()` and `cut` its `is_cut()`: for a texture decode the crate's payload is the source, not the hook's slot (2.11), and the two cut alike, to 1,024 bytes at a code point boundary, `non-string panic payload` for a payload neither a `&str` nor a `String`. A variant it does not know (the enum is `#[non_exhaustive]`): unknown, below |
| `DerefError::NotFound` (no bundle of the view holds the CAB); `Error::NotFound(NotFound::Resource(_))` from the private Environment's resource read, after the own-chain lookup missed (2.6 step 8) | the object's skip, `outside_view(<cab>)`, a resource's argument being the stream path as read (E65) |
| a read whose value has not the shape the NGUI cutter reads (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.4 step 2's rule: a Texture2D UnityPy 1.25.2 cannot build, a side, format, stream, `image_data` or `m_PlatformBlob` of another type, a name read with no name field, a head, MonoScript or Material of another shape) | as a `ParseError`'s `Read`: the object's skip, `decode_error`, detail `read`; a head's or MonoScript's makes its MonoBehaviour no atlas, counted (`unresolved_scripts`), a Material's gives no name |
| `DerefError::{Null, FileIdPastEnd, FileIdBelowStart, RawTarget, MissingObject}` | the object's skip, `bad_pointer` |
| `DerefError::Load` | classified as its inner `Error` |
| `Error::Io`, or an I/O error of the engine's on the spool (but a full one, below) or a staged file | a run failure, `host_io` (exit 74) |
| an attempt or a peek during which the counting allocator's refusal count rose, whatever it recorded; a `Budget` refusal during whose core call the attempt's or the peek's budget's refusal count did not move; zlib's `Z_MEM_ERROR` | the allocator's refusal: a run failure, `host_memory` (exit 74) |
| a spool claim refused, free space below the reserve, or `ENOSPC` or `EDQUOT` from any spool call | a run failure, `spool_full` (exit 5), its detail `ENOSPC` or `EDQUOT` when the filesystem refused, `reserve` when the reserve check did, and `size_bytes` when the counter did: spool use depends on the order inputs ran in, so it decides no input |
| `Error::HintMismatch`, once CR7 lands: a load that refused the hint the engine built from its peek table, its row count not the directory's or a padding bit set (section 6) | a run failure, `internal` (exit 70): the engine built the hint, so a mismatch is the engine's bug, never the bundle's |
| `DerefError::ContainerDropped`; an error `classify` does not know; any other zlib error; a panic outside a decoder | a run failure, `internal` (exit 70): the attempt holds every container it reads through (2.6, step 7), and an unknown variant must be noticed at once |

**The peek is excepted** by 2.6 step 4's rule: what it meets is classified as above into the bundle's peek table, and an error it reads as "not a container" records nothing until a read of that entry meets it.

**Why every refusal is final at once.** The attempt runs on one thread in program order. Its budget and every meter that books with it serve that thread alone (2.2). So each charge meets the same state in every run, and each refusal, and each growth a refusal silently shrank (`limits/vec.rs`, `grow_counted`; `budget/mod.rs`, `DecodeBuf::into_bytes`), falls at the same point. No outcome waits for a retry.

**`classify` until CR2.** One module matches the public enums: every `Error` variant, every `DerefError` variant, `ParseError`'s `Read`, `Tree` and `Deref`, and its `CheckRead`, `NoTypeTree` and `Provider` (each `asset_bundle`, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3), `ReadError`'s detail, `TreeError`, `ChargeError`, `LimitKind` and `unityrs_texture::DecodeError`. A `DerefError` that `Environment::deref` returns bare is classified as that same chain. Every `#[non_exhaustive]` enum's wildcard arm returns "unknown". Its test builds each wrapper path and checks the class.

The recursive extraction classifier is `unityrs_export::ngui::classify`;
batch adapters check its closed reason vocabulary and add spool/work sites,
without a second recursive enum matcher. Known Sprite and Mesh render exceptions
are enumerated explicitly; unknown variants or mappings stop `internal`.

**CR10 provenance and audit.** Explicit `Allocator(AllocError)` failures are
`host_memory` immediately, regardless of budget-refusal counts. A separate
allocator observation also stops a successful core call that absorbed a refusal;
batch makes no subsequent read or publication from that attempt. Observations
survive charge release and are checked again after the attempt Environment drops.
This is separate from the legacy budget-count inference and allocator NULL count
below. PR 1 leaves the existing allocation implementations and abort inventory
in place; later CR10 PRs route each detected refusal through the observer.

**The allocator.** The core turns an allocator's refusal into a `Budget` error (`limits/meter.rs`, `refusal`; `limits/vec.rs`, `reserve_exact`), and several paths absorb one silently, among them: `grow_counted` retries a smaller growth, `into_bytes` keeps a buffer it could not move (`limits/vec.rs`, `budget/mod.rs`), the run cache's `publish` returns a run uncached (`files/bundle/runs.rs`), a caller of `settle` drops a reach's refusal (`env/registry/reach.rs`), `pin::copy_out` keeps a value's slices when its copy is refused (`pin.rs`), and `with_room` takes an allocator refusal on a dependency's meter for the pool's (`pool_refused`, `env/registry/dependency.rs`), evicts every other dependency and retries. So the engine counts refusals itself:
- **The counting allocator.** The binary's `#[global_allocator]` wraps `System` and counts each NULL it returns in a const-initialised thread-local `Cell<u64>`, which allocates nothing. zlib's allocations go through it too (2.6 step 10). A worker reads its count before and after each attempt; any increase makes the attempt's outcome `RunFailure(host_memory)`, whatever it recorded, absorbed refusals included. The engine's own charged buffers allocate through one fallible helper, a `try_reserve_exact`, and one that a phase budget charges stops the run `host_memory` when the allocator refuses it. A second thread-local keeps the thread's live bytes, in `heap_cost` units (each allocation's `heap_cost` added, each free's subtracted), and their peak, which the main thread reads in phase 1 to measure `C_spec` and `C_parse` (5.3): the main thread resets its peak to its live count at step 2's start, and `p` is that peak less that count. A growing `realloc` counts at its peak: the new block's `heap_cost` is added before the old one's is subtracted, so both count while it copies, and every reservation for a library's growing buffer is sized the same way (2.9). A shrinking one (the new size at most the old) subtracts the old block's, then adds the new one's, since glibc shrinks a block in place (core 3.15; the testkit's `counted.rs` does the same), unless `System::realloc` allocates, copies and frees instead (an alignment over 16, or a new size below the alignment: std's `realloc_fallback`), which counts at its peak, as a growth does.
- **The refusal count.** A `Budget` error from a core call during which the attempt budget's own refusal count did not move is the allocator's too (it catches liblzma's refusal on any meter, which no Rust allocation sees, when no budget refusal was absorbed earlier in the call).
- **What neither sees** is a C allocation. The TLS stack allocates in Rust (2.11 step 4). glibc's own allocations (`getaddrinfo`'s and NSS's, 5.4, and `opendir`'s buffer) surface their refusals as errors: a DNS failure is a transport error, an `opendir` failure `host_io`. liblzma's `malloc` is refused unseen (a) on a dependency's meter, which `with_room` retries after eviction (`codec/lzma.rs`, `liblzmas_memory_error_on_a_dependency_counts_as_a_pool_refusal`), or (b) on any meter, outside `with_room` too (a direct read on a dependency's handle), after a budget refusal absorbed earlier in the same core call (2.7's absorbing paths), which moved the count: D's third limit (section 1, A13), until CR2. A peek's budget never refuses, so in a peek every such refusal is the allocator's, `host_memory` (section 1).

Preflight keeps refusals rare within the bound: the main thread, at its step 6 (2.11), after the deadline's check and the header variables and before any spawn, refuses to start (exit 69) when `RLIMIT_AS` or `RLIMIT_DATA` is below 1.5 times 5.1's bound (glibc's slack, 5.4) plus 64 MiB per arena of `M_ARENA_MAX` and `3 MiB x (download_concurrency + 1)`, glibc's uncounted lookup heap (5.4) (`rlimit_too_low`), when `vm.overcommit_memory` is 2 (`overcommit_strict`), when `vm.max_map_count` is below `I / 128 KiB + 8,192` (`map_count_too_low`: a mapping per buffer at the mmap threshold, and room for the rest), or when 5.1's bound passes the smaller of `MemTotal` and the cgroup's limit (`memory_too_small`): the process's cgroup is its `/proc/self/cgroup` line for v1's memory controller when one is present (a hybrid host), else its `0::` line; under v2, the smallest finite `memory.max` along the chain of that cgroup and its ancestors, as far up as the mount shows; under v1, `hierarchical_memory_limit` in its `memory.stat`, which counts its ancestors' limits as v2's chain does. A cgroup path names the directory `/proc/self/mountinfo` gives: the mount point of the hierarchy's mount whose root is the longest prefix of the path, plus the path less that root; a path beneath no mount's root has no visible limit in that hierarchy. An absent hierarchy, or v1's absent `memory.stat`, means no cgroup limit, and a v2 level without `memory.max` is skipped, the walk going on; a file present but unreadable exits 69 (`cgroup_unreadable`), and an unreadable `/proc/sys/vm/*` or `/proc/meminfo` exits 69 (`procfs_unreadable`). It also raises `RLIMIT_NOFILE`'s soft limit to its hard limit and refuses one below `4 x threads + 16 x download_concurrency + 72` (`rlimit_too_low`): each attempt holds at most its records file, an output and a staged file or a peek file, and a peek its staged file or its peek file (2.6 step 4); each of the controller's `download_concurrency` permits at most five descriptors, its socket, one per request (2.5), among them: a stage its spool file and its source or socket; an archive's stream its member list, a member and its central directory, with its temp file and its destination's parent directory (`extract.md` E62) in a directory sink or its socket in a store; a file's transfer its output and its temp file or socket; an archive's hashing pass its member list, a member and its central directory; a directory destination's hash that file; the fifth, a directory archive's alone, is counted in its permit's lookup share (below), since a permit whose token is out runs no transfer. Each of the finisher's `download_concurrency` requests holds a socket or a temp file, and a row file and its sources (step 3's requests run one at a time), a stop's frozen requests beside them (`C_net`, 5.3); each of the `2 x (download_concurrency + 1)` lookups at most 5 resolver sockets (`MAXNS` UDP ones, a TCP one and the RFC 3484 sort's UDP one; W17; unverified against glibc's source), `10 x (download_concurrency + 1)` in all; `C_net`'s one more socket and the process's own descriptors (its locks, the self-pipe, the `timerfd`, the three eventfds (the controller's wake and end ones, the finisher's wake one), each runtime's epoll descriptor and its waker's, `exit_now`'s `/dev/null`, the standard descriptors) fit in the other 62. It raises `RLIMIT_FSIZE`'s soft limit to its hard limit and refuses a finite hard limit (`rlimit_too_low`). Last, the engine runs `n = threads + 7 + 2 x download_concurrency` threads (the workers, the main, controller, lifecycle, progress and finisher threads, and the two runtimes' blocking pools, 2.11), and refuses (`rlimit_too_low`) an `n` above `RLIMIT_NPROC`'s soft limit, unless the kernel exempts the process: in the initial user namespace, a real uid of 0 or an effective `CAP_SYS_RESOURCE` or `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` (the kernel's `capable` asks the initial namespace, so a capability of another counts nothing). The namespace is the initial one when `/proc/self/uid_map` is its identity map, `0 0 4294967295`, and the effective set is `capget`'s; an unreadable `uid_map`, or a `capget` that fails, exits 69 (`procfs_unreadable`). It also refuses (`rlimit_too_low`) an `n - 1`, the threads it adds, above `pids.max - pids.current` at any level of the cgroup chain above, in the `pids` controller's hierarchy, whose `pids.current` counts the main thread already. A level whose `pids.max` is `max`, or that has none, is skipped; an unreadable `pids.*` file exits 69 (`cgroup_unreadable`). The kernel counts `RLIMIT_NPROC` over every process of the uid: the check is the process's own: another process of the uid can still exhaust the limit, and a user namespace mapped whole reads as the initial one; step 7 then fails (`thread_spawn_failed`).

### 2.8 Copy-out, records and commit

- **Spool layout and lifecycle:** `BATCH_SPEC.md` section 6.
- **Each output:**
  1. It is encoded in charged memory, and its MD5, CRC-32 and visible hash (E20) are computed, and, for one over 16 MiB, its multipart ETag at 16 MiB parts (the ETag resume and verification expect, 2.9).
  2. Its size, rounded up to the spool directory's `st_blksize`, read once at preflight, is claimed from the spool counter (export work counts it in its own fixed unit, 2.6 step 10), an atomic try-add against `spool.size_bytes`, after a `statvfs` check against `spool.reserve_bytes`. For a growing file, claim only the increase in its rounded cumulative length, never a fresh rounded block per write. Every spool write claims this way before it writes: staged bundles (2.5), peek files (2.6 step 4), outputs, record flushes, sorted runs, member lists, archives' central directories (claimed as they grow, by 2.5's rule for a body without a stated size), the outcomes files and row files. A claim counts space and does not size the file: a file's length is always its content, and no file is preallocated.
  3. It is written to the attempt's directory as `o<plan_index>`, its zero-based
     ordinal assigned to retained planned outputs before grouped export, and its
     buffer is dropped before the next output. Export skips leave ordinal gaps;
     sink arrival order never determines filenames.
- **Records.** Candidate records (key, unit key, input, `serialized_file` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3), kind, path id, MD5, CRC-32, visible hash, mode, size, multipart ETag or none, and `file`, its name within the attempt's directory, a function of the output's plan index), one member record per archive member path a candidate expands to (archive index, path, key, input; `BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.7), skip records (with `serialized_file`) and diagnostics are appended to the attempt's `records.ndjson` through a fixed 64 KiB buffer charged to `M_X`. A member path that breaks a rule of `BATCH_SPEC.md` 2.3 (`prefix_conflict` aside, which step 5 decides) is recorded with that rule and its path cut as below. A string a record takes from the bytes (a name, a CAB, a reason's argument) is cut to its first 1,024 bytes, at a code point boundary, with the cut marked (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3). Records are appended in the attempt's record order (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.4), which the `skips` and `diagnostics` rows keep, so nothing sorts them. No record holds the input's position, the run id or a spool path, so its size is a function of the input's bytes and the spec; phase 6 builds each spool path when it joins (2.9).
- **The line bound.** A record holds at most four strings, each at most 1,024 bytes before escaping: an id (no control character), a key or member path (no control character, `too_long` past 1,024 bytes), or a cut string. JSON escaping writes a byte as at most 6 (`\u00XX`, or `�` for a byte that is not UTF-8, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3), and the other fields take under 1 KiB. So every line is at most 32 KiB, whatever the number of archives.
- **Library ownership.** `unityrs_batch::spool::Directory::create` owns a newly created prepared directory; `output(plan_index)` and `records()` return sequential writers. `records::copy_output` counts file work, hashes borrowed bytes and closes the output; `records::Writer` charges the 64 KiB buffer and seals with a terminal summary. `Writer::member` takes the final archive key to count the largest manifest row. `write_failure_record` writes a separate owned directory. Writers must close before `Directory::rename` or `remove`; rename preserves claims, drop releases none, and only successful `remove` returns them. No library operation publishes a commit. The typed line contract is in `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3, provisional until B7's manifest.
- **Commit** (`extract.md` E60, all or nothing per input).
  - An attempt that ends `Done` has its directory renamed to `done/<input>/` by the controller, in 2.4's commit order, unless the stop word holds a reason at its load, when it is `stopped`.
  - An input that fails commits only its failure record (2.4): no candidate, so it competes for no key. The pool writes the record into `fail/<input>/`, and the controller renames it, as it renames a `Done` attempt's: only the controller publishes a commit.
  - Deleting an attempt's directory returns its claimed bytes to the spool counter.

### 2.9 Winners and publish

**Winners** (E31 to E34, E61; `BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.5 to 4.7), in phase 6. Each step below is an external sort of spool records and one streaming pass over the result, so phase 6 holds no table that grows with keys, units, candidates or members.
- **The sort.** `unityrs_batch::sort` takes a projection validator and total comparator. A packed arena and fixed-width index, including their allocation capacities and `heap_cost`, occupy at most 32 MiB; an in-place unstable sort allocates no comparison scratch. Drop them before merging. At most 16 inputs and one output each have a 1 MiB allocation envelope; other active storage, including carry frames, decoding, catalogs, paths and accounting overhead, stays within 8 MiB. Committed NDJSON lines remain bounded to 32 KiB including newline. Private runs have a 16-byte magic/version/projection-tag header and length-framed records, at most 64 KiB **including** their little-endian `u32` length. Projection schemas use fixed-width little-endian integers, explicit optional/enum tags and bounded UTF-8 fields; comparators use decoded semantics, not encoded integer bytes. Canonical payload bytes complete comparison ties, so a heap may use run index only for identical records. These encoded internal records, including framing, are subject to the twice-counted-size condition: each projection/join must prove in a test that its encoded size is at most twice the source record's counted work (2.6 step 10). The frame ceiling alone proves no expansion bound.
- **Ownership and execution.** A workspace creates files through `spool::OwnedFile` and the shared counter; sealed length/claim capabilities go into disk catalogs, with no owner or path vector growing with runs. Each merge reads contiguous catalog batches of at most 16, uses a fixed heap, closes exhausted inputs before unlink and releases only successful deletions. Catalogs retire after their last reader closes. Failed writes, partial deletion, cancellation and drop retain claims; no adoption from physical length and no drop-time flush. One serial phase-6 blocking task runs on the controller's spare permit, through a directly driven seam until its pool exists. It checks stop at entry, pass boundaries, before/after sorting, after sealing a run, between merge groups, on bounded reads, before writes (at most 1 MiB), before retirement and before returning readiness. After stop there is no compensating flush/cleanup; at most one already-authorized buffer can acquire a claim. Each write checks reserve, claims rounded cumulative growth, checks stop again, then writes. Equality at the reserve succeeds. Phase writes do not increase an input's export work.
- **Ingestion and phase account.** `records::read` decodes flat typed records in fixed charged buffers, without whole-file, JSON-tree or per-string allocation. It accepts ordinary JSON whitespace, including padded summary integers, and preserves repaired text and `cut`. It checks input identity, canonical output ordinals, digest/enum shapes, archive indices and copy-out's candidate-followed-by-members identity/digest relation. Exactly one terminal summary must match the committed success/failure fact; a failed input has only its failure summary. Duplicate candidates and missing unit roots are checked by later sorts/joins. Corrupt committed/private artifacts are `internal` with no culprit; read I/O is `host_io`; spool-full mutations retain their exact detail. One Input-only phase account of limit `I` is reused by phase 7. A sort audits scratch against its entry charge, allowing a borrowed charged source between passes; the whole phase audits zero after all scratch drops. Panics, leaks, and phase-account refusals within the 40 MiB proof are `internal`; allocator refusals, including absorbed ones, are `host_memory`, subject to the existing severity precedence. The controller retains account/workspace/execution owners through handover and checks canonical stop before advancing.
- **`/`-first order** (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.6) compares two strings bytewise, except that at the first byte where they differ a `/` is less than any other byte; a string that is a prefix of the other is less. It is a comparison, not a mapping of `/` to a low byte, so it holds for any bytes a path holds. In it every key's descendants (the keys that start with it and `/`) follow it contiguously, so one look at the next line tells whether a key is an ancestor of another.
- **Step 1, units.** Sort every unit line by (unit key, input id). One pass decides each unit key's winning unit by E31 and E61, keeping only the best candidate so far: within a key the candidates come in input order, so rules 1 to 3 need no more. Every other unit of the key is ineligible (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.5).
- **Step 2, member keys.** Sort one line (member key, unit key) per key of every winning unit, its unit key included, and per key of every unit of a unit key E61 withholds (one line per key and unit key). One pass: of the unit keys claiming one key, the first in bytewise order of unit key keeps it, and every other's winning unit is withheld whole (`unit_conflict`). A unit key E61 withholds stays `withheld` where it also loses a claim here: its keys' reason, and its units' candidates' rule, is `withheld`, not `unit_conflict`. Every key a winning unit claims stays reserved, even when its unit is withheld, so the pass is not repeated (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.5).
- **Step 3, plain keys.** Merge the sorted plain candidates and the ineligible units' candidates with step 2's reserved keys. A plain candidate for a reserved key loses (`unit`). A key that only ineligible units' candidates hold is withheld, `unit_lost`, its row the first by input. Every other key is decided by E31 and E61 among its plain candidates, streaming as in step 1. The output is the set `P` of keys the decision would publish, in key order.
- **Step 4, prefix and archive conflicts** (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.6). Sort `P`'s keys and the archives' keys together in `/`-first order, an archive's key before an equal key of `P`. One pass refuses a key `K` of `P` when the next line starts with `K/` (`prefix_conflict`: an ancestor of a key of `P` or of an archive's), else when `K` equals the last archive key seen or starts with it and `/` (`archive_conflict`). One archive key seen is enough, since validation makes archive keys pairwise not ancestors. The refused keys are sorted back to bytewise order and merged with `P`. A unit with a key so refused is withheld whole: its unit keys are sorted, and a merge with `P` marks its other keys `unit_conflict`. The check is not repeated. What is left is the **winners file**, in bytewise key order.
- **Step 5, archive members** (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.7). Sort the member records by (key, input) and merge them with the winners file, keeping those of each winner's candidate. A path recorded with a rule of `BATCH_SPEC.md` 2.3 (`unwritable_path`) goes to the rows only and takes no part in what follows. Sort the rest by (archive, member path in `/`-first order, key). One pass keeps the first key for a path (`duplicate_member`) and refuses a path that the archive's next distinct path starts with, followed by `/` (`prefix_conflict`), and writes each archive's member list, in member order, with each member's spool file, size and CRC-32.
- **Rows.** Steps 1 to 4 leave each key's decision (its status, rule and flip, and its row's input, MD5 and visible hash: the keeper's winner, or `BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.5's first holder, for a reserved key; the first candidate for a `unit_lost` key) in a decision file in key order, beside step 1's decision of each unit key (its rule, its winner, and whether E61 or step 2 withheld it). The `outputs` and `collisions` row files are one merge of the key-sorted candidates with them, one line per (key, candidate) for collisions, each candidate's `rule` as `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3 defines it: a unit's candidate's from its unit key's decision, a plain candidate's from its key's; `archive_members` comes from step 5.
- With `publish_incomplete: false` and a failed input, phase 7 publishes nothing and runs neither resume nor verification: every winner, and every archive with a member, is `withheld` (`publish_incomplete`), on a stop in phase 7 as at a normal end, and the head's `verified` is null (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3, 7.4).
- The decision is a pure function of the committed records, so it is the same whatever order the inputs finished in.
- **Its working set** is at most 40 MiB: a run of 32 MiB, or a merge's 17 MiB, beside at most 8 stream buffers of 1 MiB. `I >= 64 MiB` holds it, so a refusal of the phase budget in phase 6 marks a leak, a run failure `internal`.

**Canonical archive passes (B8a, B8b).** `archive::Payloads` is B7b's single numeric seam, `publish::payload::Payloads`, shared with loose publication: it opens immutable committed payloads by numeric source/plan ordinal; record reading is separate. Member paths and loose destinations never locate payloads. B8b schedules archives after every winner has finished publishing, in archive-key order: the serial drive takes them after its winners, and the pool starts its archive drives only once every winner drive has been received (an exhausted winner cursor is not enough, since a last winner may still be transferring). A drive copies one bounded descriptor (key, spec index, counts, size and the moved list capability) out of the shared metadata reader under its lock, then runs that archive's passes itself, so a resume miss transfers next on the same drive, ahead of later keys. Resume first stats the destination without following it: anything but a regular file of the archive's size on the root's mount transfers at once, and only a same-size candidate earns an expected-hash pass. Its 16 MiB driver buffer is the stream's transport; a serial run that declares archives admits its drive at `WORKING_SET + ENVELOPE`, and the pool admits winner drives at `WORKING_SET` and archive drives at `WORKING_SET + ENVELOPE`. Eligibility (`empty`, then withheld, then oversized) is decided before any payload, scratch or temp; an oversized archive's `failed` outcome is written under the commit gate. A transfer opens its parent, stats the final name without following it (a directory or another mount there is `publish_failed` at once) and opens its temp (`winners + spec index`) before the stream exists, so a destination-kind conflict (`publish_failed`) arises only before central scratch or the temp is created, or at the rename after it is retired; sink write and writeback errors stop the run. Any failure but a stop removes the temp best-effort (a stop may leave it for the next attempt's create to replace). Writeback starts each time the written bytes reach a multiple of 64 MiB and once after the stream completes, before the rename: `size / 64 MiB + 1` per archive.

The pull stream holds the existing two 65,532-byte member-frame buffers, the 1,024-byte previous path, a 4 KiB header buffer and a 64 KiB central write buffer. Replay reads directly into caller storage; a directory transport adds one charged 64 KiB buffer. Hash state is three MD5 states plus the current payload's CRC/MD5 and fixed counters. There is no member, payload or part-digest vector. Adapter/path allowance is `2 × heap_cost(workspace_path_bytes + 22) + 8,192`, covering coexisting generated paths and boxed readers; payload adapters must honor that fixed allowance. At ordinary filesystem path bounds these capacities and overhead stay below a 1 MiB per-stream admission envelope, independent of member count and payload size. `archive_alloc` supplies counted blocking-thread children and an allocation-ordinal refusal sweep; those full Cargo gates must pass before claiming measured acceptance. Shared-`I` admission and permit lifetimes belong to B8b; concurrent streams must not use the serial phase audit independently.

Each active pass creates one independently owned raw central file through `OwnedFile`, sharing the workspace counter and aggregate ledger. A checked atomic numeric file ID gives every pass, including regeneration, a distinct name; no path/owner table grows with archives. Filesystem operations hold no workspace mutex. Writers check stop/reserve, claim rounded cumulative growth, check stop again, then write. Seal closes the writer; replay checks the exact central length and EOF. Only explicit deletion after reader closure releases claims. A successful stream summary requires retirement; a failed retirement stops the run. Failure, cancellation and Drop preserve claims and perform no compensating flush or cleanup.

For archive `i`, let `C_i = round_up(layout_i.central_size, b)`. Extend the following retained handoff allowance by the sum of the largest `min(download_concurrency, archive_count)` values of `C_i`, plus the existing separately provisioned outcome files. A completed expected-hash pass must delete its central file and release pass resources before a resume miss starts transfer. Hence ordinary scheduling has at most one central file per active pass; stopped/failed owners retain claims and stop scheduling rather than accumulating abandoned files. The `6S + 2A` extraction bound is unchanged. Archive bytes stream to sink temps and never form a complete-ZIP spool allowance.

`archive_ledger` extends the actual B4/B6 shared-counter path through 256 escaped archive keys, two concurrent central owners, retirement and fresh-pass regeneration, at blocks 512, 3,072, 4,096, 5,003 and 65,536. Stop/fault tests pin retained partial/unwritten claims and successful release only on deletion. This is the B8a component proof. B8b's publication extension: each drive runs one pass at a time, and `Stream::finish` retires the pass's central file before it returns a summary, so an expected-hash pass's file is deleted before its miss's transfer creates another, and no drive holds two; at most `min(download_concurrency, L)` drives exist, so live scratch stays within the largest-`C_i` term. A failed retirement is a run failure that stops scheduling; a stopped or failed pass keeps its claim (no Drop cleanup) until the next start's owned cleanup. `archive_publish` pins this through the real B6 hand-over at the same five blocks (live claims at every archive buffer, with expected-hash and transfer passes for every archive, never exceed the largest `C_i` and all return), a stop mid-archive retaining its claim, and the pool's drives inside `WORKING_SET + ENVELOPE`; `archive_publish_faults` pins the publication fault matrix for archives (temp `ENOSPC`/`EDQUOT`/`EIO` on write, writeback and rename, `EINTR` and short writes, a rename-time kind conflict, an outcome failure after the rename, the rename-to-outcome window) and that no archive starts while the pool's final winner is still transferring. Lifetime acceptance stays B9's, and complete memory/squeeze acceptance B12's.

**Phase-6 artifact-size and lifetime ledger.**
Let `b` be preflight's block size, `E` the sum of encoded frames in one sort,
`n` its initial run count, `P = E + n × (16 + b - 1)`, and
`K = round_up(16 + 32 × n, b)`. A merge has at most `2P` live run
claims and `2K` catalog claims: consumed runs retire immediately, and old
catalogs retire before the next generation. `R` includes every other live
phase file, including a streamed pass's output while its input is read.
Committed files are separately provisioned by `S` and are never retired here.

The allowance is **`R + 2P + 2K <= 6S + 2A`**, where
`A = sum(round_up(e(key) + 512, b))` over declared archives. `A` is known
from the spec alone: `e(key)` is the exact JSON-escaped byte length of the
key as emitted in the archive row, excluding its surrounding quotes.
Archive rows and `archives.bin` each fit `A`, including
per-row rounding; their fixed fields use at most 512 bytes. The preference
index stays in measured phase-1 memory, so it adds no disk term.

The motivating counterexample remains: one successful empty input has one
4 KiB counted summary and no candidate or member work. At `b = 4,096`,
256 archives with four-byte keys require 28,672 bytes of compact rows;
1,024-byte keys require 290,816 bytes, or `71S`, against the old `6S =
24,576`. Each compact empty row including newline is `e(key) + 102`
bytes. Long keys use distinct first segments and four subsequent segments
of lengths 255, 255, 255 and 251. These spec-derived rows cannot be charged
to extraction work. The new `2A` term covers rows and private metadata
living together, even when `S` contains no candidates.

The extraction schedule rescans immutable committed records rather than
retaining an extra full candidate projection. All intermediate inventories
are external streams, never per-key in-memory sets. A sorted source retires
after its last reader closes, before the next sort; a replacement decision
stream retires its predecessor at that boundary. Empty streams need no file.

| pass | retained extraction streams (`R`, including current pass outputs) | spec-derived claims | retirement boundary |
|---|---|---|---|
| root/instance validation and root selection | root projection, unit decisions | 0 | instance projection after validation; root projection after selection |
| reservation projection and least-unit keeper | unit decisions, claims/keepers and conflict markers | 0 | claims after keeper selection; markers after unit-state join |
| whole-unit conflict join and reservation reorder | unit decisions, reserved keys and replacement streams | 0 | superseded states and reservation order after their join |
| plain-key decisions | unit decisions, reservations, key decisions | 0 | reservations after key join; candidates are rescanned |
| prefix refusal and unit propagation | unit decisions, key decisions, refusal/affected-unit streams | archive keys are read from prepared indices | refusals and affected-unit streams after final join; old decisions after replacement |
| winners handoff | final unit/key decisions, winners | 0 | retained for B6c readers |
| member selection and rows (B6c) | decisions, winners, member lists, output/collision/member rows | archive rows and metadata, at most `2A` | each intermediate at its final reader; handoff files through finishing |

Here is the B6b upper bound, including rounding rather than relying only
on the twice-size condition. Let `N` be the number of candidates, `T` the
sum of their full private frame sizes, `J` their committed record bytes
(the counted records flushes cover them), and `C` their counted output
work after `k_blk`. Then `T <= J` and `C >= N × max(4,096, b)`.
A full frame has at most 133 fixed/framing bytes and strings of at most
1,024 + 1,024 + 1,024 + 3,072 bytes (the last permits repaired text), so
one frame is at most 6,277 bytes. JSON's field names, digest hex and
escaping cover the binary fixed fields, including decision fields absent
from the source. A claim, decision, refusal or unit marker replaces a
candidate; it never multiplies it in a single stream. Each projection
therefore has `E <= T <= J <= S`, stronger than the required `2S`.

At a sort boundary the table's retained extraction streams are at most
four candidate-sized streams: unit state, key state, and two marker or
replacement inputs. Each file's header and rounding cost at most `b + 15`.
Thus `R <= 4T + 4(b + 15)`. Production runs have `n <= N`; hence

`R + 2P + 2K <= 6T + (2N + 6)b + 94N + 90 <= 6J + 6C <= 6S`

for `N >= 2`. The middle inequality follows directly from
`C >= N × max(4,096, b)`; its smallest margin is at `N = 2`.
A direct join, without a sorter, can have five streams while replacing
unit/key state, so `R <= 5T + 5(b + 15) <= 6S` there. This covers the
keepers/markers split, the unit-state replacement and the refusal overlay;
there is no sorter running beside those growing output files.
For `N = 1`, use the tighter retained-file bound
`R <= 4 × round_up(T + 16, b)`, `n = 1`, and the records flush containing
at least `T + 256` bytes including the terminal summary. The singleton
arithmetic test exhausts every frame length through 6,277 and every block
size through 6,293; above that all frames fit one block and the inequality
reduces to `8b + 2T + 30 <= 12b`. With `N = 0`, B6b retains no phase
files. An empty sort temporarily writes one catalog header, at most `b`
bytes, and retires it; `2K <= 2S` covers that edge. Archive keys merge
from prepared `/`-first indices, not spool runs.

This is a pass-by-pass B6b proof: roots retain at most two streams;
claim generation at most two; marker sorting at most three; reservation
reorders at most two; key sorting at most two; prefix refusal/marker sorts
at most four; propagation reorders at most three; final winners are a
direct join with three streams. Closed readers precede every retirement.
The instrumented tests check both each actual peak and each sort's
`R + 2P + 2K` envelope. Forced tiny runs use their actual `n`; this
production proof does not promise that every arbitrary test setting fits.

**B6c: complete rows, membership and handoff.** The same allowance holds
for every pass at production run settings (32 MiB, fan-in 16). Let `J` now
mean the total encoded committed candidate bytes, `N` their count, `F` the
counted records-flush work, and `M` the sum of B4's largest final member-row
work over **all expansions**, including losers and invalid paths. Thus
`F >= max(J + 256, 4,096)` and `S >= k_blk × (F + 4,096N + M)`.
Skipped/diagnostic records and actual payload sizes only increase `S`.
Write `h = b + 15` for a framed file's header/rounding overhead.

| B6c pass | retained extraction streams, including growing outputs | size envelope / retirement |
|---|---|---|
| row candidate projection and annotation reorder (`Rows`) | unit/key decisions, winners, candidate/annotation source | `R <= 4J + 4h`; same candidate-sort proof as B6b; candidate projection retires before annotation sort |
| direct output/collision row join (`Rows`) | unit/key decisions, winners, annotations, output and collision rows | `R <= 6J + 64N + 6h`; decisions and annotations retire after their final readers close |
| expansion identity sort (`Expansions`) | winners and output/collision rows | `R <= 3J + 64N + 3h`, `E <= M`; expansion input retires after winner join |
| winner/member join and selection reorder (`Expansions`) | preceding three streams and expansion/replacement stream | direct join adds at most `2M + 2h`; sort retains at most `M + h`; loser expansions are discarded |
| valid-path decisions (`Membership`) and display reorder (`MemberRows`) | preceding three streams, selection/replacement stream | same bounds; selection input retires before display sort; valid paths precede invalid paths only for selection |
| final rows/lists and archive metadata (`Handoff`) | winners, output/collision rows, display input, growing member rows and included lists | `R <= 3J + 64N + 4M + 6b + 90 + 2A`; display input retires after its last reader; fixed handoff stays through finishing |

The encoded-record bounds are stronger than twice counted size:

- Candidate projections/annotations/winners have framed size at most their
  committed candidate line size. Output base rows use at most that line plus
  64 bytes (the longest refusal reason); collision rows use at most the line.
  Every candidate line exceeds 64 bytes, so each is at most twice its counted
  size. Output rows select one representative per key; collision rows select
  at most one row per candidate. Neither duplicates candidates in one stream.
- Every member projection, joined frame and included-list frame has 58 fixed
  and framing bytes plus the four raw UTF-8 fields; its maximum is 6,202 bytes.
  The counted final member row has those same strings JSON-escaped and more
  fixed bytes, including hexadecimal MD5 and the largest reason. Therefore
  each frame is at most that expansion's counted member work. A public member
  row is at most that work by B4's definition. Selection never multiplies an
  expansion within one stream. Headers and block rounding are accounted
  separately below, rather than hidden in the twice-size test.

`winners::sizes` tests the actual encoders against B4's actual accounting,
including maximum escaped and repaired fields, every decision state and rule.
Lists use exactly the tested included-member frame. No extraction stream is
reclassified as spec-derived.

For each member sort, the retained bound is
`R <= 3J + 64N + M + 4h`; use the earlier `P` and `K` definitions to obtain

`R + 2P + 2K <= 3J + 64N + 3M + (2n + 6)b + 94n + 90`.

At production settings, every nonfinal run contains over 16 MiB of member
frames: the arena is 25,159,680 bytes, no frame exceeds 6,202 bytes, and the
index cannot fill first even with minimum-size frames. Consequently
`n <= 1 + M / (16 MiB)`. The extra-run overhead is less than `k_blk M`,
since `b <= 4,096 k_blk` and `(8,192 + 94) / (16 MiB) < 1`.
For `N >= 1`, `3J <= 3 k_blk F`; the remaining `3 k_blk F` and
`6 × 4,096N × k_blk` cover `8b + 64N + 184`, including the singleton.
The remaining member allowance covers `3M` and the extra-run overhead.
Thus the member-sort envelope is at most `6S`. The two direct member joins
are smaller. The candidate-row direct bound in the table fits `6S` too:
its payload is at most `6J`, while output-work floors and the counted summary
cover `64N + 6h`. The arithmetic gate includes singleton, large-count, large
member-work and unusual-block edges. Arbitrary tiny test cuts still use their
actual measured `n`; no claim is made for every possible forced cut.

Archive metadata uses raw key bytes plus **35 fixed bytes per empty framed
record**, or **67** with a 32-byte list capability. There is one 16-byte file
header. Even assigning that header to each record gives at most 83 fixed
bytes, below 512. Base archive rows have at most 192 fixed bytes, including
two maximal `u64` counts, status, reason and newline; digests/size remain null.
Since raw key length is at most `e(key)`, each spec-derived stream separately
fits `A`.

The last pass also accounts for per-archive list headers/rounding, which cannot
be bounded by pretending all lists are one file. For raw/encoded key lengths
`r <= e`, `q = round_up(e + 512, b)`, a nonempty archive obeys

`e + r + 192 + 67 + 16 + b - 1 <= 2q + e`.

This follows by splitting at `b = e + 512`; the arithmetic gate exhausts the
key-length boundaries. The extra `e` is already counted in at least one of
that archive's expansions, so its sum is at most `M`. Pooled archive-row and
metadata rounding/header costs at most another `2b + 15`. Combining these
with list contents, member rows and the still-live display stream gives the
last table entry. It is at most `6S + 2A`: `4M <= 6 k_blk M`, and records/output
floors cover the remaining candidate and file overhead as above. Empty
archives have no list; with `N = 0`, only archive rows/metadata remain and fit
`2A`, while temporary empty-sort catalogs fit `6S`.

The original quote-key gate now passes under encoded-length `A`, without
changing its encoding or measured retention. At `b = 512`, its 256 keys have
`A = 655,360`, `S = 4,096`, and allowance **1,335,296** bytes. The complete
production handoff retains rows of 548,608 bytes (claim 548,864) and metadata
of 271,120 bytes (claim 271,360), for **820,224** additional bytes. The actual
counter peak equals that retention. Both the independent encoder and actual
complete-path gate retain this case. Six key families (short, long, quotes,
backslashes, permitted C1 and multibyte UTF-8) pass at blocks 512, 3,072,
4,096, 5,003 and 65,536. Writes beyond the allowance still refuse with retained
partial claims.

Complete-path production-setting additional peaks for the membership fixture
(bytes; each actual peak and each sort envelope fits `6S + 2A`):

| B6c pass | b = 3,072 | b = 4,096 | b = 5,003 | b = 65,536 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| rows, including annotation sorts | 15,360 | 20,480 | 25,015 | 327,680 |
| expansions and winner join | 21,504 | 24,576 | 30,018 | 393,216 |
| membership | 15,360 | 16,384 | 20,012 | 262,144 |
| display reorder | 21,504 | 24,576 | 30,018 | 393,216 |
| final lists/rows/metadata | 24,576 | 28,672 | 35,021 | 458,752 |

At 4 KiB, the fixture has `S = 89,512`, `A = 16,384`, allowance 569,840;
its largest conservative sort envelope is 34,410. At the other blocks the
allowances are 1,098,720, 1,114,168 and 9,117,440 respectively.

Counted release children on native aarch64 macOS (Rust 1.99.1) generate
records incrementally and retain no dataset beside the measured phase.
At 4 KiB, production runs with 350,000 candidates
and members read 229,028,225 committed bytes; a forced 512 KiB-run case has
1,000,000 of each; a 100,000-member unit exercises unit-size independence.

| scene | `S` | allowance `6S + 2A` | actual additional peak | largest sort envelope | counted memory peak | phase-account peak |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| production, 350,000 | 1,727,731,936 | 10,366,399,808 | 529,735,680 | 598,553,814 | 34,010,688 | 34,141,312 |
| forced scale, 1,000,000 | 4,936,778,368 | 29,620,678,400 | 1,513,861,120 | 1,717,254,508 | 2,493,008 | 2,623,088 |
| production, 100,000-member unit | 494,178,368 | 2,965,078,400 | 165,826,560 | 175,536,892 | 34,271,888 | 34,402,480 |

All fourteen pass peaks and sort envelopes pass. Counted children also refuse
every reachable allocation ordinal in the complete path, concrete committed
adapter and B7/B8 readers (3,325 member/withholding and 2,433 conflict-scene
ordinals); every refusal is `host_memory`, with zero kept bytes and zero
outstanding phase charges. Their maximum counted peak is 34,271,952 bytes and
phase-account peak 34,402,480. A deliberately insufficient phase account is
`internal`. These are local phase proofs, not B12's process-level acceptance.

Measured production-setting additional spool peaks (bytes), with all retained

983,040, 1,966,080 and 15,728,640 bytes respectively, and `A = b` for its
single declared archive. Both the measured peaks and each sort's conservative
`R + 2P + 2K` envelope pass `6S + 2A` at every boundary.

The independent B6b minimal-metadata gate peaks at 299,008 bytes for 256
long keys at 4 KiB, against `6S + 2A = 2,121,728`; its short-key peak is
36,864. The complete B6c metadata produces peaks of 565,248 and 40,960
respectively; quotes/backslashes peak at 823,296. The other three block sizes
and both key lengths also pass, including 8- and 64-input scale cases and a refusal beyond the cap.
The gate also covers maximum-byte keys containing quotes, backslashes, C1
controls and multibyte Unicode at all four block sizes. Controls below U+0020
and U+007F are forbidden in keys; permitted C1 characters stay UTF-8 in JSON.
At `b = 512`, the quote and backslash families each claim 548,352 row bytes,
exceeding the former raw-length `A = 393,216`, but fitting encoded-length
`A = 655,360` (and `6S + 2A = 1,335,296`).

The counted scale children write their dataset before measuring selection.
At production settings, 350,000 candidates occupy 177,928,225 record bytes:
`S = 1,611,530,240`, phase spool peak 264,867,840, counted heap peak
34,010,752 and account peak 34,141,312 bytes. With 1,000,000 candidates and
512 KiB forced runs, record bytes are 508,778,225, `S = 4,604,780,544`,
phase spool peak 784,244,736, counted heap peak 2,493,072 and account peak
2,623,088 bytes. The latter exceeds 256 initial runs, hence three merge
generations at fan-in 16. All scratch charges clear; both memory peaks fit
40 MiB. A 100,000-candidate single unit has `S = 460,980,224`, spool
peak 105,312,256, counted heap peak 34,271,904 and account peak 34,402,480
bytes, also with zero outstanding scratch. A separate child refuses all
1,160 selection allocation ordinals,
requiring `host_memory` and zero outstanding charges at every ordinal.

`tests/phase6_ledger.rs` is the empty-archive gate: real B4 empty commits,
256 archives, both key lengths, block sizes 3,072, 4,096, 5,003 and 65,536,
and scaled input counts. It holds rows and independently encoded metadata
at once, checks their peak against `6S + 2A`, then proves a write beyond
that cap still returns `spool_full(size_bytes)` without returning claims
on drop. `tests/sort_scale.rs` separately measures production runs and
multi-generation forced runs. Selection tests must measure each actual
pass with all retained files live; the table is a lifetime obligation,
not a claim that the sorter alone proves the complete decision pipeline.

The phase-6 committed-failure adapter uses the controller's committed state
and received attempt state. Any additional compact failure bit is retained in
measured phase-1 preparation; missing payload files do not imply failure, and
no unmeasured per-input table is introduced after extraction.

**Publish**, in phase 7.
- **Resume** (E43). With `resume` set, the object store's LIST of the output prefix (keys in ascending UTF-8 byte order) is merged with the winners file, one LIST page in memory at a time, beside the scheduler (Transfers, below). The merge never waits on the scheduler or on a hashing pass: it keeps the `ETag` and size listed for each of the spec's archives (`C_base`, 5.3), and the scheduler takes a winner or an archive only once the merge has passed its key (W19). A winner whose key is listed with its expected ETag (its MD5; a file's over 16 MiB: the multipart ETag its record carries, 2.8; an archive's: its multipart ETag at 16 MiB parts, `BATCH_SPEC.md` section 8) is `present` and not PUT. An archive's expected ETag, or in a directory its MD5, needs its bytes: resume computes it in a first pass over the archive's stream, without uploading, when the scheduler takes that archive. That pass and a directory destination's hash (E43) are **hashing passes**, each reading the stop flag before each buffer and returning when it is set, as 2.5's local copy does: each holds a `download_concurrency` permit and its buffers' `heap_cost` (an archive's: its stream's buffers, below) reserved from the phase budget, each waited for as a transfer's is (below); so at most `download_concurrency` central directories are live at once. A hashing pass returns its result to the controller and ends, returning both. The scheduler goes on to later keys meanwhile, and takes the transfer of a key whose pass found no match ahead of any later key once a permit and its reservation are free (Transfers, below). A listed location not above the one before it is skipped when its size is 0: `Path::parse` lists a folder marker `k/` as `k` (`src/path/mod.rs`), which no output can be, since none is empty. Any other LIST page out of order, or a LIST that fails, degrades to "PUT everything" with the warning `resume_list_failed`. In a directory, each winner's destination is checked as E43 says.
- **A LIST** goes through `PaginatedListStore::list_paginated` with `max_keys` 1000, the prefix as given, and each call first reserves 40 MiB from the phase budget, waited for as a transfer's is (below), and holds it until the merge drops the page: `object_store` reads a page's body whole and parses it with `quick_xml::de::from_reader` (`src/aws/client.rs`), whose `overlapped-lists` feature keeps, with no limit, every event it skips while it fills a list (quick-xml 0.38's `src/de/mod.rs`). A page of 1,000 keys of at most 1,024 bytes, each byte escaped to at most 6, with its other fields, is under 7 MiB of body, the connector's cap for it, and the connector's scan bounds what else a page holds (below). At those bounds, each growing `realloc` counted at its peak (2.7), a page's parse holds: its body, under 14 MiB, since `collect_bytes` grows a chunked body by doubling (`src/util.rs`) and the `Bytes` keeps that capacity; its owned copies, under 16.5 MiB, each tag's bytes, name, text run or kept string at most twice the body bytes it copies (a merged run's doubling, an empty element's name in its start and its end), in at most 4 x 16,064 + 3,002 allocations of at most 32 bytes' `heap_cost` overhead each; its skipped events, under 4 MiB, 98,304 slots of 40 bytes at the queue's last doubling; and its structs and reader buffers, under 1 MiB. That is under 36 MiB, so 40 MiB is reserved. Collecting the body peaks lower, under 23 MiB at its last doubling, and the body is freed when `from_reader` returns, before the keys become `Path`s. A reservation refused with nothing running, or a page whose continuation token is over 1 KiB, fails that LIST, by the reason the LIST has (`resume_list_failed`, `verify_list_failed`).
- **Transfers.** A transfer reserves its buffer from the phase budget once, at its start: `heap_cost(size)` for a single-part PUT (files up to 16 MiB), or one 16 MiB part for a multipart upload, whose parts go one at a time. It holds that reservation until it ends. A multipart transfer reserves its part-ID vector at its part count when it starts (`C_net`, 5.3), and drops its part buffer before `complete_multipart`, so the reservation covers the part list's copies and the request's XML that `object_store` builds (about 3 MB at 10,000 parts, `src/client/s3.rs`). The scheduler takes winners in key order, then archives in key order, and, under resume once it has checked the merge's position, starts a transfer when a `download_concurrency` permit is free and its reservation is granted; otherwise it waits for a running transfer, hashing pass or LIST to end. Nothing waits for a permit or a reservation while it holds one: the scheduler takes the two together or neither, and a LIST reserves only once the merge has dropped the page before. A transfer's or hashing pass's reservation refused with none running is a run failure `internal`: each is at most 16 MiB plus an archive's stream buffers, below `I`. An archive over 10,000 parts (156.25 GiB) is `failed` (`archive_too_large`) without an upload: phase 6's member list gives its size.
- **Outcomes.** Phase 7 starts by claiming, as any file is (2.8), and zero-writing from the controller's 64 KiB zero buffer (5.3) the spool files `winners/outcomes` (one byte per winner, at its index in the winners file) and `winners/archive_outcomes` (one 128-byte record per archive; at most 256, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.1) (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.1, which lists the codes and reads a missing or short file as 0, `not_written`, past its end). An outcome `pwrite` error stops the run: `spool_full` on `ENOSPC` or `EDQUOT`, otherwise `host_io`. The controller writes `present` at a winner's or an archive's index with `pwrite`, for either sink, in the poll in which it learns that resume found the bytes: from the merge, for a winner in an object store, else from a hashing pass's result. A transfer writes `written` or one of the `failed` codes (with an archive's size, MD5 and ETag) with `pwrite`, in the same poll in which its last response arrives, so every outcome the controller saw is in the files when it hands over; a directory transfer writes its own, under the commit lock (below). A byte left 0 is `not_written`, a transfer a stop left in flight among them. Each index has one writer, and every write happens before the hand-over, so the finisher never reads a record in the middle of its write. The zero-writes stay on the controller for simplicity, not because the finisher needs them: `BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.1 reads a missing or short file as 0, `not_written`. Nothing else holds per-key state.
- **Verification** (object store). After the last transfer the controller LISTs the output prefix again and merges it with the winners file and the archives, as resume does. Every key marked `written` or `present` must be listed with the ETag this attempt expects, or its outcome becomes `mismatch` (status `failed`, reason `publish_mismatch`). A key whose ETag is not an MD5 (`etag_not_md5`) is compared by size, and the head counts it in `verified_by_size`, a weaker guarantee (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2). A LIST that fails or comes out of order changes no outcome, and the head says `verified: false` with the warning `verify_list_failed`; one a stop interrupts says `verified: false`, with no warning. Every try of this attempt's PUT of a key carries the same bytes, so only another writer can cause a mismatch: an earlier attempt's late request, or a writer outside the run. What verification and the caller's duties guarantee together is `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2.
- **The directory sink** holds the root's lock (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2) for the process's life, writes each file as a temp file named `.unityrs-tmp-<run_id>-<n>` beside its destination (`<n>` a fixed `0` for the head and each row file, a winner's index in the winners file, and the winners' count plus an archive's index), opens paths beneath the root and within its mount (E62), and runs `syncfs` after the row files and before the final head (2.11), on the root lock's descriptor, held since preflight: one opened later would miss a writeback error another process's `syncfs` already saw. Each transfer starts its file's writeback with `sync_file_range(SYNC_FILE_RANGE_WRITE)` after its write, and after every 64 MiB of a streamed archive, so that `syncfs` waits mostly on I/O already under way. Preflight refuses it on a Linux kernel older than 5.8, read from `uname`'s release (`kernel_too_old`), whose `syncfs` reports no writeback error. It refuses a root that folds letter case or Unicode normalization (`sink_root_unusable`, detail `case_insensitive` or `normalization_insensitive`), by `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2's probe of two pairs of names in the root and in the run's own metadata directory, made after the head's fence and removed before the `running` head: keys are compared as bytes, and folding them would make a run's outputs depend on its sink. A directory below the root with a case folding of its own is unsupported, unprobed. A transfer's last step is its **commit**, made under the sink's **commit lock**: read the stop flag, and when it is clear rename the temp file over its destination and `pwrite` the outcome. At its hand-over the controller takes the commit lock, then sends the one-shot to the finisher (2.11), and keeps the lock, parked. So every rename has its outcome in the files the finisher reads, or never happens: the directory sink's commit boundary. The exception is an outcome `pwrite` that fails after its rename: the run stops (`spool_full` or `host_io`, above), and the key reads `not_written` though it is present, which `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.4's "`not_written` for every other" covers. No key segment may start with `.unityrs-tmp-` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 2.3), so a leftover temp file is never taken for an output, and the caller may delete it (E62).
- **The zip format** (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 8) streams each archive from its member list, with its central directory spooled while the members stream. Its stream's fixed buffers (the member list's, a member's and the central directory's reads, and that directory's writes) count in its transfer's reservation, or its hashing pass's. In a store, each central-directory write buffer is written by a blocking task under the archive's permit, a per-permit chunk write, awaited under the stop (2.11, *Threads*), so a throttled write holds no hand-over; it is awaited before its permit takes another try, so the permit keeps at most one blocking task outstanding, a stale lookup included: a stale lookup keeps the permit's lookup token, and the permit's next try waits for that token (W17). The order holds both ways because a part is assembled from the stream into its buffer before its first try, outside any try's timer, and each try sends that filled buffer (`object_store`'s `PutPayload`): no chunk write runs inside a try's body reads, so a try's timeout never leaves one behind it. A central directory is deleted, and its claim returned, when its transfer, or resume's hashing pass, ends.
- **The object store** follows `extract.md` E37. The engine makes every try itself: each client's `RetryConfig` has `max_retries` 0, so each `object_store` call is one HTTP request, and the transfer retries a request up to the sink's `attempts` with `http`'s backoff (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4), reading the stop flag before each try.
  - **The connector.** `object_store`'s errors cannot classify a try: its `RetryError`, which holds the status, is in a private module (`src/client/mod.rs`). So every client is built with the engine's own `HttpConnector` (`AmazonS3Builder::with_http_connector`), whose `HttpClient` wraps the HTTP service (2.5) as `object_store`'s `HttpService` (`src/client/http/connection.rs`), one connector per runtime (the controller's store clients share one, the finisher's has its own, 2.11), and records, in a task-local slot the try sets around its call, `connected` once the connection is up (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4), the request's status, its `Retry-After` and the S3 error code (`<Code>`) a body names, at any status. A body of any status but a 2xx the connector reads itself, at most 64 KiB of it, into one buffer allocated once, and hands `object_store` a response whose body is that buffer, as one chunk, ended there: a longer body is cut, never failed, and the try is classified by its status (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.6). With `max_retries` 0, `object_store` reads a 4xx's body whole and never reads a 3xx's or a 5xx's (`src/client/retry.rs`). It caps every 2xx body `object_store` reads (`src/aws/client.rs`), passing on no byte past the cap: a LIST's at 7 MiB (above), a head GET's at 1 MiB + 1 byte, and any other (a create's, a complete's) at 64 KiB. Each capped body reports a size hint clamped to its cap: `collect_bytes` sizes its buffer from the hint (`src/util.rs`), which is the response's `Content-Length` (`src/client/http/body.rs`), so no server's `Content-Length` sizes an allocation. Past its cap, any but the head's fails the try as a 2xx the call returns as an error (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.6). A head GET, a read-back's included, is read through `GetResult::into_stream` into a buffer of the engine's own of 1 MiB + 1 byte, never through `GetResult::bytes`, which sizes its buffer from the response's range (`src/lib.rs`): a head that fills it is unreadable (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2), and the rest is never read. A resume or verification LIST's body also passes, chunk by chunk, a tag scan of the connector's own, whose state is a few counters, a quote flag and a fixed 16-byte name buffer, so it allocates nothing; the bucket proof's body (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2) is read under the LIST cap and dropped, so `object_store`'s parse sees none of it and the try's recorded 2xx is the proof. A page fails its try, as a 2xx the call returns as an error, once it holds more than `max_keys` `<Contents>`, more than 1,000 `<CommonPrefixes>`, more than 16,064 start tags (16 per entry and 64 more), more than 64 start tags after the first `<Contents>` outside a `<Contents>` or `<CommonPrefixes>`, nesting deeper than 8, or a tag, comment, CDATA section, processing instruction or text run over 6 KiB (a run is the character data between two tags, references and CDATA included, which comments and processing instructions do not end, as quick-xml merges it). An empty element `<x/>` counts as a start tag and adds no depth; a `<!…>` declaration or a `<?…?>` instruction is markup under the 6 KiB limit; and the scan tracks quotes, since a quoted attribute value may hold `>`. Each try is classified by `BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.6's table from that record and the call's result.
  - **The store's strings.** An `ETag` the engine keeps (a part's, a head write's or read's, a completed upload's, or a listed archive's under resume) over 100 bytes fails its try, the LIST's for a listed one, as a 2xx the call returns as an error (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.6). An upload id over 1 KiB fails its transfer (`publish_failed`), unjournaled, after one abort by that id. A LIST's continuation token over 1 KiB fails its LIST (above).
  - **Its requests** go through the HTTP service (2.5), so `object_store` applies none of the builder's client options (only its own connectors call `ClientOptions::client`, `src/client/mod.rs`).
