For a store client the service sets, beyond 2.5's, exactly: one `timeout_ms` timer per try, in the engine's try wrapper, over the `object_store` call and every body read after it (a create's, a complete's, a LIST page's, a head GET's stream, a read-back's), which drops the try's future when it fires, the try then taking the row the slot gives (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.6): its status's, once one is recorded, else the first or the second by `connected`; no redirect, so every 3xx reaches 1.6's table; and `https` alone unless the endpoint is `http`. So a client's connections are its requests in flight (2.5; `C_net`, 5.3).
  - **`unsettled_tries`** (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3), on which the caller's no-wait rule rests, is an atomic each try that `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.3 counts (the finisher's row-file tries among them) increments at its start and decrements when 1.6's table settles it, so a try a stop froze stays counted; the final head reads it.
  - **The clients.** `MultipartStore::create_multipart` takes no options (`src/aws/mod.rs`), so a multipart upload's Content-Type is its client's default. The controller has one store client per multipart Content-Type, `image/png`, `application/json` and `application/zip`, each built at preflight with `with_default_content_type`; single PUTs set theirs per request. They share one connector.
  - Each multipart upload id is appended to the journal after its create and before its first part, and removed only after a successful complete or abort (E64; `BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.1). When the append fails, a run failure (`host_io` or `spool_full`, 2.7), the transfer aborts the upload once by its id, which is not a committing try (2.11), and ends. Only the controller's transfers use multipart: the finisher writes single PUTs (2.11).

**Publication and outcomes ledger (B7b).** The completed handoff stays live
while the two outcomes files are zero-written. Publication adds no payload
spool and manifest assembly adds no second row spool: 16 MiB chunks go directly
to sibling sink temps. Sink bytes remain 5.6's separate filesystem provision.
Numeric payload access is `publish::payload::Payloads::open(input, plan_index)`;
it opens immutable committed bytes, independently of record readers and loose
destinations, and is also the archive generator's source seam. The committed
failure adapter uses the controller's confirmed commit plus its received attempt
state or selected pre-dispatch failure. It probes no payload path and allocates
no post-extraction failure table.

Use B6c's `J`, `N`, `M`, `b`, `S` and encoded-key `A` above. After the display
input is retired, retain the winners, output/collision rows, member rows, included
lists and archive row/metadata streams. Add `round_up(W, b)` winner-outcome bytes,
where `W <= N`, and `round_up(128L, b)` for `L` declared archives. Archive outcomes
fit the existing archive/list inequality, with no third `A`:

`e + r + 192 + 67 + 16 + b - 1 + 128 <= 2 round_up(e + 512, b) + e`.

The extra `e` is covered by counted member work for nonempty archives. Pooled
file rounding adds at most one further block. A conservative retained envelope
for `N >= 1`, including both outcome files, is

`3J + 65N + 3M + 8b + 128 + 2A <= 6S + 2A`.

The counted records-flush and per-output floors used in B6c cover its fixed
terms; the independent arithmetic test checks singleton, large-count, member
work and unusual-block edges. When `N = 0`, only pooled archive rows, metadata
and outcomes remain, bounded by `2A + 3b + 16`; one committed summary gives
`6S >= 6b`, covering the remainder. Zero-length outcomes claim zero bytes.
Positional rewrites never add another extent claim. Failure and drop retain
claims; no new cleanup promise is made.

| publication boundary | retained spool files / additional lifetime |
|---|---|
| entry and zeroing | complete handoff plus both full rounded outcome reservations; partial zeroing retains claims |
| resume hashing | same retained set; reads only, no new spool stream |
| loose transfer | same retained set; sink sibling temps use the separate sink-filesystem provision |
| authorized commit | rename and positional status write under one gate; no second extent claim |
| manifest assembly | handoff and outcomes remain; complete-line chunks stream to sink temps, with no overlaid spool copy |

The complete empty-archive gate now retains outcomes too, over all six encoded
key families and five block sizes. At `b = 512`, the 256 quote-key case claims
**852,480** additional bytes against **1,335,296** (`6S + 2A`). The nonempty
handoff/outcomes gate covers blocks 512, 3,072, 4,096, 5,003 and 65,536, with
zero, singleton and multiple winners.

B7c's concurrent directory publisher runs on the existing blocking pool.
A single charged streaming cursor assigns each winner its canonical outcome
index, then releases its queue lock before any hashing or transfer. At most
`min(download_concurrency, floor(I / 18,878,464), winners)` drives run together.
Each retains one charged 16 MiB buffer across winners, lending it to resume
hashing before a miss immediately reuses it for transfer. The shared dispatcher charges 512 KiB of fixed overhead; its reader and
bounded task/frame buffers are charged separately, together fitting the
second MiB of even a single drive envelope;
there is no per-winner queue or retained path inventory. The serial library
seam remains available; `Busy` never attempts a failing reservation.
Pool threads audit their own allocator refusals; the shared account's zero
check occurs only after normal drain, never while stopped tasks retain claims.
The caller freezes the allocation-free commit gate before inspecting outcomes.
Authorized rename and outcome writes have no intervening stop check. A rename
error, including perform-then-fail, leaves the outcome unknown. Archive
execution remains refused by capability validation.

### 2.10 Why D holds

Concurrent publication reads the same sealed winner order and assigns outcome
indices before dispatch. Completion order changes only progress and observed
peaks. The normal finisher joins those indices back into ordered row streams;
thread count, buffer loans and resume hits do not choose winners or row order.

**The argument.** An attempt is a program run on one thread. Everything it reads is fixed by D's inputs:
- its staged bytes and its view's staged bytes, read whole from read-only spool files that nothing writes during the run (2.5);
- the spec: its limits, `M` and `D`, its rules, its entries for the view's bundles;
- the registration order, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.3, which depends only on the ids in the view;
- the peek table's outcomes for its view (2.6 step 4), each a function of one bundle's staged bytes, `M` and the spec, written once by a peek on a budget and a meter of its own before any attempt is dispatched, and never after;
- which failure an input names before dispatch (2.3, rule 1), a function of its own view's staging outcomes and, when it staged whole, its view's peek outcomes, never of whether another view had a member peeked: a view that did not stage whole is committed at staging's end, before any peek.

Everything it writes to is its own: its budget, its meters, its private Environment's registry, LRU, cells and work counts. No other thread books with them (2.2). The core keeps no state across threads but the budget and the provider chain, and its thread-locals carry nothing from one call to the next (A12); version 1's provider chain is empty. So the attempt computes the same records in every run. Absent a stop, whether it runs at all depends only on its view's staging and, when that staged whole, its view's peeks (D's second limit for the first; the second are functions of the bytes, above); a stop, another attempt's or a peek's run failure among them, leaves it `not_run`.

Archive determinism extends this argument to the immutable included lists and payloads: the same layout and explicit stored headers feed one pull stream for hashing or transfer. Payload CRC/MD5 is recomputed in that read and checked against the committed metadata. No loose publication result, scratch file ID, read chunk size or scheduling order enters archive bytes. Stream summaries alone do not acknowledge public success.

**Against earlier reviews' counterexamples.**

| counterexample | why a sealed attempt is immune |
|---|---|
| a cold decode's transient peaks above a warm read's cached run, so a read succeeds only after another input cached the run | no attempt reads a run another attempt cached. Every read of the attempt meets only runs its own earlier reads cached, in program order |
| a growth or a shrink silently cut back by a pool another attempt filled, which moves a later refusal of the input's own meter | the attempt's budget is filled only by its own charges |
| a dependency's growth ladder settled lower under contention, so a read site fits warm and not cold | as above, for the Dependency side |
| overlapping entry spans: which runs are cached, and their cost, depend on which reader came first | the attempt's private opens see only its own reads |
| a flagged input's private registry takes room a shared one does not | every attempt registers exactly its view, in one order, so its registry's charges are a function of its view |
| a registration refused by a pool whose fill depends on every other bundle registered | registration happens inside the attempt, on its own budget |
| a failed load that published some of its CABs before it failed | under `batch()`'s `Lazy` a failed `load_dependency` publishes none (CR1, landed: core 4.1, *a failed dependency load publishes nothing*); under `Eager` it keeps what it published. Either way a failed load of a view member also fails X, and no read follows it |
| a dependency's work allowance used up by other inputs' reads | each attempt opens its own copy, with its own allowance |
| a same-named CAB in a bundle outside the view, or its failure | the private registry holds only the view |
| a lookup that reaches a dropped slot of a bundle outside the view | as above |
| a nested container registered when first reached, by whichever attempt got there | only this attempt reaches its private Environment's containers, in program order |
| a source file rewritten in place between two attempts' reads, its size and modification time kept (design round 2) | every attempt reads the staged copy (2.5) |
| another input's declaration of a discovered name adding bundles to X's view (design round 2) | there is no discovery; a bundle has one declaration, and only declarations reached from X enter its view (2.3) |
| an allocator refusal that depends on the process's total memory (design round 2) | the counting allocator sees every refusal of a Rust allocation, absorbed or not, and stops the run (2.7); liblzma's two unseen paths are D's third limit until CR2 (A13) |
| a download that times out only beside others (design round 2) | staging has no whole-request timeout, only an idle timeout and a rate floor (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.4), so a slow neighbour costs time; a slow lookup holds only its own permit's lookup token (2.5), so a neighbour's slow DNS costs time too, not a failure; a failure that remains is D's second limit |

**Tests.**
- `an_inputs_records_do_not_depend_on_what_runs_beside_it`. The engine scene runs at `threads` 1, 2 and 8; with the inputs in 20 seeded orders; with `I` at the smallest valid value and at 4 times it; with an unrelated dependency that fails to download; with a view whose two members fail at staging (`integrity_mismatch` and `limit(input_bytes, ..)`); with a bundle added that holds a same-named copy of a CAB in another input's dependency. Every input's records must equal those of a run whose spec holds only that input and the declarations of its view, timings and counters aside.
- `round_one_counterexamples_change_no_record`: one scene per row of the table above, each compared with its solo run.
- `an_attempt_books_with_no_budget_but_its_own`: a test budget records the thread of each reservation; every reservation of an attempt comes from its worker, while its attempt runs.
- `a_view_member_that_fails_to_load_fails_the_input_and_nothing_reads_after_it` (it counts the attempt's Environment calls after the failure: under `Lazy` a read there would answer as before it, so the answers cannot show one).
- `a_source_rewritten_during_the_run_changes_no_record`: a local dependency rewritten in place, size and modification time kept, after staging.

### 2.11 Lifetime, stops and the finish

Transport and staging retain their owners outside the controller's unwind/drive scope. An ordinary try failure drops its response; process stop freezes the future set, sockets, file owners and outstanding operations for B9's exit/reset path. Cancellation wakes the controller; canonical `Control` remains the stop truth. Staging claim, write and pool failures keep `Culprit::None`. Download details are a fixed typed value mapped to both failure records and bundle rows, with retained structure growth measured in phase 1. Diagnostics never render URL-bearing client errors, response headers or newly received redirect values; no public manifest field or closed reason is added.


**Implementation ownership.** B7 supplies reusable local host checks, spool
startup, directory preflight and ordinary execution spawning as library
components. B9 owns the final ordering below with signal setup, panic and
stderr services, the runtimes and hard exit, and may reorder or wrap those
components. B7's temporary normal-run subset is stated in `BATCH_SPEC.md`
section 10; it does not implement this whole lifetime sequence.

B9a supplies the process stop word, checked timing, stable wake registrations,
opaque resettable sockets, signals, lifecycle and bounded diagnostics as library
primitives. Lifetime CLI fields remain refused until B9b owns the complete
startup, hand-over and stopped finisher. `Control` maps its existing run-failure
vocabulary to the separate process `Stop`; lifetime reasons never enter attempt
classification. The local host already prepares channels and idle workers
before spool startup and binds their context afterward. The two-runtime
preparation is independent; B9b replaces the local host's pool when adopting
it, rather than running that extra pool alongside both prescribed pools.

The single checked deadline conversion consumes the validated `DateTime` and
one wall/boot sample pair. It uses signed Gregorian arithmetic (years 0000
through 9999), applies the offset, and maps accepted second 60 to the following
POSIX second. Validation retains the first nine fractional digits; boot
deadlines are rounded toward the past to milliseconds, less than 1 ms early.
Past or reached cutoffs refuse startup; unrepresentable instants fail checked
conversion, never wrap into an immediate stop. Production uses BOOTTIME with
no `Instant` fallback. Wall samples, boot samples and timer-arm failure are
independent test seams.

The temporary normal host prewarms the blocking pool and spawns idle workers
before spool cleanup, binds their context after startup, and acknowledges the
running head before staging. After normal publication drain and the final stop
check it freezes the commit gate, posts finish progress, writes all eight row
families, calls `syncfs` on the retained root-lock descriptor, and writes the
final head by temp `fsync`, rename and head-directory `fsync`. A row failure
nulls only that family and zeros its counts; a sync failure nulls all families.
Received figures remain independent. Finisher parse/allocation failures affect
the code and progress reason without changing the frozen normal stop snapshot;
only a final `syncfs` failure sets its head's `stop_reason` to `host_io`.
Final-head failure preserves the last
acknowledged status in progress, even if rename physically succeeded. Expected
skips alone keep exit 0; unused failed dependencies contribute 4. The result
is stored before the priority exit line, whose acknowledgment waits at most
one second. Non-normal hand-over retains lock leases in every outstanding pool phase
(staging, failure records, selection and publication) and bypasses this path.

**Threads.** The main thread runs, in order:
1. the panic hook (*Panics* below), the test hooks, each read from its variable (*Test hooks* below), and, under `test-hooks` with a `count` action armed, CR8's load-name and span-read tables (`unityrs_core::testing::reserve_span_reads`, 2.6 step 4), the stderr writer's own description (*The standard descriptors* below), under `test-hooks` with `trace` set the lifecycle trace FIFO (`<trace>.lifecycle`, the `trace` row), opened `O_WRONLY | O_NONBLOCK | O_CLOEXEC | O_NOCTTY`, `ENXIO` (no reader, or not openable as a FIFO) being 69 `startup_failed` with the detail `open <ERRNO name>`, and a path that an `fstat` after the open finds is not a FIFO 69 `startup_failed` with the detail `fstat` alone (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.3), the five signals step 4 handles blocked (`pthread_sigmask`), so one sent before step 4 waits for its handler, SIGTTIN and SIGTTOU set to `SIG_IGN`, so the engine's own reads and writes never stop the process (SIGTSTP still does, but for init, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.1), and SIGXFSZ too, so a write past `RLIMIT_FSIZE` fails with `EFBIG`, a `host_io` (2.7), and kills nothing;
2. phase 1, validation (2.1), of `JOB.json`, opened `O_RDONLY | O_NONBLOCK | O_NOCTTY` (the flags from `libc`), so a FIFO cannot hold the open and a terminal device does not become the controlling terminal, and read whole into a buffer sized by its `fstat` + 1, which grows only if the file grew while it was read, and never past 64 MiB + 1 byte (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 1). The parse borrows its strings from that text, and validation's end copies the strings the spec keeps into a table of their own, so the text is dropped when validation ends. Every graph walk is iterative, the `deps` closure among them, and every structure derived from the spec is built here, the dependency graph, the masker's secret list, the controller's `uncommitted` set (2.4) and the bundles' peek words (2.6 step 4) among them, so `C_spec` measures it as the heap left after the parse, counted from step 2's start, as phase 1's peak `p` is (5.3), and step 1's test-only tables fall outside both. The list's spec part is built here and its header part at step 6, sharing the values' `Bytes`, neither changed after step 6; its credentials part is a set-once slot (`OnceLock`) the controller fills. So the stderr writer takes no lock. A refused spec's `exit` line and stderr line hold only its rule and pointer (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 10), never the parser's message;
3. E57's allocator;
4. the self-pipe, the stop word, the lifecycle thread's `timerfd`, both tokio runtimes (the controller's and the finisher's, each moved to its thread at its spawn), the controller runtime's cancellation token, two **wake eventfds**, the controller's and the finisher's (`EFD_NONBLOCK | EFD_CLOEXEC`, *The lifecycle thread* below), each registered as a tokio `AsyncFd` on the runtime that waits on it, under that runtime's `enter()`, a failed registration exiting 69 (`startup_failed`), the end channel (std's `sync_channel(threads)`, which the controller reads only by `try_recv`, 2.4) and the controller's end eventfd (`EFD_NONBLOCK | EFD_CLOEXEC`), the stdin watch's two buffers, its `PIPE_BUF` line buffer and its 4 KiB stdin read buffer, allocated here on the main thread and moved into the stdin watch's thread at its spawn, so a refusal is a startup failure here and never on the thread that owns `T_hard`, and the one rustls `ClientConfig`, in an `Arc`, that the HTTP service (2.5) hands tokio-rustls for every connection. It has the `ring` provider; the roots `webpki-roots` compiles in, so no host CA store is read (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.2); `alpn_protocols` `["http/1.1"]`; and `resumption` `Resumption::disabled()`, since rustls's default keeps a session cache of 256 server names of up to 8 tickets each, which would grow with the hosts a run meets. B10a declares `tokio` (`rt`, `net`, `time`, `sync`), `hyper` (`client`, `http1`, and `server`, for the typed `Error::is_parse_too_large` of 2.5's try classes: it adds only `httpdate` and changes no client path), `hyper-util` (`tokio`, for `TokioIo`), `url`, `webpki-roots`, `rustls` (`ring`, `std`, `tls12`) and `tokio-rustls` (`ring`, `tls12`), the last two with `default-features = false`, since tokio-rustls's default is aws-lc's (`extract.md` X15 bans a second rustls, OpenSSL, native-tls and aws-lc, none of which the others bring: `object_store`'s `cloud` feature turns on `reqwest`'s `rustls-tls-native-roots` with its default features off, which puts rustls, tokio-rustls and hyper-rustls on `ring`, theirs off too). `http-body-util` is a dev-dependency for parser fixtures. Controller/store integration adds tokio `macros` for `select!`, `tokio-util` (`CancellationToken`), `futures-util` (`FuturesUnordered`), and `async-trait` for `object_store`'s `HttpService` when their production call sites land. Under `stop_on_stdin_eof`, step 4 also checks fd 0 (*The standard descriptors* below). It records whether `getpid()` is 1: the engine is then its PID namespace's init, as a container's entrypoint is (*Every exit* below). Then, before any thread is spawned, it installs the handlers of SIGTERM, SIGINT, SIGHUP and `SIGRTMIN` with `SA_RESTART`, and SIGABRT's (*Every exit* below), reading each old action first: SIGTERM's, the parent-death one (`SIGRTMIN`) and SIGABRT's always, and SIGINT's and SIGHUP's only where the old action is not `SIG_IGN`, an inherited ignore being kept, as shells keep it for a background job; then it unblocks the five (`pthread_sigmask`), whatever mask it inherited, so a signal held pending meets its handler. Every write and `poll` loop retries `EINTR` all the same. Any other signal `BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.1 names, and one of the five before step 1, takes its default action, as 9.1 says, init's case included;
5. `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG` and E46's `getppid` check (*Parent death* below). A failure in step 1 or steps 3 to 5 exits 69 (`startup_failed`, its detail as `BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.3 says), the main thread writing the `exit` line (a step-1 failure's as section 10 says); so does a store client whose build fails in preflight (2.9), through the controller's hand-over;
6. the deadline's conversion and `BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.1's check (`deadline_too_close`); each header variable read once (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.2, its `header_env_*` reasons), the masker's list gaining the values; and 2.7's host checks, in its order: the address-space and data limits, the overcommit mode, the map count, the host's memory, the open-files and file-size limits, and the thread limits. A refusal exits 69, its lines the main thread's writer thread's with a deadline and its own without one (*The main thread's `exit` line* below);
7. the spawns: the progress thread; then both runtimes' blocking pools, each spawned whole, so no thread is spawned after this step: the main thread spawns `download_concurrency + 1` blocking tasks on each runtime, each of which, under the start count's mutex, adds one to the count unless it is closed, and then waits, as the main thread does, until the count reaches `2 x download_concurrency + 2` or 1 s after the first spawn, whichever is first (a timed `Condvar` wait, W20), and ends, its thread kept in the pool by tokio's `thread_keep_alive(Duration::MAX)`, since the controller's pool sits idle through phase 5; when its wait ends, the main thread closes the count under its mutex, so a task that starts later adds nothing. tokio queues a task whose thread the OS refuses with `EAGAIN` while the pool has a thread, and panics at any other refusal, or at `EAGAIN` with no thread (tokio 1.54.1, `src/runtime/blocking/pool.rs`), so the count, not the spawn, shows a queued task's shortfall. Then the finisher, the lifecycle thread and the workers, and the controller last, given the pre-made token. A failed spawn, a panic from `spawn_blocking` (caught around that call, whichever refusal caused it, not as a main-thread panic), or a start count short of `2 x download_concurrency + 2` when the wait ends exits 69 (`thread_spawn_failed`), with nothing written to the spool, the sink or a head: once the progress thread runs, the main thread hands the `exit` line to its mailbox, waits at most 1 s for its report, as the finisher's step 4 does, and calls `exit_now(69)` without writing;
8. the controller's join, which never returns (W18): if it does, the main thread requests the stop `internal` and parks.

**The main thread's `exit` line.** Before it writes or hands over a 69, the main thread stores 69 in the final code (*One stop path* below), so a second signal exits 69. It writes a line itself only while the progress thread does not exist: phase 1's 78, the 69s of steps 1 and 3 to 6, a failed spawn of the progress thread, and `--check`'s line. That line has `seq` 0 and is one write, under `PIPE_BUF` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 10). **Without a deadline** the main thread writes its lines itself. To a pipe, a FIFO or a terminal it writes the `exit` line through a description of its own, stdout opened again as `/proc/self/fd/1` with `O_WRONLY | O_NONBLOCK | O_CLOEXEC | O_NOCTTY`, as fd 2's is (*The standard descriptors* below), and it writes that description, trying again every 1 ms while it is full; a socket's line goes through the writer thread (B2's form of a `poll` for `POLLOUT` and a `send(..., MSG_DONTWAIT)`: the same bound, in safe code). The `exit` line and the stderr line share one budget of 1 s, counted from the first write and checked before every try, a partial write's and an interrupted one's included, and a line, or its rest, is dropped when it is spent. When the reopen fails (`EACCES`, as for a pipe another uid created, after a change of uid), the line goes through the writer thread instead, which the main thread waits on for what is left of the budget, as the deadline path does. So another writer of the same pipe holds the two lines, and the signals that wait behind them, for at most that second. To a regular file or a block device it writes as it is. That is: a start that fails before phase 1 accepts a spec (step 1's 69s), a spec phase 1 accepts with `deadline: null`, a 78, since a spec phase 1 refuses has no deadline even when its `JOB.json` names one, and `--check`, which sets no deadline either way and does no work. **With a deadline**, which phase 1 accepted from the spec (the 69s of steps 3 to 6 and a failed spawn of the progress thread), every early line goes through a short-lived **writer thread**, whatever the descriptors' types: a `poll` that finds room bounds nothing, since another writer of the same pipe can fill it before the write, and writeback can hold a regular file's write for minutes (A11). The writer writes the `exit` line on stdout first, then the stderr line. It is the only other thread then (2 MiB of stack), so 2.7's thread checks, which count step 7's spawns, cover it. The main thread waits for it, on a channel, at most `min(1 s, the time left to the deadline - 500 ms)`, the time left read by the wall clock before step 6's conversion and by `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` after it; then it calls `exit_now` with the status, whether the writes returned or not. When that wait is not positive (a deadline less than 500 ms away, or past), it does not wait, and no line is promised: the exit status is the record. A panic on the writer thread ends the wait, its channel's sender dropped, and `exit_now` follows with the status (*Panics* below). The throttle's sleep is killable, so `_exit` ends a write of the engine's held there (A11), though not one waiting on another process's inode lock (*What cannot be bounded* below). When the writer's spawn fails, both lines are dropped. The exit status stays authoritative. **Test:** in `extract.md` X8, a 69 `deadline_too_close` (a deadline inside the grace) and a host check's 69, each with a regular-file stdout whose write the `point` hook holds in the writer thread, each exiting 69 by the deadline, its `exit` line missing; a host check's 69 with a pipe stdout that another writer fills after the engine could have polled it, its reader stopped, exiting 69 by the deadline; a 69 `deadline_too_close` with a full pipe and the deadline 0.6 s away, exiting by the deadline with no line promised (step 6 checks the deadline first, and the grace is at least 10 s); `--check` with a past deadline and a pipe stdout, its `memory_bound` line written; a 78 and a failure before validation, each with a pipe stdout another writer keeps full, their line dropped when the 1 s budget is spent (B2's tests add a full pipe the process cannot reopen, a full socket, and one full pipe as stdout and stderr, which takes one budget, not two); and a 78 with a pipe stdout the process cannot reopen (`fchmod`ed to 0000), its line written through the writer thread within 1 s.

Every park in this plan is `loop { park() }`. Every spawned thread has an explicit stack: a worker's is 2.4's; the controller, lifecycle, progress and finisher threads, and the main thread's writer thread (*The main thread's `exit` line* above), take 2 MiB each (`std::thread::Builder::stack_size`); both runtimes' blocking pools 2 MiB a thread (tokio's `thread_stack_size`). The main thread's is counted at 2 MiB plus the environment block, whose size is known at start; its touched depth is bounded by validation (serde_json's 128-level recursion limit) and the spawns (5.3). So `RUST_MIN_STACK` changes nothing. Every one runs its body inside `catch_unwind` (*Panics* below). A thread that drives a tokio runtime (the controller, the finisher) holds it outside that closure, which borrows it, and never drops it: after a panic the thread parks or calls `exit_now`, so no runtime's drop waits on its blocking pool's tasks. Staging and transfers are futures the controller polls inside its one `block_on` future (a `FuturesUnordered`), on a current-thread runtime that only it drives, never `tokio::spawn`ed; each is created only when it takes its permit, so at most `download_concurrency` exist, beside one LIST. No async task calls the core. Each runtime's blocking pool is `download_concurrency + 1` threads, all spawned at step 7: one for each of its `download_concurrency` permits (the controller's for stages, transfers and `BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.2's aborts; the finisher's for its step 2's aborts), under which at most one blocking task is outstanding (a lookup, holding the permit's lookup token, 2.5, a copy, a chunk write, a store archive's central-directory write buffer among them, 2.9, a pass or a directory transfer), and one for its **spare permit**, which the requests that hold no other take one at a time: a LIST, the bucket proof's among them, a head read, write or read-back, a row file (the finisher's step 3 takes any of its permits, below), and the controller's **moved writes**, each awaited in `block_on` under the stop (*What cannot be bounded* below): every failure record's file, which the controller then renames itself (2.4), and every phase-6 write, its sort runs, merges, row files, winners file, decision file, member lists and `P`, each task reading the stop flag before each write buffer of at most 64 MiB and returning unfinished when it is set (2.9). So a blocking task queues only behind a thread that has finished its task, which runs it next (tokio's `src/runtime/blocking/pool.rs`). `TOKIO_UNSTABLE_SHARDED_BLOCKING_QUEUE`, which the caller's environment may set, selects a sharded queue with the same claim-or-spawn rule (`src/runtime/builder.rs`, `src/runtime/blocking/sharded.rs`), so it changes nothing this relies on. Any `JoinError` of a blocking-pool task, a panic's or a cancellation's, is a stop `internal`: the controller requests it, hands over (2.4), and only then writes one stderr line naming the task's kind (a local copy, a stage's write, a hashing pass, a directory transfer, a store archive's central-directory write, a failure record's write, a phase-6 task), with a panic's payload cut as the panic hook cuts it (*Panics* below). Every connection is one request's future's, and ends with it (2.5), so the connections are the requests in flight, however many hosts the URLs name.

**Test hooks.** Every seam the gate uses is a field of one `TestHooks` value, compiled only under the cargo feature `test-hooks`, which CI's features and scale jobs pass explicitly (`--features test-hooks`) and the release build lacks: a hook-dependent integration test declares `required-features = ["test-hooks"]` in its `[[test]]` entry, so a plain `cargo test --workspace` builds no hooks, and batch takes no dev-dependency on itself; the testkit has no dependency on batch (`extract.md` X15). The binary reads each from its variable at step 1; a test that drives the engine through its library API sets the field itself. Unset, a hook does nothing.

| hook | variable | what it does |
|---|---|---|
| `extra_roots` | `UNITYRS_TEST_EXTRA_ROOTS` | the path of a PEM file whose certificates join the rustls config's `RootCertStore` |
| `resolve` | `UNITYRS_TEST_RESOLVE` | a comma-separated list of `host=addr:port`, each answering that host's lookup in the HTTP service (2.5) in place of `getaddrinfo`, for staging and the store clients alike; `host=addr:port+<ms>` answers after that delay, and `host=addr:port+hold:<path>` once the test creates `<path>`, after appending `resolve <host> held` to the `trace` log, each blocking task held on its thread as a slow `getaddrinfo` is |
| `handover` | `UNITYRS_TEST_HANDOVER` | a path to which the finisher writes, once it has taken the reason from the hand-over (2.4), the ids of the inputs it will write `stopped` and the culprit's, with its kind, `input` or `bundle` |
| `trace` | `UNITYRS_TEST_TRACE` | a path to which the controller appends one line per dispatch (a peek's `peek <bundle id>`, 2.4), per input staging's end or the peek pass's end marks failed (2.4) and per rename into `done/` as the controller makes it, right after the rename and before any `point` (2.4's commit order), a `rename failed: <id> <errno>` line for a rename that fails, `<id>` the input's id, `<bundle id>` a bundle's, as a `RunFailure`'s stderr line names it (2.4), and in every trace and stderr template here, `<input>` being a position in spool paths alone (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.1), then a `handover` line at its hand-over, written before it releases the finisher, after which no rename is logged, the finisher appending `commit found at take: <id>` when its snapshot's one stat finds the hand-over slot's input committed and `commit unknown at take: <id> <errno>` when that stat fails otherwise than `ENOENT`, either line overriding any rename line for the input (2.4), each try a line at its end with its bundle, its class (2.5) and its errno, if any, each worker a line `stop <reason> <id>` once its `request_stop` returns (a peek's naming its bundle, `stop <reason> peek <bundle id>`), each runtime a line per blocking task it spawns, naming the runtime, the task's kind and its bundle, with its outstanding blocking tasks, the new one included, a task counted until its closure returns (a lookup's lowering the count before it gives its token back), the lifecycle thread a line at each of its two plain trace points, appended whenever `trace` is set, whatever `UNITYRS_TEST_POINT` holds, to the **lifecycle trace FIFO** (`<trace>.lifecycle`), a FIFO at the `trace` path with `.lifecycle` added, opened by step 1 (2.11 step 1; *The lifecycle thread* below). The test's side meets five requirements; R1-R5 bind every run that sets `trace`. **R1.** The test sees every line a run writes to the FIFO, and nothing from any other run, in the FIFO, the `trace` log or a `wait` path; a rerun (R4) starts the scene again from its initial state: a new FIFO, `trace` log, `wait` paths, spool, sink root or store prefix, `handover` path and test servers' logs. **R2.** In every run that sets `trace`, except H6's startup tests of the open, the engine's open finds a reader, and no line is dropped for a full FIFO. **R3.** Every wait in a scene (a named line, a file, a server's state) fails the test by a timeout; a run that ends without a named line fails it at once, except where X10 lets the wait end at the process's end (the peek-pass stop scene). **R4.** Every test child runs through `unityrs_testkit::child`, as CLAUDE.md requires: a child `SIGKILL`ed from outside is run again, never read as an answer. Where a test needs more from it (a running child's pid or `pidfd`, a live stderr or FIFO drain), the PR adds that to `unityrs_testkit::child` with its test, as `child.rs` asks; no scene spawns around it. **R5.** An engine the test runs under another uid (the unopenable-stdin scene and X8's uid-drop scenes, when the test is root) can open the FIFO, append to the `trace` log, and reach every path its hooks and the spec name. How the test creates, opens, drains and reaps is designed, from H1-H10, by the first test PR whose tests set `trace` (whichever lands first: X8's startup tests, X9, X10 or X11), with the testkit entry of R4 and H10, and reviewed there; later PRs use it. The checklist keeps every finding of design rounds 33 to 35 on these points: (H1) creation and mode: a new FIFO and `trace` log per run, any old one unlinked (`mkfifo` fails on an existing path), concurrent runs each with a `trace` path of its own; `mkfifo`, whose mode the umask masks, then `chmod`, or `fchmod` on the read end once opened, to 0666, or `chown` to the child's uid, every directory on the path searchable by that uid, and the `trace` log writable by that uid (R5); (H2) the read end: its flags and timing (a blocking open waits for a writer; `O_RDONLY | O_NONBLOCK | O_CLOEXEC` before the engine's start, or Linux's `O_RDWR`), and its close before the next run, so a run sees no stale line (R1, R2); (H3) the drain loop: a 0-byte read before step 1's open is not the end; a child that never opened the FIFO leaves no `POLLHUP`; the loop polls the FIFO with the child's `pidfd`, or `waitpid(WNOHANG)` at an interval, its `poll` retried on `EINTR`; a `pidfd` reports the exit, not the reap, so another reaper races it (`ECHILD`) and a `POLLHUP` spins between the two; the read to 0 bytes after the exit; and no reader joined before the reap; (H4) the `sudo … unshare --pid --fork setpriv` init scene in its three forms (CI's VM, as root, `--user -r`): the `pidfd` is that of the process the test spawns (sudo's, or unshare's), whose exit follows the engine's, and a run's timeout kills the engine by its host PID, since killing sudo passes no `SIGKILL` on and `unshare` without `--kill-child` leaves the engine holding the write end, and sudo's `use_pty` monitor sits between them; before a rerun, an old engine left running is killed; open point: sudo's `use_pty`; (H5) the parent-death scene: the reaped child is an intermediate and the engine still holds the write end, so the drain keeps polling through `EAGAIN` until the engine's `POLLHUP`; and the orphaned engine, and its outside `SIGKILL`, with the test as subreaper or the outcome stated; (H6) the startup tests that open no read end or make no FIFO: `ENOENT` leaves the path empty, the no-reader `ENXIO` makes the FIFO and opens no read end, and the `fstat` case puts a regular file there; (H7) library-API children: the reader inside the child, its drain ending with the child (the engine's `_exit`) or at once for a call that returns before step 1; how its verdict, a named-line timeout or a failed check, reaches the parent before `exit_now`'s `_exit`, past the engine's panic hook; and the watch-end run's live stderr drain, which `child::output`, collecting at the end, does not give; (H8) the run's own bound: the testkit's guard, what it kills (the engine, in H4's scene), the watch-end run under `deadline: null`, a named-line wait that fails at once when the drain ends without its line, but for R3's exception, and the drain itself bounded only by the run's own bound, never by a named-line timeout; the timeouts by the testkit's guard (`DEADLOCK_GUARD`) (R3); (H9) spawning and reaping from one long-lived thread, since `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG` fires when the spawning thread exits, which would stop the run as `parent_died`; (H10) the testkit's entry for these scenes (R4): the pid or `pidfd` it exposes, a whole run again, the scene rebuilt (R1), on an outside `SIGKILL` but never on the test's own kill, and `child::direct_runs`'s acceptance of it, with its test; before a rerun, an old engine left running is killed; and the PR rewrites `child.rs`'s module docs about a child the test kills; open point: X7's server as a service child. Its lines: `lifecycle_pipe_read reached` after it reads a self-pipe byte and before that wake's work, and `lifecycle_stop_woken reached` once its first stop wake's work is done, after the finisher's wake eventfd write (*The lifecycle thread* below; `extract.md` X10's held-stop rule and tally scene), and each `point` hold its own (below) |
| `files` | `UNITYRS_TEST_FILES` | the path of a fault plan for every file call on the spool, the sink and local sources: each call is logged, and the plan can fail it with an errno, perform it and then report an errno (a call that took effect and returned an error, such as a rename into `done/`), delay it, give `statvfs` or `st_blksize` a figure, refuse `openat2` or `STATX_MNT_ID`, or plant a mount crossing |
| `host_root` | `UNITYRS_TEST_HOST_ROOT` | a directory at which step 6 reads `/proc/self/cgroup`, `/proc/self/mountinfo`, `/proc/self/uid_map`, `/proc/sys/vm`, `/proc/meminfo` and `/sys/fs/cgroup`, and preflight reads `uname`'s release (its `release` file); a `capget` file there makes step 6's `capget` fail (`EPERM`) |
| `spawn` | `UNITYRS_TEST_SPAWN` | the thread (`progress`, `finisher`, `lifecycle`, `worker` or `controller`) whose spawn fails |
| `start_count` | `UNITYRS_TEST_START_COUNT` | how many of step 7's start tasks add nothing to the count |
| `init` | `UNITYRS_TEST_INIT` | step 4 records the engine as init whatever `getpid()` returns (*Every exit*) |
| `point` | `UNITYRS_TEST_POINT` | a comma-separated list of `<point>=<action>`; an entry naming one of the lifecycle thread's two trace points (the `trace` row) is refused when the hooks are parsed, step 1's 69 `startup_failed`, and the library API's field cannot name them, its type holding only the armable points. The points: a step of the main thread, a thread's loop, a named key's directory-sink commit, under the commit lock, before its flag read, before its rename and before its `pwrite` (2.9), a decoder, a named input's attempt and its first read (before 2.6 step 2's `read_path`), a named bundle's peek (before its open, 2.6 step 4), a named input's registration's start and end (before and after 2.6 step 4's registrations, before step 5), a phase-6 sort run, inside its pool task, so `refuse` arms that task's thread, a phase-7 transfer's buffer, a blocking-pool task, a named input's commit, between its steps 1 and 2, after the slot is set and before the stop-word load, and between its rename's `trace` line and its bit's clear (2.4's commit order), every 1,000th of the controller's renames in staging's end's and the peek pass's end's failure-record commits, between its steps 1 and 2, after the slot is set and before the stop-word load, a named input's failure-record write on the pool (after it creates `fail/<input>/` and reads the stop flag clear, before it writes), after the transfers drain and after verification, a worker between a named input's run failure and its `request_stop` (2.11), the finisher before it takes the reason and in a named row file's lookup, the stderr writer before a named thread's write, the main thread's writer thread before its write, a named input's stage before it stores its socket entry and, apart, after it loads the closing flag clear, before its `connect`, where `wait` blocks the controller's thread, and two points that take only `spin` and `wait`, which there makes only async-signal-safe calls: `exit_now` after its resets and the SIGABRT handler after its resets (*Every exit*). The actions: `panic`; `spin`; `wait:<path>`, which appends `<point> reached` to the `trace` log, then waits until the test creates `<path>`; `refuse`, which arms one refusal on the point's thread for a fallible allocation alone, disarmed at its attempt's, its peek's or its phase's end if unconsumed: the next allocation inside a fallible helper's `try_reserve_exact` (the engine's, 2.7, or the core's, CR8), behind a charged buffer on an attempt's or a peek's budget or a phase budget, gets NULL from the counting allocator and counts as a refusal, so the worker's count check or the phase's helper makes it `host_memory`, and no infallible allocation meets it; at an attempt or a peek, `leak`, a charge to its `Input` budget left at its end, which the leak check finds (2.2); and, at a named input's registration's start and end, `count`, which appends to the `trace` log CR8's counts of span reads of each row, read through `unityrs_core::testing::span_reads`, keyed by the load id, mapped through the load-name table to the engine's bundle id, and the row's index path (`Rank::path` for a registered row), summed over load ids, skipping the `bare` key, with the `whole` key, any `unmapped` count and the tables' overflow flag, so the two lines' difference is step 4's own reads (2.6 step 4's test) |
| `zip_offset` | the library API | the offset at which an archive's first member starts, so a test reaches zip64's forms (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 8) |
| `skip_host_checks` | the library API | step 6's host checks skipped, for `extract.md` X13 |
| `tally` | the library API | the testkit's `Tally`, which the engine's counting allocator feeds, every engine thread registered with it, the runtimes' pool threads through tokio's `on_thread_start`, counting each thread's bytes and its number of allocations through a const-initialised thread-local index, set when the thread registers, for `extract.md` X12, and X10's count of the lifecycle thread's allocations after its start, which that thread marks before its first `poll` (A13); a test thread reads the tally while the `exit_now` point holds, in a child that drives the engine through its library API, as X12 does: after a stop, once the lifecycle trace FIFO (`<trace>.lifecycle`) shows `lifecycle_stop_woken reached`; after a stdin watch that ends with no stop, once its stderr line has arrived on a pipe stderr, the controller held at "after the transfers drain" until then; each wait under the `trace` row's requirements, R3 among them (`extract.md` X10) |

**One stop path.** A stop has a **reason**, and each reason one exit code (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.3): `deadline`, `sigterm`, `sigint`, `sighup`, `parent_died` and `stdin_eof` exit 6; `spool_full` 5; `host_io` and `host_memory` 74; `internal` 70. No stop exits 7: a 7 is a hand-over's or the finisher's own (below).
- **`request_stop(r, culprit)`** is the one way to request a stop, and it is async-signal-safe. `r` is a reason and, for `spool_full`, its detail, a small code (2.7); no other reason has one, so `internal`'s is null and no panic text reaches the head. `culprit` is the input whose attempt failed the run, the bundle whose peek did (2.6 step 4), or none. It first stores both in the **stop word**, one atomic, the culprit with a kind bit, input or bundle, since an input's attempt and its own bundle's peek name one id, by a compare-and-swap that keeps the more severe reason, with its own detail and culprit, by `BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.1's precedence; of two equally severe, the first stays. The kept one is the stop's **culprit**: none for a stop no attempt or peek caused (a signal, the deadline, the parent's death, stdin's EOF, or the controller's own request, a blocking-pool task's failure among them). Then it writes one byte to the lifecycle thread's self-pipe, which never blocks (the pipe is non-blocking, and a full pipe already holds a wake). The **stop flag** is "the stop word holds a reason", so a stop holds for every thread from the moment `request_stop` returns. Its callers: the signal handlers (SIGTERM, SIGINT, SIGHUP and the parent's death), the lifecycle thread's timer and stdin watch, a worker before it sends `RunFailure` (2.4), and the controller (a spool claim refused in staging, a blocking-pool task's failure, a failed rename into `done/`, classified by 2.7, a phase-budget leak in phase 6, a host error in phase 7, `extract.md` E62, its own panic), which then hands over in the same turn (2.4) and writes the stop's stderr line only after its hand-over.
- **The lifecycle thread** wakes on the byte. Its only I/O is reading its self-pipe and its `timerfd`, both non-blocking, and `exit_now`'s in-place socket disconnects; it never reads stdin, so no read can hold `T_hard`. Under `stop_on_stdin_eof` the **stdin watch** runs on a small thread of its own, which requests the `stdin_eof` stop like any other caller of `request_stop`, and writes the one stderr line an ended watch writes, never to a regular file or a block device (*The standard descriptors* below); its thread is never joined, since a read that another reader of the description outran may block it until `_exit`. Neither thread allocates after its start, which each marks to the `tally` hook before its first `poll` (*Test hooks* above): the watch's two buffers come from step 4; its line, which only an ended watch builds (*The standard descriptors* below), is built and masked in the `PIPE_BUF` buffer, the masker writing into that fixed buffer, its fixed text and the masker's output cut to it as every stderr line is; `stdin_watch_ended` is an atomic flag; and the lifecycle thread's wakes are atomic stores and eventfd `write`s alone: it sends on no channel, takes no lock and unparks no thread (W9; A13; `extract.md` X10 counts it). Test-hook code on the thread (`point`, `trace`) allocates nothing after the mark, or runs with the mark cleared around it, so the count leaves it out; each of its writes there is a single `write(2)`, with no lock, of a line under `PIPE_BUF`, so W9's "takes no lock" holds under `test-hooks` too. It writes no regular file but a held `point`'s `reached` line, which it appends to the `trace` log before it waits; that write is made only at a hold the test arms, on a thread the test already holds, so it delays only a held thread and never a `T_hard` the run relies on. Its two trace lines go to the lifecycle trace FIFO (`<trace>.lifecycle`, *Test hooks* above, the `trace` row), under `test-hooks` alone, a FIFO and never a regular file, since a regular file's write can be held by writeback or a journal commit (*What cannot be bounded* below) and this thread owns `T_hard`; a run writes a few lines, so the FIFO never fills, R1 keeps out a stale line, and a failed write (`EAGAIN`, `EPIPE`) drops its line, as the stderr writer's does (*The standard descriptors* below). The two: `lifecycle_pipe_read`, after it reads a self-pipe byte and before that wake's work, so a stop by a signal or by another thread's request is logged there after its store, while a deadline or stdin-EOF stop, the thread's own request, reaches it only after its first wake's work; and `lifecycle_stop_woken`, once that first stop wake's work is done. At its first wake at which the stop word holds a reason it arms the **hard exit** at `T_hard = min(deadline, now + grace) - 500 ms`: one atomic, set once by compare-and-swap, here or by the finisher (below), whichever comes first. Then it writes the controller's wake eventfd, once, at that first wake only, so a controller waiting in phase 5, whose `poll` watches that eventfd (2.4), or in `block_on` sees the stop, its reason the stop word's: in `block_on` the controller selects on that eventfd's readiness (a tokio `AsyncFd` on its own runtime) and, woken, cancels its runtime's token itself, on its own thread. It then sets its timer for the stored `T_hard` and writes the finisher's wake eventfd: each finisher wait, for a permit, a token or a try, selects on that eventfd's readiness only while `T_hard` is unarmed, registering it before it reads `T_hard`, so no wake is missed; once woken, or once it reads `T_hard` armed, it selects on its deadline's timer instead, and ends there (steps 2 and 3), so it never spins on the eventfd, which, never read, stays ready once written. Each runtime has one `AsyncFd` per eventfd it waits on (step 4), shared by its concurrent waits, since a second registration of one descriptor fails (`EEXIST`). An eventfd `write` is one non-blocking system call, and two writes never fill its counter. tokio's `CancellationToken::cancel` and `Notify::notify_waiters` are never called on this thread: neither is shown allocation-free from a pinned source, since the repository pins neither `tokio` nor `tokio-util` yet, so the eventfds take their place.
- **Every exit** the engine makes goes through `exit_now(code)`: set the closing flag, disconnect the live socket table in place, then `libc::_exit(code)`, without flushing, allocating, taking a lock, turning a runtime or running a destructor. On Linux reset uses `connect(fd, AF_UNSPEC)`, leaving the descriptor, open file description and Tokio readiness registration unchanged. The disconnect readiness signal is `EPOLLHUP`, not `EPOLLERR`. Zero linger is set before connect so an ordinary owner close also sends RST. The static table has 514 entries, one per permit across both runtimes, and admits no fd below 3 or duplicate description. Publication precedes the closing load; closing, publication, load and scan are `SeqCst`. An opaque owner prevents external close/dup. Each entry packs its descriptor and generation in one atomic; reset rechecks that exact generation by CAS-sealing it before the syscall. Ordinary release CAS-clears the entry before owner close. If reset wins, the owner retains the sealed description through process exit. This closes the reuse window that a generation load alone would leave between its last check and syscall. Generations never wrap; exhausted entries refuse admission. Nested scans may reset the same sealed description; interrupted resets retry, other errors are counted and scanning continues. A connect crossing reset is disconnected again before exposing its result. B10c must adopt this ownership boundary: the older HTTP raw-fd callback closes in the wrong order and is not a production reset-table adapter. macOS uses best-effort `shutdown(SHUT_RDWR)`, non-normative. The Linux process acceptance requires a loopback peer to observe `ECONNRESET` while the reset child remains held, both after completed connect and after an unawaited nonblocking connect. True `SYN_SENT` has no peer socket; its local disconnect is separate evidence.
  The **SIGABRT handler** performs the same resets. It is installed without `SA_RESETHAND`, so a second SIGABRT on another thread also meets the handler and its resets. In an ordinary process it then restores SIGABRT's default action (`sigaction(SIGABRT, SIG_DFL)`) and raises SIGABRT again, delivered with that default action on handler return. As namespace init it retains its handler and calls `_exit(134)` after reset. Racing default-action signals retain the limitations below.
- **At `T_hard`**, if the process still runs, the lifecycle thread calls `exit_now` with the **end code**: the final code once stored (by the finisher, below, or by the main thread, *Threads* above), else the stop word's code. It writes nothing first.
- **A second signal**: each SIGTERM, SIGINT or SIGHUP handler first increments the **signal count**, a separate atomic, and the one that finds it already above 0 calls `exit_now` at once with the final code once stored, else with the stop word's code, or its own reason's when the word's is less severe. Only those handlers touch the count: a stop for another reason (the parent's death, stdin's EOF, the deadline, a run failure) and the finisher's taken bit leave it at 0. So an orchestrator's SIGTERM after a stdin EOF or a run failure is a first signal, the parent's death after a group SIGTERM is no signal, and the finisher still writes the manifest.
- **The finisher takes the reason**, with its detail and culprit, when it starts, by setting the word's taken bit. On a stop's hand-over it uses the reason it took and arms `T_hard` itself if the lifecycle thread has not yet, so on every stop path `T_hard` is armed before step 3. On a normal end's hand-over it uses none: a reason the word holds by then came after the controller's last check, and, like a later request, it changes nothing but the finisher's try budget, which can leave row files null (step 3: a 4). Once its last head write has ended (at once when the finish writes no head), the finisher stores the code it will exit with in the **final code**, an atomic: the written head's code when that write was acknowledged; 4, or a more severe code, when it failed, never started or its outcome is unknown (step 3); 7 when superseded; with no head to write, the stop's code, or a hand-over's. A 7, a superseded end's or a hand-over's, yields to a stop's code more severe by `BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.1's precedence: the reason the finisher took, or one the word holds at a hand-over; a hand-over's 69 yields to none, and its 74 only to 70. The `exit` line names the reason that set the code. This is the exit rule `BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.1 and `extract.md` E47 cite: a `T_hard` or a second signal exits with the final code once it is stored; before that, with the stop's code, and a final head write in flight may still land. With a deadline, the deadline less the grace is such a stop, so a normal-end finish ends by the deadline too, but for *What cannot be bounded* below.
- **The deadline** is converted once at start to a `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` instant. The stop starts at `deadline - grace` (E45). The lifecycle thread's timer, for that stop and then `T_hard`, is a `timerfd` on `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` in its poll set, made at step 4, so a suspend cannot delay its wake. A `timerfd_settime` that fails is a stop `internal`, and the thread then times each `poll` to the time left to `T_hard` on `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, at most 1 s, recomputed after every return, `EINTR`'s included.
- **Parent death** (E46). At step 5 the binary sets `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG` to a signal of its own, libc's `SIGRTMIN`, whose handler requests the stop `parent_died`. When the spec gives `parent_pid`, it then compares `getppid()` with it, and a difference (the parent died before the call) stops at once. Without `parent_pid` there is no start check.

**What the stop flag stops.**
- The controller dispatches nothing more, hands over to the finisher and parks until `_exit`, so the main thread's join never returns: a return from `main` would run exit handlers under the finisher. It is the only party that starts the finisher: after phase 7 on a normal end; on a stop at its next check; from its `catch_unwind` handler, with the stop `internal`; and at a 69 or 7 in preflight or at the `running` write (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2), or a 74 at the spool's cleanup (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.2) or a directory sink's `running` write (`extract.md` E62), with no stop reason but, for the `exit` line, the reason that set that code (`host_io` for the 74) and its fixed-form detail (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.3, section 10), so the finisher writes no head (steps 3 to 5). It joins no worker and no transfer. Its checks: in preflight, between its steps; every 1,000 entries that `BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.2's cleanup or a `Failed` attempt's delete (2.4) removes, a stop leaving the rest to the next start; before each rename into `done/` (2.4's commit order), a `Done` attempt's or a failure record's, at staging's end, at the peek pass's end and in phase 5, a stop leaving the rest uncommitted, `not_run` `stopped`, since each end sets every such bit before its first commit; each turn of its loop; in phase 6, between its tasks, each task making its own checks, before each write buffer of at most 64 MiB and at least once per 64 MiB a pass or a merge reads (2.9); in phase 7, before each transfer's start, each of resume's hashing passes' and each LIST page's (a pass also reads the flag per buffer, 2.9), after the transfers drain and again after verification; and at once after its own request. A stop seen at any of them makes the end a stop (in phase 7, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.4's row "in phase 7, on a stop"). A check every 64 MiB of a local read leaves a stop waiting well under a second of reads and CPU work on a local disk (A11), against a grace of at least 10 s; a buffered write the controller makes itself can hold it longer (*What cannot be bounded* below).
- Each attempt returns at its next check (2.6 step 11), and each peek between its rows (2.6 step 4), with the outcome `Stopped`.
- After the hand-over nobody polls the controller's runtime, so every staging and transfer future stays where it was, with its buffers and phase-budget reservations, until `_exit`. A request the server had already received may still complete; `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2 says who covers that.
- No transfer starts a try of a committing request (a PUT, a `CompleteMultipartUpload`) or a new multipart upload once the flag is set: it reads the flag just before each try, which the engine makes itself (2.9). A directory transfer reads it before each write buffer, as 2.5's local copy does, and on a stop returns without renaming, leaving its `.unityrs-tmp-*` file; its rename reads it under the commit lock, which the controller takes at its hand-over (2.9). The finisher's own writes are the exception.

**The finisher** owns every manifest write after `running`, the `exit` line and the process's exit, on a normal end and on a stop alike. It is started in preflight, parked on a one-shot channel, and run once, by the controller's hand-over (above). It runs on a current-thread runtime of its own, with a store client of its own, built in preflight after the credentials are read and before the spool starts, and never uses the controller's: that client's requests are futures on the controller's runtime, which nobody polls after the hand-over. From its take to its `exit` line it is the only producer of progress snapshots, since the controller writes none after its hand-over: it posts one at its take and one after each step, so the stream shows 0 `queued` once a stop is seen; each has `phase` `finish` and counts the inputs from its snapshot, a dispatched input that did not commit in `running` and `queued` 0, and its `exit` line counts that input in `not_run` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 10). In order:
1. **Close the journal.** When the spool's lock was never taken, this step and the next do nothing: a journal there is another process's. It takes the journal's mutex by a `try_lock` polled every 10 ms, which, once `T_hard` is armed, gives up at `T_hard - head_reserve` (step 3): a transfer can hold the mutex across a throttled append (W7). Giving up, it skips this step and the next, leaving every upload to the next start's aborts (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.2), as step 2 already may. Under the mutex it marks the journal closed; after the close nothing removes a line. It reads the journal streamed through a fixed buffer, holding only the ids of the aborts in flight, at most `download_concurrency` (step 2). A create whose response comes after the close is never journaled, and the bucket's lifecycle rule removes its upload (E64). The finisher starts no multipart upload, so the journal holds every upload the attempt can leave but those.
2. **Abort** every upload id the journal holds, in parallel, at most `download_concurrency` at once, each request with a timeout of `min(5 s, T_hard - now - head_reserve)` (step 3), 5 s when `T_hard` is not armed; when that is under 500 ms, it aborts nothing and leaves them to the next attempt's start (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.2), as it leaves the ids still waiting for a permit at `T_hard - head_reserve`, where step 2 ends, waiting for no token (2.5).
3. **The final manifest.** It uses the reason it took (none on a normal end), which no later stop changes, and the phase the controller reached. What it writes is `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.4's row: nothing in its first row; otherwise every row file, then `syncfs` for a directory, then the head, fenced (7.2). A `syncfs` error is `host_io` (74): the head is then written as `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.4 says, and if that write fails too the head stays `running`. A row file is written in chunks of at most 16 MiB, cut at line ends, each one single PUT or one file (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.1), streamed from the spool's files without a sort. In 7.4's second row (an object store only: the directory sink writes every head on the controller's or the finisher's own thread, so none is in flight at the hand-over), the final head carries the precondition of the `running` write's last try: it lands whether an unsettled try landed or not, through 7.2's read-back and rewrite when one did and they fit before its deadline (at the minimum grace, 10 s, `T_hard` is 9.5 s after the stop, so the head's whole 5 s reserve lies after it and all three may fit), and a try that lands later fails its precondition. A failed precondition is read back as `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2 says: superseded, the finisher writes nothing more, and a final write 7.2 gives up on is a write that fails. A write that fails, its outcome unknown included, or never starts, makes the code the more severe, by `BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.1's precedence, of 4 (`publish_failed`) and the code it had (the stop's, or 9.2's); the stored head is then the earlier one, unless a try whose response was lost lands.
   - **Its requests** (the row files, then the head's write, a read-back and its 7.2 rewrite) run one at a time, each on whichever of its runtime's `download_concurrency + 1` permits has its lookup token home (2.5); one still waiting for a token at its deadline does not start.
   - **Once `T_hard` is armed**, every try of the finisher but the head's, in flight or started later, ends by its deadline, `T_hard - head_reserve`, where `head_reserve` is 2 s, plus 1 s for the head's write, and 2 s more in 7.4's second row (5 s), for its read-back and rewrite, and no try is retried by 1.6's table. The remaining row-file chunks are skipped when less than `head_reserve` is left, and the head names that row file null in `rows`. Each of the head's requests ends by its deadline, `T_hard - 2 s`: its first write starts with whatever is left, and a read-back or the rewrite only with 1 s of it. So the head always gets its try, unless a call *What cannot be bounded* outlasts `T_hard`, dirty-page throttling holds a buffered write the try waits behind until then (*What cannot be bounded*), or stale lookups hold all `download_concurrency + 1` tokens, as when DNS is failing the head's own lookup too.
4. **The `exit` line.** It hands the line to the progress thread, which writes it before anything else and never overwrites it, and waits for the progress thread to report it written, at most 1 s, and never past `T_hard` when it is armed. After that wait, before step 5, it writes its stderr warning of a failed stat at its take, when there is one (2.4), a write whose hold `T_hard`, armed by then, bounds.
5. `exit_now` with the final code.

**Its working set** is fixed: one 16 MiB chunk buffer and a 1 MiB read buffer, one row file at a time, a 64 KiB buffer on the outcomes files, step 1's journal read buffer and the ids of step 2's aborts in flight, step 3's head read-back, and its runtime and client (5.3, `C_fin`). The rows it writes are already in their order in the spool: the `inputs`, `bundles`, `skips` and `diagnostics` rows in input-id order through the spec's id permutation, which the controller keeps from validation (`C_spec`); the `outputs`, `collisions`, `archives` and `archive_members` rows from phase 6's files, each status read in the same order from the outcomes files (2.9).

**When the grace runs out first,** the lifecycle thread's `_exit` ends the process. The stored head then says whatever its last write said: `running` with its deadline when the final write did not land. A reader reads it by `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.4's rule for a `running` head. The `exit` line may be missing; the exit status is authoritative.

**A held stdout.** To a regular file or a block device, the host's writeback can hold one write for minutes (A11), a progress line's or a stderr line's. No path that must end by `T_hard` waits on such a write: nothing joins the progress thread, and only the finisher and the main thread's failed-spawn hand-over wait on it, each for at most 1 s, the finisher's never past `T_hard` (W8, its step 4); the finisher's stderr warning of a failed stat (2.4) comes after its head and that wait, before step 5, and only `T_hard` bounds its hold; its mailbox's mutex is never held across a write (W16); the hard exit and a second signal call `exit_now`, which writes and flushes nothing; and the lifecycle thread writes no regular file but a held `point`'s `reached` line, which it appends to the `trace` log before it waits, a write made only at a hold the test arms, on a thread the test already holds, so it delays only a held thread and never a `T_hard` the run relies on; its two trace lines go to the lifecycle trace FIFO (*The standard descriptors* and *The lifecycle thread* below). Nor does a stop wait on one: the controller writes the stderr line of a stop it requests (a blocking-pool task's failure, a failed rename's `rename failed: <id> <errno>`, its own panic) only after its hand-over, and a worker its run failure's only after its `request_stop` (2.4), so a held line delays neither the stop nor the head's try. The throttle's sleep is killable (Linux's `balance_dirty_pages`), so `_exit` ends an engine write held there, and with it an engine write waiting behind it on the same file's lock. It does not end a write waiting on a lock another process holds: when another process writes the same regular file and is throttled holding the file's inode lock (*What cannot be bounded* below), the engine's write waits uninterruptibly, so a caller that needs the deadline gives the engine a stdout and a stderr of its own, a pipe or a file no other process writes (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 10). The `exit` line may then be missing, and the exit status is authoritative (`extract.md` E4). Before step 7 no lifecycle thread exists, but the spec's deadline does, from phase 1: with a deadline, every early line goes through the main thread's writer thread, which it waits on for at most `min(1 s, the time left - 500 ms)` (*The main thread's `exit` line* above). Without one (a start that fails before phase 1 accepts a spec, a 78, an accepted spec with `deadline: null`, `--check`), the main thread writes its lines itself: to pipes, FIFOs, sockets or terminals, both lines share one 1 s budget from the call, on their own non-blocking descriptions where they can be reopened or through the writer thread for the time left, as *The main thread's `exit` line* above states; to a regular file or a block device, with no spool yet, only other processes' dirty pages, the host's or the memory cgroup's, or another writer of the same file (*What cannot be bounded*), can hold it, and nothing bounds the start in time. **Test:** `a_held_stderr_line_delays_no_head` (`extract.md` X10): a blocking-pool task's panic, with a regular-file stderr whose write by the controller, which writes that panic's line, the `point` hook holds past the grace; the head is still written, by the finisher's try.

**Panics and stderr.** The binary installs its own panic hook at start. On every call it overwrites a preallocated slot of the panicking thread, length included, with the panic's location and the payload, cut to at most 1,024 bytes at a code point boundary, or the fixed text `non-string panic payload` when the payload is neither `&str` nor `String`, and writes nothing (a blocking-pool thread has no slot: its payload reaches the controller in its task's `JoinError`, *Threads* above). So no slot shows an earlier attempt's panic. The thread's `catch_unwind` then reads the slot:
- a worker or the controller: its payload into the skip record's `detail`, `cut` true when the hook cut it (a texture decode's panic, which the decoders contain themselves, takes its payload from `DecodeError::Panicked` instead, cut by the same rule, 2.7), or, for a run failure (2.4), a stop `internal`, its location and payload in 2.4's stderr line only;
- the progress thread: a stop `internal`; it writes nothing more, so the finisher's wait for the `exit` line runs out (step 4);
- the main thread: `exit_now` at once with 70;
- the main thread's writer thread (*The main thread's `exit` line* above): its panic drops its channel's sender, which ends the main thread's wait, and the main thread calls `exit_now` with the status it holds;
- the lifecycle thread, which owns the hard exit: `exit_now` at once with the more severe of 70 and the recorded reason's code;
- the finisher, which owns the end: `exit_now` at once with the more severe of 70 and its code so far. The stored head says what its last write said.

So no panic leaves the process running with nobody to end it, deadline or not. Every engine mutex a panicking thread held (section 4) is taken by its next user ignoring the poison (`PoisonError::into_inner`): that panic is already a stop `internal`, and a journal line it tore is skipped (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.1). Every stderr write the engine makes goes through one writer (*The standard descriptors* below), which first replaces every occurrence of each secret the engine holds (the credentials, the header values, and each URL's query and userinfo; an empty one skipped, every other masked however short) with a fixed mask, then writes the line in one write of at most `PIPE_BUF` bytes, cut at a code point boundary when longer, and drops it on any write error, `EAGAIN`, `EPIPE` and `ENOSPC` among them, counting it (`stderr_dropped`); a short write (a socket's, a terminal's) drops the rest of its line, counted alike. So a stderr that is never read stalls nothing.

**The standard descriptors.** The engine never changes the flags of the descriptions the caller gave on fds 0, 1 and 2, which may be shared between them (`2>&1`, a terminal). At step 1 the main thread gives the stderr writer a description of its own, by fd 2's type. A pipe, a FIFO or a character device other than `/dev/null` is opened again as `/proc/self/fd/2` with `O_WRONLY | O_NONBLOCK | O_CLOEXEC | O_NOCTTY`, and the new description is `dup2`ed onto fd 2, so Rust's own writes there (the allocation-error hook, `rtabort!`) cannot block on the caller's pipe either. A socket stays as it is, written with `send(..., MSG_DONTWAIT)`. Any other type (a regular file, `/dev/null`, a block device) is written as it is, a file's or a device's write a W5 call. When the reopen fails (`EACCES` after a change of uid, say), the main thread `dup2`s `/dev/null` onto fd 2, so nothing the process writes there can block, and the writer drops and counts every line; when `/dev/null` cannot be opened either, the start fails at step 1 (step 5's `startup_failed`), its `exit` line on stdout. The progress thread takes `EAGAIN` on stdout as not ready and `poll`s, since a caller may give a non-blocking stdout, and finishes a partly written line before it writes the next, the `exit` line included. The stdin watch's own thread (*The lifecycle thread* above) reads stdin only under `stop_on_stdin_eof`, and never reopens it. At step 4 an fd 0 that is not a pipe, a FIFO, a socket or a terminal, or not open for reading (`F_GETFL`), cannot be watched (a regular file, `/dev/null` and `/dev/zero` are always readable) and exits 69 (`startup_failed`, its detail `stdin`); an fd 0 the caller closed is one, since Rust's runtime opens `/dev/null` there before `main`. fd 0 is in the stdin watch's poll set for `POLLIN | POLLHUP`, and at a wake it takes `FIONREAD`'s count. `POLLHUP` with no count above 0 is EOF, with no read, so a FIFO's next writer cannot make a read block; otherwise it reads at most that count, up to its 4 KiB buffer, or 1 byte when the count is 0 or the call fails, a read that cannot block while the engine is stdin's only reader (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.1), and that, when another reader takes the bytes first, holds only the watch's own thread, never `T_hard`; a socket is read with `recv(..., MSG_DONTWAIT)`. A 0-byte read is EOF, and so are `EIO` on a terminal (a background job's read, or a hung-up terminal) and `ECONNRESET`, `ETIMEDOUT` or `ENOTCONN` on a socket; `EAGAIN` and `EINTR` mean `poll` again. `POLLERR` or `POLLNVAL` without that EOF, or any other error, ends the stdin watch without a stop: it stores `stdin_watch_ended`, the head's warning, before it writes its one stderr line (a connected UDP socket as stdin, its peer's port closed, ends it so: the ICMP error is `POLLERR` with no EOF, `extract.md` X10's scene). Only an ended watch builds that line; an EOF builds none. On a regular file or a block device the line is dropped and counted (`stderr_dropped`) instead: writeback can hold such a write for minutes (A11), and the lifecycle thread owns the hard exit. An EOF or an ended watch ends the watch's thread, so nothing polls fd 0 again.

**What cannot be bounded.** Every wait above has a timeout or a cancellation but a running lookup and two kinds of filesystem call. tokio cannot cancel a lookup's blocking task, so a running `getaddrinfo` is bounded only by the host resolver's configured timeouts and attempts (A17), though `_exit` ends it. Linux cannot interrupt the filesystem calls. One is any call (a read, a write, a rename, `fsync`, `syncfs`, `statvfs`) on a filesystem that stops answering. The other is a directory sink's final `syncfs` and, on ext4 in `data=ordered` or `data=journal` mode, the head's temp-file and directory `fsync`s and the uploads journal's syncs (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.1, made under the mutex the finisher's step 1 takes), whose journal commit waits on other files' data: each lasts as long as the unflushed data on its filesystem takes to write, the spool's included when they share one: 2.9 starts each output's writeback as it commits, and 5.6 recommends a spool on another filesystem, but a backlog can still hold the process past `T_hard`, leaving the head `running`. On ext4 in `data=ordered` mode a metadata call (a rename, a create, an unlink) may also wait on a journal commit that waits on that backlog, and so may a buffered write's timestamp update, which starts a jbd2 handle (`file_update_time`), and a write to a page under writeback on a device that needs stable pages, which waits for that writeback (each unverified). An unlink's final `iput` may also wait for the file's writeback (`truncate_inode_pages_final`), and a truncate for its pages' (each unverified), so every unlink or truncate the controller makes can wait as long: `BATCH_SPEC.md` 6.2 step 5's cleanup, the likeliest after a run killed at its deadline, a `Failed` attempt's delete (2.4), a failed staging try's truncate (2.5) and an archive's central directory at its transfer's end (2.9); phase 6's deletes are made by its pool tasks (2.9). Dirty-page throttling is a third kind. While the host's dirty pages are over its global dirty limit, or a memory cgroup's over the cgroup's, each counted across every filesystem, a buffered write can be held for minutes (A11). For the engine's own writes it cannot hold the process past `T_hard`, since the throttle's sleep is killable and `_exit` at `T_hard` ends such a write, but it can hold the controller before its hand-over and cost the head its try (step 3).
