The core counts allocator refusals by the meter's `Reservation` too, and a refusal a caller's code returns (a fetcher's, a codec's) where it was made: by a meter of the core's, or by `LimitError::refused` when the caller built a `LimitError`; another error a caller builds is no refusal of the core's and counts nothing |
| **CR9, wanted; not a prerequisite of version 1** | **Scope.** A per-load opt-in: a flag on `load_dependency`'s new parameter beside CR7's hint (CR9 adds it there, or brings that parameter if it lands first). `Dependency` keeps it beside its `source`, and every reopen and retry carries it (`env/registry/dependency.rs`, `handle`), so no `FileContext` field is needed. `EnvOptions::unitypy()`, a bare `BundleFile::open` and every load that does not ask, the resolver's among them, stay whole-block, and a nested bundle of an opted-in load does not inherit it (nested bundles are not expected in real builds, core Q10). The engine sets it for an attempt's dependency members (2.6 step 4), not for X's own bundle, whose CAB parse is followed by its `.resS` reads in the same block. Only the built-in LZMA cuts, so a caller's own `Codec::Lzma` turns it off (`codec/mod.rs`); LZ4, user codecs and any block a decryptor reads (`files/bundle/decode.rs`, `decrypted`) stay whole-block, with the whole-length prepay. A read inside an opted-in LZMA block of a dependency member (on the corpus, its one large block) decodes only about as much of that block as the read needs. **What it must keep.** A read that succeeds returns exactly the bytes the block's stream decodes to at `[lo, hi)`, as an unlimited whole decode writes them. A read whose decode meets a data failure below its cut fails with the data failure an unlimited whole decode of the block meets there (so a failure before or inside `[lo, hi)` fails it, as it fails the eager read); a data failure's place is the number of bytes liblzma has written when it returns `LZMA_DATA_ERROR` (its `total_out`, the bytes every call of that decode attempt (one dictionary, `lzma_attempt`) wrote from the block's start, the probe's byte included); `LzmaFail::Data` carries the place and the pre-probe count: below the cut, the read fails; at or past the cut, the failure is kept and the read succeeds, whichever call meets it: the call that fills the output to the cut (liblzma decodes one symbol ahead, so a match at offset `cut` is met there) or the probe after its byte (checklist 17); the probe's byte counts in the place (keep or fail against the cut), while the retry test and its widening both read the pre-probe count, so that a load that does not opt in changes no outcome; and every data failure a read reports or keeps is the whole decode's own (a smaller dictionary's error is retried, never reported or kept), so outcomes change only as **Records change** says. Every byte it decodes or holds is charged to the meter, so 5.1 and `D` hold. Every byte it writes, and every compressed byte it consumes beyond those, counts toward the open's decode work under `max_decode_work`, as core 4.1's bounded-work row counts the whole-block path, so every open stays within `4 M` (2.6 step 7) and `limit(decode_work)` stays the refusal; how it counts is the PR's. It is deterministic within an attempt, which is sealed and runs its reads in order, a reopen keeping no outcome. **Records change, both ways.** A failure at or past the cut no longer fails a prefix read, unless the open already keeps it (checklist 16 and 17), so a prefix read's outcome can depend on the open's earlier reads and on `D`'s evictions, deterministically within the sealed attempt, and a read past the prefix costs the prefix plus its next cut, or plus the block when it leaves the block, the prefix's live slices still charged, so `limit(decode_work)`, `limit(input_memory)` and `D` outcomes, and the head's `dependency_peak` and `decode_work_peak`, can move either way; on the corpus, by estimate, no outcome changes (the next column). Landing it widens differences (c) and (d): `BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.3 (c) and (d), `docs/plans/core.md` 3.13 (c) and (d), and `PARITY.md` section 19.3's two `LoadMode::Lazy` rows. It changes core.md section 0's decision 4 (each block learned once per open, and a lazy read's equality with the eager read), 3.6's lazy-read, **Runs**, **Decode work** and **What a read costs** bullets, the module docs' **Block outcomes** (`files/bundle/mod.rs`) and 4.1's bounded-work row; three code docs, `files/bundle/decode.rs`'s **The input is work**, `codec/lzma.rs`'s **The dictionary** and `RunIndex`'s covering docs (`files/bundle/index/mod.rs`); and `BundleCounts` (`files/bundle/counts.rs`), its `decoded` and `redecoded` (checklist 18 and 21); and it adds to 5.5's Dependency peak a term CR9's own PR states; with their tests. **CR9's contract is its own core PR's.** CR9's own core PR designs its contract (what is cut, memory, work, byte extent, refusals, kept failures, counts) against the code it changes, and is reviewed as its own design before it is built. The checklist below carries every point the plan's review raised. **The core PR's checklist**, one point each. The contract as the plan's review left it (design rounds 22 to 29): (1) what is cut: a read whose span lies in one unknown opted-in LZMA block, past any cached prefix of it, decodes that block only to its cut, an in-block length, `hi` less the block's start, and a read inside the cached prefix decodes nothing; (2) the floor: a first cut is at least 4 KiB, so a 21-byte read decodes 4 KiB; (3) the re-cut and doubling: past a cached prefix the cut is `max(hi less the block's start, twice the cached cut)`, decoded from the block's start, again and not whole because the interim reach's 21-byte `is_bundle_entry` read of a member (`env/registry/reach.rs`) comes before its CAB parse, which would otherwise decode the 69 MB block whole; (4) the earlier prefix stays cached and charged until the re-cut's run publishes, a successful publish dropping it; (5) the block's end: a decode that reaches it or ends short of the cut is the full decode, learned and kept, a read ending exactly at its block's end leaves it learned, and the declared size never caps a cut; (6) a read that leaves the block, a span of two or more blocks, decodes its unknown blocks whole, its last included, and a read whose bounds need the total length (`entry_span`'s negative offset or size, through `measure_all`) decodes every block whole; (7) a prefix is never learned, an exception to the module docs' rule that every block starting below a read's end has a known length (`files/bundle/mod.rs`); (8) the generations: a block's successful cuts are at most about log2(its length / 4 KiB) per open while its runs stay cached, and under refused publishes only the work limit bounds them; (9) memory: nothing is allocated past the cut, each window clipped to the cut less the bytes produced and the segment's room capped at the cut, the declared-size hint applying below that cap, the cut applying to one segment and cleared at the next block's start; (10) a prefix read retains at most its cut-sized run, a cut's own peak is at most twice the cut (a growth holds both buffers, `limits/vec.rs`) plus the dictionary and the decoder's state, and beside a re-cut sit the live earlier prefix and its slices, at most about one cut more while publishes succeed, 5.5's term; (11) the dictionary: `min(named, clamp(cut, 4 KiB, 8 MiB))`; under CR9 a prefix decode's retry widening is capped at `min(named, 2 x cut)`, which, the widening reading the pre-probe count, is a bound the PR tests, not a change, and a whole decode widens as now; (12) work: a prefix decode counts at least the larger of the bytes it writes, a retry's rewrites among them, and the compressed bytes it consumes, at most their sum, plus the run index's steps, and is not prepaid (the whole-length prepay, `files/bundle/decode.rs`, `reading`), so a prefix read is refused only when its own count passes the allowance; (13) the totals over a block's successful cuts: bytes written at most about twice the last cut, a retry's rewrites aside, and work at most about twice the last cut's own, retries included; (14) byte extent: a cached prefix never serves a read past its byte extent, every lookup that finds a covering run checking a prefix's byte extent against the read's end, and not a full run's, since a full run ends before `hi` only where the read runs past the data; that covers every way a prefix's block is learned while the prefix is cached, a refused full publish (`Published::Refused`, `files/bundle/index/mod.rs`), a refused step count, a size −1 row's `measure_all` and a later block's measuring walk, and a run that replaces a prefix leaves no run containing another (core 3.6's **Runs**); (15) refusals: a refused publish leaves the index as it was, after a first cut no prefix, after a re-cut the earlier prefix, the next read past it cutting at `max(hi less the block's start, twice that prefix)` again, and after a full decode a stale prefix under byte extent, a later read's work counted again; (16) kept failures: a kept full-decode failure fails a later prefix read at once (`runs.rs`, `measure`), and a prefix's data failure is kept as the block's outcome, since the codec returns one only with the named dictionary or one at least as large as the retry test's count (the pre-probe count) (`codec/lzma.rs`, **The dictionary**), so it is the full decode's own, and an outcome can depend on the open's earlier reads and on `D`'s evictions; a block's kept outcome is checked before any cached prefix, the pre-search's included; (17) the probe: a decode that meets a data error at or past the cut, in the call that fills the output to it or in the probe, keeps it as the block's outcome, and that read still succeeds; (18) counts: a prefix decode's bytes count in `decoded`, and `redecoded`, by its definition, counts the bytes of its block an earlier decode of this open wrote, for a known block its whole length, as now. Design round 30's points: (19) live uncached runs: a refused publish returns its run uncached (`runs.rs`), live through its slices, so the buffers beside a cut are every distinct older backing buffer still live, uncached ones among them, and the bound of (10) holds only over generations whose publishes succeeded; (20) the unknown block end: an unknown block's end is unknown until it is decoded and its declared size plays no part, so (1) and (6) are stated in what the core knows (a read whose first block is unknown opted-in LZMA cuts at `hi` less its start whatever the declared size, a decode ending short being the full decode, and the read going on as a span, its later unknown blocks whole with the prepay), and the first dictionary is `min(named, clamp(min(cut, declared), 4 KiB, 8 MiB))`, revising (11); (21) `redecoded` for an unknown block: the lesser of this decode's length and the longest earlier prefix; (22) whether a read that keeps a data error (in the call that fills to the cut, or in the probe) caches its prefix or returns it uncached; a prefix cached with a kept error serves no later read; (23) the work total under an adversarial stream that consumes most of its input in its first 4 KiB, its consumed bytes totalling about the generations times the last cut's; (24) `publish`'s `Covered` made extent-aware, without which a re-cut is answered with the old prefix (`files/bundle/index/mod.rs`, `covering`). The mechanism (design rounds 26 and 27): how the prefix leaves `first_block` (a one-block span, `span_end` not entered); the work counters (a running maximum, or prepay and give back), and whether input is counted before or after it is read (round 6's "refused ... unread", `files/bundle/decode.rs`); the room cap through `make_room` and the window clip; the byte-extent checks in `cached()`, `publish`'s `Covered` and the pre-search; the retry's truncation; the retry test and its widening (`codec/lzma.rs`'s `max(2 x produced, 2 x dict)`) both reading the pre-probe count, so a prefix decode's retry stays within `min(named, 2 x cut)`, a bound the PR tests, and a whole decode widens as now; `is_bundle_entry`'s 21-byte read before CR7; liblzma's buffer-space path against clipped windows; the one-byte probe at the cut, into a byte not kept; the probe's count and its byte, a change only in an opted-in decode, and only when the place reaches the cut (the probe's byte at `cut - 1`): the error is classified first and the byte then appended, a refused append being that refusal; everywhere else the probe maps its error before its byte, as today (`codec/lzma.rs`), and the probe at the cut writes into a byte not kept; open points for the PR: a no-progress probe at the cut (`LZMA_BUF_ERROR`), the exactly-`cut` test avoiding `cut = 4096`, and a block cap at `cut - 1` in an opted-in load (`max_block_output`), whose prefix read keeps the error but cannot append the probe's byte. **The PR's tests:** a prefix read, its retained charge at most the cut-sized run, its peak at most twice the cut plus the dictionary and the state, and its work at least the larger of the bytes written and the compressed bytes consumed and at most their sum, plus its steps; a work upper bound the whole-length prepay fails, on a block whose stored bytes exceed the sum of both terms plus the steps; an allowance between the prefix's work and the stored length, under which the prefix read succeeds; a window request larger than the remaining cut, allocating nothing past it; a one-block bundle read through a prefix, giving the CAB's bytes; a prefix in a block k > 0 behind known blocks, its cut measured from the block's start; a second read inside the prefix, decoding nothing; a cut to `c1`, then a read to `c2` inside the block, cutting at `max(c2, 2 x c1)`, its work about the two cuts plus steps; a re-cut's peak, at most the old prefix plus twice the new cut plus the dictionary and the state; a refused re-cut publish, then a read past the old prefix, cutting at twice the old cut; an entry ending exactly at its block's end, the block learned; a stream whose decode meets `LZMA_DATA_ERROR` after exactly `cut` bytes (a match at offset `cut` whose distance reaches before the stream's start), run with `declared = cut` and with `declared = cut - 1` (a literal pending, then the probe), each read succeeding and the error kept, with the one retry expected when the first dictionary is below `named`, its work including the retry's rewrite; a read whose probe keeps an error, then the same read again, and the exactly-`cut` stream read twice: each second read fails at once, with no decode; a load without the flag, `declared` 4095, `named` above 4096, an invalid match at 4096: no retry, and the same charges and work as today; the corpus's shape, a 21-byte read, then the CAB parse, the first cutting at 4 KiB and the second at the CAB's end or twice the first; a prefix read followed by a read that leaves the block, its work about the prefix plus the block plus steps and its held memory at most the live prefix plus the block; a span whose first block is known and whose last is unknown, decoding the last whole; a block that ends before the cut, learned and kept; a negative offset, which decodes the block whole; a valid stream with a short declared length, decoding correctly within the prefix bounds; a prefix that succeeds where a whole-block decode fails, by a corrupt tail past the cut, and by `max_block_output` set between the cut and the stream's length; a stale prefix: a prefix cached, then a refused full publish, a size −1 row's `measure_all` or a later block's measuring walk, then a read past the cut, giving the full bytes; a kept failure in both orders: a prefix read, then a farther read meeting the failure, then a prefix read failing at once; and a farther read meeting it first, then a prefix read failing at once; a prefix's own data failure kept, a corrupt byte before the cut, then a farther read failing at once with no decode; a cut above 8 MiB that needs a long-distance match, its dictionary at most `min(named, 2 x cut)` after the retry; a publication refusal after a first cut, after which a read cuts again; a prefix read after an eviction and reopen; a decrypted block, a user LZMA codec, an LZ4 block in an opted-in load, a nested bundle of an opted-in load and a load without the flag (`unitypy()`, a bare open, the resolver's), each staying whole; and, from design round 30: a large first cut whose publish is refused, its result retained, then a read of the block's first 21 bytes cutting at 4 KiB and a re-cut to 8 KiB beside it, the held memory counting every live buffer; repeated refused publishes, their results retained; a large refused publish followed by a smaller cut, `redecoded` counting the smaller cut's bytes alone; a read past a block's declared end that leaves it, its dictionary the whole decode's. | a read near a block's start costs that prefix, not the block: the NGUI cutter's cross-bundle name read (2.2) parses the larger crossed block's CAB entry, its first 0.18 to 0.26 MB (181,472 B on build A, 263,152 B on build B; the 2.1 MB block's CAB entry was not measured; the `_MainTex` object ends 0.02 to 0.10 MB in, and the rest of the block is the `.resS`), so CR9 would cut that reach's 45 to 48 GB a run to about 0.12 to 0.18 GB a build. By estimate it changes no outcome on the corpus, where the opted-in dependency blocks are read only for the CAB; its figures change: `dependency_peak`, the `inputs` rows' `ms` and the head's `vm_hwm`. For each of the 686 inputs (685 on build B) crossing the larger block, the crossed member's share of its `dependency_peak` falls from about 145 MB to about 9 MB (its 8.1 MB file, read whole, plus under 1 MB of prefix and transient, a cut-sized dictionary assumed), and its figure from about its view's staged member bytes plus 137 MB (135 MB on build B) to about those bytes plus 1 MB; the four a build that cross only the 2.1 MB block were not measured for room. Measured over `m_Dependencies`' direct views on both builds, those inputs' staged member bytes are a median 13.5 MB and at most 17.3 MB, so their figure falls from a median of about 151 MB (149 MB on build B) and at most about 154 MB (152 MB) to a median of about 14.5 MB and at most about 18 MB. The head's figure, the most of any input, falls from about 154 MB (152 MB) to about 18 MB by `m_Dependencies` views, the largest view over all inputs staging 17.3 MB of members on both builds (18.0 MB on build B through the closure), or to 25 to 31 MB by the manifests' dependency lists, the graph a first consumer builds (`extract.md` E9), whose largest view is no crossing input's. The head's `decode_work_peak`, the most of any one meter, does not move on either build: the container holding the larger block is itself an input, the largest by decoded size, and its own attempt, which opts nothing in, decodes that block whole with or without CR9. All of these are estimates from staged sizes, not measured peaks. | every read decodes its whole block (5.5), and the NGUI reach costs what 2.2 measures |
| **CR10, required to remove the post-head allocator-abort interim (B7c Addendum 3); separate core/export PR** | Every infallible allocation reached after the running head in `unityrs-core` or `unityrs-export`; the inventory by module and function is below. Make owners, collections and reason construction fallible and return allocator refusal to the caller, or reserve actual heap storage before the running head and transfer it into these calls. A meter/accounting charge alone is insufficient. Preserve metering, owner lifetimes and classifications; report refusal as `host_memory` after charges drop. | Within-M host overcommit refusals surface as `host_memory`; no batch or CLI exception is added. The release sweep checks the innermost allocating function, rejects unlisted aborts and stale entries for the current platform, and requires `host_memory`/74 with the existing head kept for every other refusal. | Until CR10, allocator refusal at these sites aborts the process (2.7). The checked function inventory must shrink as CR10 makes them fallible. |


CR10's full inventory follows. Paths in each crate are relative to `src/`.
“All” includes Linux and Darwin; a path-dependent allocation need not occur
in every fixture. The platform tags describe allocations, not numeric sweep
positions.

| crate | module | function and allocation | platform |
|---|---|---|---|
| `unityrs-core` | `budget/mod.rs` | `charged_bytes`: `Bytes::from_owner`'s owner box, reached by `DecodeBuf::into_bytes` and charged `RawStr` construction/concatenation | All |
| `unityrs-core` | `files/mod.rs` | `RawFile::with_reader`: RawFile `Arc` | All |
| `unityrs-core` | `files/bundle/open.rs` | `BundleFile::open_with_reader`: parsed-container `Arc::new_cyclic` | All |
| `unityrs-core` | `files/bundle/runs.rs` | `BundleFile::read_span_pinned` → `cached`: std's lazily boxed mutex | Darwin only |
| `unityrs-core` | `files/bundle/index/mod.rs` | `RunIndex::publish`: run-index `BTreeMap` | All |
| `unityrs-core` | `env/mod.rs` | `Environment::new`: Environment and dependency-cache `Arc`s | All |
| `unityrs-core` | `env/mod.rs` | `Environment::read_path`: std Unix long-path `OpenOptions` storage through `File::open` | All, on long paths |
| `unityrs-core` | `env/load.rs` | `Environment::try_load_dep`: dependency `Arc` | All |
| `unityrs-core` | `env/registry/register.rs` | `Registry::prepare` → `new_slot`: path-owner and Slot `Arc`s; `Registry::apply`: registry maps | All |
| `unityrs-core` | `env/registry/lru/mod.rs` | `DepCache::touch_paid` → `Lru::use_key`: dependency-cache `HashMap` | All |
| `unityrs-core` | `env/registry/lru/mod.rs`, `env/held.rs` | `DepCache::touch_paid` → `held::lock`: std's lazily boxed mutex | Darwin only |
| `unityrs-core` | `limits/meter.rs` | `Meter::with_decode_work`, reached through `new` and `for_input`: account and meter `Arc`s; `Meter::reopen`: meter `Arc`; `Meter::unlimited`, reached through `NodeList::trusted`: unbounded-budget `Arc` and meter construction | All |
| `unityrs-core` | `typetree/rows.rs` | `NodeRow::new`: head-row type/name text copies; `NodeList::trusted`: head-tree meter construction | All |
| `unityrs-core` | `typetree/tree.rs` | `Tree::compile_inner`: compiled Tree `Arc` | All |
| `unityrs-export` | `ngui/read.rs` | `Reader::new` → `head_rows`: row `Vec`s and text copies via `NodeRow::new`, `NodeList` `Arc`s, compiled `Tree` `Arc`s and their meters | All |
| `unityrs-export` | `ngui/plan.rs`, `ngui/keys.rs` | `Attempt::plan`: template clones through derived `Template::clone` (piece `Vec`) and `Piece::clone` (text `Vec`s) | All |
| `unityrs-export` | `ngui/classify.rs` | `Reason::plain`, `Reason::decode_error`, `text`, `with_argument`: owned reason/detail strings; `limit`, `decode`, `render`: formatted limits; `panicked`, `entry_error`: owned reason/detail construction. Their `budget`, `charge`, `tree`, `parse`, `deref`, `error`, `resource`, `outside_view`, `outside_view_cab`, `internal` and `mesh` wrappers reach these constructors | All |
| `unityrs-export` | `ngui/register.rs` | `dependency_failed`: formatted dependency wrapper; `container`: container reclassification; `open`, `opened`, `own`, `internal`, `peeked`, `Peeked::member`, `Peeked::finish`, `same_open`: reason construction through classify's constructors and these wrappers | All |

The executable exclusion inventory is
`crates/unityrs-batch/tests/controller_support/refusals.rs`. Its keys are the
**innermost owning `unityrs_core::` or `unityrs_export::` functions** on the
allocating backtrace, including inline frames. Closure numbers, generic
arguments, allocation sizes and ordinals are not keys. A std generic function
that mentions a core/export type does not own that allocation. The test
rejects an innermost batch/CLI function even when a core/export ancestor is
listed, and rejects an unsymbolized abort. `Cargo.toml` defines
`[profile.sweep]` with `inherits = "release"` and `debug = "limited"`:
qualified inline function names as well as line tables;
`line-tables-only` alone produced unqualified names on Darwin. CI's `scale`
command includes `--test controller_alloc` under `--profile sweep`; use
that profile for manual refusal sweeps too. The release profile is unchanged.

For the current synthetic sweep, **Linux expects all 18 `Platform::All`
function entries**: `charged_bytes`, `RawFile::with_reader`, `Reason::plain`,
`BundleFile::open_with_reader`, `Environment::new`,
`Meter::with_decode_work`, `Environment::try_load_dep`,
`Registry::new_slot`, `Registry::apply`, `RunIndex::publish`, `Lru::use_key`,
`head_rows`, `NodeRow::new`, `Meter::unlimited`, `Reader::new`,
`Tree::compile_inner`, `Template::clone` and `Piece::clone`.
**CR10 PR 1 boundary.** `AllocError { requested, what }` is carried by value
through charge, detection, tree, read, parse and dereference errors. The default
`MemoryBudget::allocator_refused` observer is notified once at detection, never
again by conversion or cloning. `SealedBudget` keeps a separate saturating
allocator counter, excluded from its budget-refusal total. Export probes check
it even after successful calls; registration checks it after post-commit settle
before handing a file to another read. Batch drops the entire attempt before
its account audit and refuses success/publication on any observation. The
independent global allocator NULL counter remains a second detector. CR1's
successful-commit boundary is unchanged. PR 1 migrates no allocation site and
removes no inventory entry; the support primitives and site migrations follow.
CR2's public refusal-query API is not supplied by CR10.

**CR10 PR 5 detection obligation.** Pass a budget or meter into detection so
allocator refusals notify the audit, or explicitly document returned-only
allocator provenance there. The reach and export bundle probes must return
allocator detection errors even when ordinary malformed detections mean no bundle.

Darwin additionally expects `BundleFile::cached` and `env::held::lock`.
The inventory tags those two mutex allocations as Darwin-only; Linux does
not expect them. Entries state their exercised scopes (`peek`, `attempt`,
`complete`); `stage` and `failure` have no exclusions. All five controller
scopes include the live progress writer through its terminal acknowledgment.
Separate `progress` and `progress-delayed` scopes arm refusal after
`Service::start` returns and require zero allocations through snapshots and
the terminal line; the latter holds the writer before clock initialization.
Startup waits for writer readiness. Its procfs clock and RSS reads use fixed
stack buffers through `sys`, including the clock read checked under refusal
in the library tests. The progress scopes have no exclusions. Every entry
for the current platform must abort at least once in each named scope, or it
is stale.
The broader CR10 request also covers branches this fixture does not reach,
including other Reason constructors, meter reopen and long-path opens; these
are not dormant exclusions. Every other refused allocation must return
`host_memory`/74 and keep the head's `running` status and write sequence.

**Withdrawn with design round 1's shared cache:** `Environment::evict_dependencies`, a public `with_room`, `deref_held`, and the `DecodeWork` request. A sealed attempt's Environment is dropped whole, and no outcome waits for a retry.

---

## 7. Assumed (not checked)

- **A1.** Sizes not measured: a transfer's connection and TLS state, with one request's URL, signing and request-head copies at `BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.2's and 1.6's caps, one response head at the 128 KiB cap the HTTP service sets (2.5), after a 1xx at that cap, and a redirect's state, the current URL (at most 16 KiB as the `url` crate serializes it) and the join of a `Location` of at most 16 KiB, whose `String` grows by doubling to at most 64 KiB (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.2, 1.4), is at most 4 MiB (`c_net`), at the larger of two instants below. rustls's share is in `heap_cost` with each growing `realloc` at its peak (2.7), from rustls 0.23.46's and rustls-pki-types 1.15.1's source. rustls reads a list by pushing onto an empty `Vec` (`msgs/codec.rs`), and bounds a handshake message at 64 KiB (`MAX_HANDSHAKE_SIZE`), not its elements: a TLS 1.2 Certificate of 21,844 empty entries (3 bytes each) is a `Vec` of 24-byte `CertificateDer`s at capacity 32,768, 772 KiB, whose last growth peaks at 1,160 KiB beside the old 388 KiB, and which `into_owned` keeps, or copies at 516 KiB beside it while it runs; one of 16,383 one-byte entries (4 bytes each) is a `Vec` at capacity 16,384, 388 KiB, beside which `into_owned` copies each entry with `to_vec`, a block of 32 bytes' `heap_cost` each, so 900 KiB is kept (`msgs/handshake.rs`); a TLS 1.3 one of 13,106 minimal entries (5 bytes each) one of 48-byte entries at capacity 16,384, also 772 KiB; and a CertificateRequest of 21,842 one-byte names (3 bytes each) a `Vec` of 24-byte `DistinguishedName`s at capacity 32,768 (772 KiB), each name a block of 32 bytes' `heap_cost` (683 KiB), beside which the client collects `acceptable_issuers`, 21,842 slices of 16 bytes (344 KiB, `client/common.rs`). The handshake's worst instant is that collection on TLS 1.2, while the chain, the OCSP staple and the ServerKeyExchange are kept until ServerHelloDone (`client/tls12.rs`): a deframer buffer under 128 KiB (a `Vec` grown in 4 KiB steps), the chain's 900 KiB, the staple's and the ServerKeyExchange's 64 KiB each, the request's 1,799 KiB, a send buffer of 64 KiB (`DEFAULT_BUFFER_LIMIT`) and received plaintext of 16 KiB, each with one more record, and keys and state under 16 KiB: 3,083 KiB. TLS 1.3's CertificateRequest comes before its Certificate, and no name outlives it (`client/tls13.rs`), so its worst is about 2 MiB. The HTTP service runs hyper's handshake only once tokio-rustls's has returned (2.5), and tokio-rustls keeps no buffer of its own: its early-data buffer exists only with early data on, which the service never sets (tokio-rustls 0.26.6, `src/client.rs`). So at the handshake a connection holds rustls's 3,083 KiB, the redirect's state (under 128 KiB), the request's URL, signing and head copies (under 128 KiB, hyper's head encode at its growth's 3-copy peak among them; a bundle's header block is `C_hdr`'s, 5.3) and the 64 KiB streaming buffer: 3,403 KiB, about 3.3 MiB. After it, rustls keeps the chain, the deframer, its buffers and state, 1,156 KiB, beside hyper's read buffer, under 896 KiB however the server paces its records: its head part, a 1xx's consumed prefix included, stays under 512 KiB, its last growth peaking under 768 KiB, and the body's, read only into an empty buffer, peaks under 384 KiB beside it, the two under 768 KiB once the body has ended (hyper skips a 1xx with `split_to` and checks a complete head's length, or an unfinished head's buffer, against its caps, never the prefix a 1xx consumed, `src/proto/h1/role.rs`, `src/proto/h1/io.rs`; bytes' `reserve_inner` keeps the consumed prefix when it grows the buffer, to at most twice its old capacity or the prefix and the room asked, and allocates a new one while the head's `HeaderMap` slices hold the old: bytes 1.12.1, the version `Cargo.lock` pins, `src/bytes_mut.rs`), at a chunked body's end a trailer section's buffer, which `max_header_size` caps too, under 256 KiB with its growth peaking under 384 KiB, then that section's parsed copies beside it (`src/proto/h1/decode.rs`), the head's parsed copies (custom header names, the reason phrase, the `HeaderMap`, `object_store`'s header `String`s), each set of copies about 140 KiB, hyper's write buffer, which under `writev(true)` queues at most 16 slices of the body's `Bytes` and copies none (`src/proto/h1/io.rs`; tokio-rustls reports vectored writes anyway, `src/client.rs`), and the same three: at most about 2.7 MiB over TLS, and 1.6 MiB over `http`, which has no rustls state. A head GET adds `object_store`'s copies of its head (the `ETag` and its clone, the `x-amz-meta-*` fields; `src/client/header.rs`, `src/client/get.rs`): about 2.3 MiB over TLS while `get_result` holds them, and one `ETag` copy at the body's end, within the 2.7 MiB. A registered bundle's uncharged heap is at most 1 KiB. Test, each server trusted through `extra_roots` and reached through `resolve` (2.11's *Test hooks*): the counting allocator on a 100 MiB download over TLS from a server that offers h2, which must negotiate HTTP/1.1, on connections to 300 host names from a server that sends a maximal chain, an OCSP staple and 8 session tickets of 64 KiB on each (the heap flat across them), on a server that sends, over TLS 1.2, a Certificate of empty entries and one of one-byte entries, an OCSP staple, a ServerKeyExchange whose signature fills it and a CertificateRequest of one-byte names, each at the 64 KiB limit, and over 1.3 that Certificate and CertificateRequest, on a staging and a store request at those caps, one with a non-ASCII path, on a staging request through five same-origin redirects with 16 KiB `Location`s and through five relative ones with non-ASCII paths, on a redirect whose `Location` of about 100 KiB needs percent-encoding (`redirect_refused`, unparsed), in a unit test of the service's hyper half, generic over its IO, over an in-memory stream that fills every read hyper offers from a whole 129 KiB head, so the head completes in the read that passes 128 KiB (`head_too_large`), on one TLS 1.2 connection whose server sends the Certificate of a leaf the test root issued, then one-byte entries, then a 1xx and a head of 100 custom-named fields with a non-standard reason phrase, each near the 128 KiB cap and sent in records of about 12 KiB, and a chunked body that ends in a trailer section near 128 KiB, and on that response over `http`, on a head GET whose head is near the cap with `x-amz-meta-*` fields, and one whose `ETag` is near the cap, failing the 100-byte check only after `object_store`'s copies, and on a view of `max_view_bundles` bundles. The parsed spec is measured at run time (`C_spec`); 5.6's example estimates it at 4 times its JSON, which a spec of short ids and long `deps` lists passes (about 11 times).
- **A4.** A registry entry costs about 300 B per CAB, from core 3.13's list, not measured. It sizes `D`; the budget enforces whatever it is. While a member loads, its rows are staged (CR1): the list and every row's charges until its commit (core 3.13), about 240 B a row more for rows of distinct new keys, more for a row whose key an earlier row of that load took (a copy and a share) or one already registered (its `lowered` name, beside the slot it replaces, still charged), so the points where a load near `D` is refused moved, in no one direction (a load may be refused where it fit before CR1, and a retry may fit where the refused first attempt's registrations no longer stay; core 3.13). The version's budgets hold whichever way; `D`'s 256 MiB dwarfs the change.
- **A5.** Measured on the corpus, in aggregates, for 2.2's cost: on both full builds (2026-11-11), build A (4100), 11,556 bundles and 1,772.6 MB, and build B (100056), 11,501 bundles and 1,317.7 MB; and, where marked, on the 551/545-input sample of each (2026-11-09):
  - views, in the 551/545 sample: 324 of each build's sample inputs have no dependency; the mean view is 2.1 to 2.5 bundles (1.8 to 2.0 in a full build), the 99th percentile 6 to 10, the largest 22. On the preview diff below, 1,830 of the 1,869 inputs have dependencies;
  - holders: a full build has about 335 distinct dependencies, about 190 of them held by one view, and 4 held by 741 to 3,516 views each; per sample build the top 4 hold 80 % of the (input, dependency) pairs;
  - blocks: 23,060 of the 23,061 bundles of both full builds and UnityPy's samples are one block, nearly all LZMA. Those 23,061 are G1's 23,062 inputs (`docs/plans/core.md` section 6) less one of UnityPy's 5 samples, which is not a bundle;
  - registration's peek, asked in every attempt before the table (2.6 step 4): on a member of one block it decoded the member whole, so it cost the every-member-reached worst case, to the byte. Against a run that decodes each bundle once (about 5.2 GB a full build on build A, 4.3 GB on build B), it added +93.4 GB (17.9 times) on build A by direct views, and on build B +103.6 GB (24.1 times) by direct views and +255.8 GB (59.6 times) with the closure, every view taken by `m_Dependencies` (direct: the bundle and the bundles its `m_Dependencies` names; closure: their closure); by the builds' manifest dependency lists each figure is at most 2 % more. The largest view's peeked dependencies came to 87.5 to 89.4 MB by `m_Dependencies` and 116.0 to 128.4 MB by the manifest lists, within 5.6's example `D` of 256 MiB;
  - on a first consumer's preview diff (build B to build A: 1,869 inputs, its production task count; views by `m_Dependencies`, direct and closure alike), the per-attempt peek added 71.5 GB, 28 times the inputs' own decoding (2.55 GB): about 113 to 135 s of wall time at 8 workers, at a single-core LZMA rate of 66 to 79 MB/s, where the whole run was expected to take 30 to 60 s;
  - the peek table (2.6 step 4): one peek per bundle of a view that staged whole, per run; on this corpus about the decode-once work, an estimate, not a bound (a peek is bounded by its own `4 M`): about 5.2 GB and 4.3 GB a full build, and on the preview diff the inputs' 2.55 GB and their dependencies' bytes once. The interim's per-attempt peeks (2.6 step 4) add nothing here: no bundle repeats a key in its directory, no direct view holds one key in two members, and no bundle has a nested container, on both builds and the diff; in general each is a decode of its member's block in every attempt whose view holds it;
  - at version 1's reach (the NGUI cutter's `_MainTex` name), on views by `m_Dependencies` (2026-11-11, both builds and the preview diff, UnityPy 1.25.2 agreeing on every atlas and every sheet outcome): 694 atlases in 690 inputs on build A, 693 in 689 on build B and 660 in 656 on the preview diff each read one dependency block to get one name, the `_MainTex` texture's `m_Name`, reached through the atlas's own Material (no material is in another bundle). Two blocks are crossed a run, each its bundle's one LZMA block: one of 67 to 69 MB, read by 686, 685 and 652 inputs, and one of 2.1 MB, read by 8, 4 of which read the first too. That decodes +47.6 GB on build A, +46.2 GB on build B and +45.3 GB on the preview diff: 9.1 and 10.8 times the decode-once work, and 17.7 times the diff inputs' own 2.55 GB. The larger block decodes at 168 to 170 MB/s on one core, 2.1 to 2.6 times the build-wide 66 to 79 MB/s (it is mostly RGBA32 sheets), and the 2.1 MB one at 331 MB/s, so the reads, each block at its own rate, cost 280, 275 and 266 s of CPU (the smaller block's share 0.05 s), 33 to 35 s at 8 workers, measured, not scaled from the sample. Every one ends in `sheet_not_found`: the name matches none of the input's own Texture2D objects, and step 2's try of the atlas's name fails too. The read parses each crossed block's CAB entry, the larger block's being its first 0.18 to 0.26 MB (the `_MainTex` object ends 0.02 to 0.10 MB in; the rest of the block is the `.resS`; the 2.1 MB block's entry was not measured), so a decode cut as CR9 cuts (section 6) would cost about 0.12 to 0.18 GB a build. With the peek table it is the only per-attempt decoding left on the corpus: no `m_Script` or Sprite sheet leaves its bundle, and the interim's peeks find nothing (above);
  - the distinct dependencies of a full build decode to 262 to 276 MB; the sample's to 119 to 189 MB.
  - the texture export (section 8): at most 50.54 MiB of export peak a texture and 87.66 MiB of export work an input, on both builds; the largest input holds 124.6 MiB at its export, below its decode peak;
  - the candidate bytes `S`, each file claimed at `max(4 KiB, its size rounded up to 4 KiB)`: about 1.91 GB a full build (the texture PNGs about 1.84 GB, measured, the rounding adding 2.6 %; atlas JSON 1.1 MB, exact; atlas crops about 18 MB, estimated, within 10 to 97 MB; Sprite PNGs about 0.6 MB, measured (section 8); the records files at their floor of 4 KiB an input) and about 0.35 GB on the preview diff (its texture PNGs about 0.32 GB). Member records and failure records are not counted, and the crops' bytes are estimates until that path is built. At 5.6's example, with `max_export_work` at 2 x `M`, the spool rule asks about 18 GiB a full build and 7 GiB on the diff (5.6).
  Not measured (section 8): the atlas path's export peak and work (the sprite path's is, 2026-11-11), and validation's time.
- **A7.** With E57's settings, glibc's arena retention keeps peak RSS within a few percent of the live heap. The core's rules do not bound fragmentation, and a memory cgroup also counts the spool's page cache. The nightly measures both.
- **A8.** Version 1 configures no typetree provider: the spec has no field for one, and the base context's chain is empty. The corpus's typetrees are embedded; whether every NGUI atlas MonoBehaviour's is, is checked by the nightly (its `no_typetree` count).
- **A9.** Staging is a small part of a run on the corpus, so finishing it before extraction costs little (2.1).
- **A10.** The MonoBehaviour script hook (`Environment::object_tree`) dereferences `m_Script` inside the core. It matters only for stripped MonoBehaviours with a script provider, which version 1 does not configure.
- **A11.** The spool, the output directory, every local source, and stdout and stderr where they are regular files are on local filesystems whose calls complete, and nothing but the engine writes the spool while it holds the spool's lock. Network filesystems are out of scope in version 1. A filesystem that stops answering can hold a thread in a call Linux cannot interrupt, past `T_hard` (2.11). A call that completes can still take minutes. Measured (2026-11-10, a 200-byte line appended to a regular file on the spool's filesystem beside a writer filling it, in a memory cgroup): on a disk capped at 20 MB/s one such write was held 225 s, for as long as the writer kept the cgroup over its dirty limit; on a fast SSD the most was 1.4 ms. The dirty limits are the host's global one and the memory cgroup's, each across filesystems (`balance_dirty_pages`' global and memcg domains, each counting its dirty pages on every filesystem), so a file on another filesystem than the spool's is held too, and so is a write in a cgroup with no dirty pages of its own while the host is over its limit; the measurement tested the same filesystem only, in a cgroup. So a regular-file stdout or stderr can hold a line's write for minutes, and no path that must end by `T_hard` waits on one (2.11, *A held stdout*); any other buffered write of the engine's own can be held as long, which can cost the head its try but not hold the process past `T_hard`, since `_exit` ends a write the engine's own throttle holds (2.11, *What cannot be bounded*). Two waits `_exit` cannot end are a file's lock that another process holds while throttled: an engine write to a regular file another process also writes waits uninterruptibly on its inode lock (ext4's `inode_lock`, XFS's `IOLOCK_EXCL`, overlayfs's own inode's), and on XFS a buffered read of a local source another process is writing waits alike on `IOLOCK_SHARED`. Version 1 assumes neither: a caller that needs the deadline gives the engine a stdout and a stderr no other process writes, and sources no other process is writing (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 10; 2.11, *What cannot be bounded*).
- **A12.** The core keeps no state that one thread's call leaves for another's, except the budget, the provider chain and, under CR8's test feature, its refusal counters and its span-read table, which it only writes, and the load ids it draws from one process-wide counter and the load-name table, which only key that table; and, under that feature (`testing.rs`, the whole module), the thread-locals `IN_FALLIBLE` (the fallible helper's mark) and `VISITING` (`span_reads`'s reader), each restored by its guard when the call that set it returns, unwinding included. Its non-test thread-locals: in `limits/meter.rs`, the pool-refusal count (read as a difference) and the grant frames (each lazy computation opens and closes its own); in `env/registry/dependency.rs`, `RETRYING`, `RETRIED` and `RUNNING` (restored or cleared by their guards when the outermost `with_room` returns, unwinding included); in `env/registry/mod.rs`, `LOAD_KEYS_HERE` (read as a difference, `env/container_index.rs`). So what an earlier attempt on the same worker left in them changes nothing. Tests: `an_inputs_records_do_not_depend_on_what_runs_beside_it` at `threads` 1, where every attempt follows another on one thread; `the_core_has_no_unlisted_thread_local` (a source scan for `thread_local!` outside `#[cfg(test)]`, and for a `static` with interior mutability outside CR8's feature, against this list).
- **A13.** Under preflight's resource-limit, overcommit and memory checks, the allocator rarely refuses an allocation within 5.1's bound. When it does (a cgroup that refuses mappings, `vm.max_map_count` reached), the counting allocator sees every refusal of a Rust allocation, absorbed or not, zlib's included (its `zalloc` is Rust's allocator, 2.6 step 10), and the run stops (2.7). The two it cannot see are liblzma's C `malloc` refused (a) on a dependency's meter, which `with_room` takes for a pool refusal and retries after eviction, and (b) on any meter, outside `with_room` too (a direct read on a dependency's handle; not in a peek, whose budget never refuses, section 1), after a budget refusal absorbed earlier in the same core call, which reads as a budget refusal (`codec/lzma.rs`, `failure`; `limits/vec.rs`, `grow_counted`): each can decide a record by the host's memory without a trace, D's third limit (section 1). glibc's own allocations are unseen too, but their refusals surface as errors under D's first two limits (2.7). CR2, or an `lzma_allocator` the core routes through Rust's allocator, removes both. An allocator refusal in an infallible allocation (an uncharged collection, engine code, tokio, the TLS stack) aborts the process (`SIGABRT`: from 2.11's step 4, exit status 134 after its resets; as init before step 4, 139, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.1), with the head left `running` and no `exit` line. Its message goes to fd 2: to the writer's own description or `/dev/null` (2.11), or, for a socket stderr that is full, in a write that may wait until `T_hard`, or, for a regular-file stderr, in a write writeback can hold for minutes (A11), until `T_hard` too, since `_exit` ends the engine's own throttled write, but not a wait on the file's inode lock that another process's throttled write holds (2.11, *What cannot be bounded*). The lifecycle thread, which makes `T_hard`'s exit, allocates nothing after its start (2.11): it reads no stdin, the stdin watch, on its own thread, building and masking its one stderr line in a buffer allocated on the main thread at step 4, and its wakes are atomic stores and eventfd writes, never a channel, a lock, an unpark or a tokio call, so no refusal can abort on that thread and hold the hard exit. A panic on that thread, which needs a bug, allocates its payload, and a refusal of that allocation aborts on the thread that owns `T_hard`, its message's write to a full socket stderr then ending nothing. That module and the masker deny clippy's `panic`, `unwrap_used`, `expect_used`, `indexing_slicing` and `arithmetic_side_effects`, so the thread has no panicking construct of its own; a panic in std or in code it calls (a `copy_from_slice` given unequal lengths, an `assert!`) needs a bug, and is accepted. **Test:** `extract.md` X10 counts, by the `tally` hook's per-thread count (2.11), read by a test thread while the `exit_now` point holds, in a child that drives the engine through its library API, zero allocations on the lifecycle thread after its mark: in three stopping runs, a stdin watch that fires, a deadline stop and a signal stop, once the lifecycle trace FIFO shows `lifecycle_stop_woken reached`, and in a run whose stdin watch ends with no stop (a connected UDP socket as stdin, 2.11), once its stderr line has arrived, the controller held until then, each wait under 2.11's `trace` row's requirements, R3 among them; that count replaces a refusal case there.
- **A14.** The caller keeps the duties of `BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.2 (one attempt of a `run_id` at a time, no writer of its keys but its attempts, the stated wait before a retry), and the store's LIST shows a write once it is acknowledged, as S3 does. The head's guarantee rests on both; verification checks the second for this attempt's own writes.
- **A15.** `object_store` 0.12.5's behaviour is read from its source (`extract.md` section 3, item 1; 2.9). The store's responses are checked (2.9): a 2xx body past the connector's cap, a LIST page past its scan, an `ETag` over 100 bytes, an upload id over 1 KiB and a LIST's continuation token over 1 KiB each fail where 2.9 says, and no `Content-Length` sizes an allocation. What remains assumed is `hyper`'s: it never sends a request's bytes twice within one try.
- **A16.** The spool directory's `st_blksize` is its filesystem's allocation unit. Where the unit is larger (ext4's bigalloc, ZFS's record size), claims undercount, and `ENOSPC` can come below `size_bytes`: still `spool_full`, a run stop, so B and D hold. A copy-on-write spool makes 2.9's failed outcome `pwrite` after a rename reachable.
- **A17.** The host's resolver returns within its configured timeouts and attempts. An NSS module that hangs is out of scope in version 1, as a filesystem that stops answering is (A11).

(A2, A3 and A6 were design round 1's assumptions about the shared cache, and went with it.)

---

## 8. Open, for the review rounds

**Final export-work default (B7c).** `max_export_work` stays `2 × M`, final.
Measurements through the full export paths, including records and member
work, give a maximum of about 139.8 MiB on build A, at a texture input;
build B is just under it, about 137.3 MiB. The p99 is about 10.6 MiB.
At `M = 256 MiB` the default has about 3.7 times the measured headroom;
at the measured minimum `M` near 140 MiB, about twice. Keeping the default
proportional to `M` also fits larger inputs that larger-`M` runs admit.
The default is a bounded-work policy with explicit overrides, independently
of B12's provisional memory constants. Synthetic v1 goldens may be recorded.

1. **Sealing.** Its cost is measured with registration's peek, which design round 22 computes once per run (2.2, 2.6 step 4, A5). Beside the staged files (2.5), the peek table is the one state an attempt reads that another thread writes, and both are written before the first attempt is dispatched (2.10). A review should look for any other state an attempt reads that another attempt can write.
2. **Staging** copies every local bundle into the spool (2.5). Design round 4's review found that nothing reads a source after its copy.
3. **Overlapping staging with decoding** is given up (2.1). A later version may start an attempt once its view has staged.

**Measurements on the corpus**, both full builds: build A (4100), 11,556 bundles, 1,772.6 MB, and build B (100056), 11,501 bundles, 1,317.7 MB. Results:
- **Texture2D formats** (2026-11-10, UnityPy 1.25.2 over every Texture2D of both builds): 0 of 44,309 are outside E15's five (BC7 about 50 %, DXT1 33 %, DXT5 11 %, RGBA32 4 to 5 %, RGB24 1.6 %), so a consumer's cutover loses none to `unsupported_format`. 263 have a side of 0 (`empty_image`). All are on the Windows standalone platform.
- **CPython's JSON encoder** (2026-11-10, the oracle's CPython 3.11, recursion limit 1,000): `json.dumps` at `indent=2`, and at `indent=None`, reaches 994 nesting levels from a module-level caller. So X5's reference has about 480 frames of headroom over `max_value_depth` (512), and does not reach the limit.
- **Dirty-page throttling** (2026-11-10, a 200-byte line appended to a regular file on the spool's filesystem beside a writer filling it): yes. On a disk capped at 20 MB/s one write was held 225 s, while the spool's writer kept the cgroup over its dirty limit; on a fast SSD the most was 1.4 ms. E4's spacing stays limited to pipes, FIFOs, sockets and terminals, and no path to `T_hard` waits on such a write (2.11, A11).
- **The sealing cost and the dependency peak, with registration's peek** (2026-11-11, from both builds' blocks and directories, with views by `m_Dependencies` and by the builds' manifest dependency lists, and a single-core LZMA rate taken over their distinct dependencies): the peek, asked in every attempt, decoded every view member whole, 17.9 to 59.6 times the decode-once work by `m_Dependencies`, and 28 times the inputs' own decoding on a first consumer's preview diff; the largest view's peeked dependencies came to 87.5 to 89.4 MB by `m_Dependencies` and 116.0 to 128.4 MB by the manifest lists, within the example `D`. Design round 22 computes the peek of each bundle of a view that staged whole once per run, into a table the attempts read (2.2, 2.6 step 4, A5, 5.5).
- **The texture export's peak and work** (2026-11-11, the texture path built with `unityrs-texture` and `unityrs-imageio`, over every Texture2D of both builds, each charged on a meter of its own in the engine's order, 2.6 step 10): build A has 22,258 Texture2D, 22,083 exported, 175 `empty_image` and no error; build B 22,051, 21,963 and 88, no error. A texture's export peak is at most 50.54 MiB on both builds (RGBA32 2048 x 2048 with no mips: the source 16 MiB, the image 16, the PNG output at `deflateBound` 18.1, deflate's state 0.39, the rows 0.04), its p99 9.94 MiB and its median 1.00 MiB on build A and 0.99 on build B; its export work at most 22.54 MiB, p99 5.07, median 0.27. Over the inputs with a texture, an input's export work is at most 87.66 MiB on both builds, its p99 11.14 MiB on build A and 11.21 on build B, its median 0.29. The largest input holds 124.6 MiB at its largest texture's export, below its 145.2 MB decode peak, and no other input passes 54 MiB. On one core, decoding runs at 916 MB/s of pixels (BC7 521) and encoding at 23.8 MB/s (BC7 17.6), so the export takes about 326 s of CPU a build, 97 % of it PNG encoding, about 41 s at 8 workers. So 5.5's `export_X` takes the measured form, `M`'s example stays 256 MiB, and `max_export_work`'s default is finally 2 x `M` (5.5, 5.6).
- **The NGUI reach and the candidate bytes** (2026-11-11, both builds and the preview diff, on views by `m_Dependencies`): the cross-bundle `_MainTex` reads decode +47.6 GB on build A, +46.2 GB on build B and +45.3 GB on the preview diff, 266 to 280 s of CPU at each block's own rate (168 to 170 MB/s for the larger), 33 to 35 s at 8 workers, and every one ends in `sheet_not_found`, so on this corpus they buy no output (2.2, A5). No `m_Script` or Sprite sheet leaves its bundle, and the interim's peeks (2.6 step 4) find nothing, so it is the only per-attempt decoding left on the corpus. The candidate bytes `S` come to about 1.91 GB a full build and 0.35 GB on the diff, in claimed bytes, the crops' and Sprites' estimated (A5); with `max_export_work` at 2 x `M`, 5.6's spool rule asks about 18 GiB a full build and 7 GiB on the diff (5.6).
- **The sprite path's export peak and work** (2026-11-11, the `sprites` kind built in `unityrs-export`'s attempt, each bundle an input on a view of itself, `M_X` a meter that keeps its high-water mark, 2.6 step 10): build A and build B each have 80 Sprites in 25 inputs, all 80 exported, none skipped, every image RGBA, and no sheet outside its bundle. An input's sprite export peak, above what its attempt held when the export began, is at most 2.20 MiB on both builds, its median 0.53 MiB; its export work (the sheets', merges' and images' pixels, and each PNG at its 4 KiB blocks) at most 1.32 MiB, its median 0.18 MiB, the largest pixel buffer a 1.00 MiB sheet. Both are far below the texture path's (above), so they move neither `M`'s example nor `max_export_work`'s final default (2 x `M`). Measured again after each fold of the build's review that could move them (the atlas lookup's walk, its name index and its counted work; a sheet's file identity from its own rows; the objects read as UnityPy constructs them; the merge's, answer 11's and the texture's order): every figure the same.

- **Phase 1's time and peak** (B1's parser, release build, one test thread: `crates/unityrs-batch/tests/measure.rs`, 2026-11-11 for review round 2's fold and 2026-11-12 at each later budget, and `crates/unityrs-batch/tests/slowest.rs`, 2026-11-12, for review rounds 3 and 4; each with the peak `p` above the main thread's live count at the parse's start, the 64 MiB text included). The host: an Apple M1 Max (8 performance and 2 efficiency cores, 12 MiB of L2 a performance cluster, 64 GiB), running an aarch64 Linux VM of 10 vCPUs and 32 GiB, natively; the scattered shapes run twice and the rest at least once, the VM's load under 1.5 at each start. The largest legal specs: 63.1 MiB of plain bundles in 0.6 s, `p` 118.1 MiB, `C_spec` 35.1 MiB; 63.7 MiB of `deps`-dense bundles in 0.8 s, `p` 196.5 MiB, `C_spec` 46.3 MiB. The views' check (2.3 rule 4): a dense set at the budget's most (its inputs' views reading 2^24 ids in all) in 0.9 s, `p` 206.6 MiB; 100,000 such inputs, the shape that took 840 s before the budget, refused `view_size` at the 2nd in 1.4 s, `p` 220.3 MiB (2026-11-12, at 2^24). Review round 2's slowest shape is a view of 65,535 members scattered among 2,000,000 bundles, each member's list one to 37 ids, the first of them the next member in ascending order of place: at 2^30 7.7 to 21.6 s, at 2^28 2.6 to 5.9 s, at 2^27 1.7 to 4.4 s, at 2^26 1.2 to 3.3 s, the most with eight ids a member whose ids share their first five bytes; a dense set at the budget in 1.8 s at 2^30 and 1.2 s at 2^28; the walk at the budget beside the slowest-parsing rest of a 64 MiB document (hosts of four `xn--` labels at 243 bytes, hosts with an uppercase letter, unique dependencies, `deps` lists outside every view) in at most 2.1 s at 2^30 and 1.6 s at 2^28, that rest alone in 0.8 to 1.5 s. Review round 3's shapes (2026-11-12, two runs each, the VM's load under 1.5 at each start): the same scattered walk over id-only bundles, the most a 64 MiB spec holds (4,705,960 beside a ring of 65,535 members, fewer beside longer lists or with ids sharing five bytes), at 2^28 in 5.3 to 16.9 s, at 2^27 in 3.8 to 9.4 s, at 2^26 in 3.1 to 6.0 s, the most a ring whose ids share five bytes, `p` at most 405.3 MiB, each id read about 55 ns in the ring; 1,363,124 `prefer` entries naming 100,000 inputs beside 1,900,000 id-only dependencies in 1.0 s, `p` 227.3 MiB; the bundles' index at its largest (id-only unique dependencies, one of them listed) in 1.9 s, `p` 410.6 MiB; a 64 MiB unknown member's name of `/` or of `~`, which its pointer writes in two bytes each, in 0.1 s, `p` 320.0 MiB, the refusal keeping its 128 MiB pointer, which the `exit` line cuts to 1,024 bytes. Review round 4's shapes (2026-11-12, two runs each at every budget, the VM's load under 1.5 at each start), each id read's cost its time less that of the same document whose inputs list nothing, per id: id-only rings of 65,535 members whose order of listing is a fixed pseudo-random permutation of their places, so no stride prefetch or sequential read of the walk's targets helps; one ring among 4,705,960 bundles, 10.9 to 12.2 s at 2^26 (135 to 152 ns an id; the same ring in ascending order 4.9 to 5.6 s, 47 to 55 ns); 8 rings spread among 4,168,741, 13.0 to 13.1 s (164 to 167 ns); 34 disjoint rings over all 2,257,129 bundles, the inputs cycling among them, 16.7 to 19.0 s (222 to 254 ns), the slowest; with ids sharing five bytes, fewer bundles fit and the sort's fixed cost is 2.3 to 4.8 s, the walk 106 to 174 ns an id, at most 14.7 s. Each id costs about the same at every budget, so the time falls with it: the slowest at 2^25 in 9.7 to 10.3 s, at 2^24 in 5.8 to 6.0 s (the disjoint rings; 5.7 to 5.9 s the spread rings whose ids share five bytes), at 2^23 at most 4.9 s; `p` at most 404.8 MiB. 220,764 `url` bundles of 32 one-character headers each, accepted in 1.6 s, `p` 230.8 MiB, `C_spec` 166.8 MiB; a 64 MiB run of digits under an unknown member, in 0.16 s, `p` 184.0 MiB (`float_roundtrip` buffers the digits), refused `syntax` as an integer past the largest double and `unknown_field` as a fraction; 2,796,185 halfway decimals (`2.4703282292062327e-324`) under an unknown member in 0.12 s, `p` 64.1 MiB. So the budget is 2^24, and the slowest phase 1 measured is 6.0 s. The worst 64 MiB documents, legal or not, by shape: zeros as an array at the top, under a known field or under an unknown member, 64.1 MiB (an unknown member's value is read for `syntax` alone); escaped one-character `deps` ids, 65.0; empty `deps` lists, 64.9, and 325.2 in full lists; escaped ids, 342.0; a 60 MiB `url` or `endpoint`, non-ASCII, of spaces, after an uppercase letter or with its slashes escaped, 60.1 to 120.0 in at most 0.05 s, refused before the URL parser; dependencies of an id alone, 463.1; unique dependencies of an id alone, 378.6; empty `prefer` entries, 297.2, and 219.6 in 3.2 s where their keys share five bytes. So `p` is at most 463.1 MiB on the documents measured, under 512 MiB, and the slowest of them takes 3.2 s. No progress line is written during phase 1 (E4), and the signals step 4 handles stay blocked through it (2.11 step 1), so a stop sent during it is seen at step 4, at most the slowest phase 1 measured after it, 6.0 s on this host; `shutdown_grace_ms`'s minimum is 10,000 for that reason (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.1), which leaves a stop held that long 4 s of the caller's grace, and the engine's own count of the grace, from step 4, is unchanged (2.11 step 3). An amd64 container under emulation on the same host (Rosetta), run beside other work, is no x86 measure, but gave the same peaks in review round 2's fold, the slowest phase 1 then in 7.7 s at 2^28 (19.6 s at 2^30) and the slowest document in 6.0 s.

Still to measure:
- publish time from the corpus machine to stores in two or more regions, against a baseline client that keeps a pool: every store request makes a full TCP and TLS handshake, since no client keeps an idle connection (2.5) and resumption is off (2.11 step 4), so each PUT and part pays its round trips. A pool comes back only as the HTTP service's own, with a connect limit that a term and a W row count. This needs the owner's stores.

**Planned optimization: a shared pre-decoded dependency cache.** Not in version 1. It keeps D and B in this form:
1. **Selection per bundle.** Whether a bundle Y is pre-decoded is a function of Y's own spec entry and bytes alone: a per-bundle flag (`"predecode": true`), and all of Y's blocks decoding within the pre-decode meter's limits. Never a rule over other entries ("in some view", "what fits after the others"): either would make Y's path, and so the records of every input that reads Y, Y itself included, depend on bundles outside their views.
2. **All or stop.** When the selected set's decoded total passes the pool, the run stops before extraction (exit 5). It never falls back per bundle.
3. **The pool inside `I`.** Pre-decode runs sealed, one bundle per budget. Each `Decoded`'s retained bytes stay charged to a run-level pool budget of limit `P` until extraction ends; its decode's transients (one file's last growth and its dictionary, 5.5) go on a per-bundle meter of limit `M`, released when it returns (CR6). At most `floor((I - P) / M)` bundles pre-decode at once, so the transients fit under `I - P`. Validation becomes `P + M + D' <= I`, where `D'` is an attempt's Dependency budget for parse state and for the blocks of any bundle nested in a pre-decoded one, which keeps its own compressed blocks and is decoded in each attempt. Attempts read the pool's `Bytes` slices without charge, so their leak check still reads zero. The joined data grows on the per-bundle meter and moves to `pool` once, at its final size, with the `Decoded` tables; liblzma's unseen refusal on that meter is D's third limit in this mode too (A13).
4. **Equality across modes is not promised.** A `"shared"` and a `"sealed"` run may differ under tight budgets; D holds within each mode, since the mode is in the spec.

It needs CR6 (section 6). A full build's distinct dependencies decode to 262 to 276 MB (measured, 2.2), so a pool of 1 GiB holds them. It saves the extra decoding of 2.2's cost that the peek table leaves, the NGUI cutter's reads, not the per-attempt parse. Those reads, measured at +47.6 GB on build A, +46.2 GB on build B and +45.3 GB on the preview diff, 266 to 280 s of CPU, 33 to 35 s at 8 workers, are the only per-attempt decoding left on the corpus (2.2), which is what "not in version 1" weighs: the cache would make them one decode of each crossed block a run, and CR9 (section 6), a smaller change, about 0.12 to 0.18 GB a build.

**Follow-ups** (earlier rounds' LOW findings not fixed in this text):
- The core's evict-and-retry for direct reads on a dependency's handle (3.12), which would let a read fit that a full cache refuses today.
- `{container}` in key templates (`extract.md` E27).
- A spec field for the manifest's location (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 7.1).
- `PARITY.md`'s IDAT chunk size, `max(65536, 4 x width)` (`extract.md` E19): settled in P1b. `PARITY.md`'s PNG bytes row says it, from Pillow 9.5.0's source (`src/PIL/ImageFile.py:513`), `test_rows_extractor.py` png_bytes pins it at width 16500 on the oracle, and `unityrs-imageio`'s fixtures at widths 16384, 16385 and 20000.
- An optional prune of keys under the output root that the manifest does not list.
- The NGUI atlas's material field name (`material`, or `mMat` in some versions): settled in P1b as `material` (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 5), measured on a corpus of two builds of one game, where every NGUI 3 atlas has the field (in one build, set on 737 atlases and null on its 769 reference atlases; in the other, 735 and 765). Still to do: name a component atlas (`UIAtlas`) by its GameObject, so it can join `script_classes`' default.
- A `PARITY.md` row, before X2 meets it, for UnityPy's sprite-sheet cache, keyed by path id alone, which aliases two sheets of one path id in different CABs that one sprite file reaches; the engine's groups keep them apart (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 4.4).
- Honouring `HTTPS_PROXY` and `NO_PROXY`; a private CA for a self-hosted store, through a future spec field naming a CA file (version 1 trusts only `webpki-roots`, `BATCH_SPEC.md` 1.2).
- A literal `control_char` in a template (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 2.1): validation leaves it to each expansion, whose key the attempt refuses (`unwritable_key(control_char)`); a static check would refuse the template at validation.
- Decided (B2): the exit-code split. An *expected skip* (`BATCH_SPEC.md` 9.3) is one whose object reads correctly and that version 1 leaves without an output by its fixed scope or by a rule about the object's own content or name: `unsupported_format(<name>)`, `unsupported_platform`, `ngui_layout_unsupported`, `empty_rect`, `empty_image`, `duplicate_name` and `reference_atlas`. It sets no code, so a run whose only skips are expected exits 0, `complete`; 3 stays for a skip whose data could not be read, resolved or decoded, an `unsupported_format(<decimal>)`, `no_typetree`, `unnamed_atlas` and the keys the caller's templates make unwritable or collide, and `counts.skips_expected` counts the expected ones apart (7.3). 9.3 gives every object skip its class, by this test, so a new reason is classed by it too. On real builds reference atlases alone, hundreds a build, would otherwise make every run exit 3, and 3 would stop meaning "review the skips".
- B7c executes the temporary local normal-run subset (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 10); unsupported execution capabilities remain 78 `usage` before mutation.
- B2 catches a panic on the engine's command paths (`unityrs batch ...`, `unityrs --version`) and exits 70 with no `exit` line (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 10). No path B2 builds can panic without a test hook, and B2 adds none, so the test is B9's, with the panic hook and the `point` hook (2.11's *Panics*, *Test hooks*).
- Deleting a run's spool directory after a normal end with an empty journal, as an option.
- LISTing only the winners' key range for resume and verification, instead of the whole prefix.
- Refreshing temporary credentials during a run, instead of the caller's duty to give ones that outlive it (E37).
- X's registry slot parses an entry of X a second time when a lookup through the registry reaches it (charged to `D`); sharing X's own handle's parses would remove that.
- Decided (design round 24): version 1 keeps the reference's cross-bundle name read, the NGUI cutter's `_MainTex` name through the material (`BATCH_SPEC.md` section 5). A rule that a `_MainTex` outside the input's own files gives no name would remove that decode, and on this corpus change no outcome, its figures changing as CR9's do (2.2), but it would change records wherever a crossed texture shares a name with one of the input's own textures, and version 1's records are measured against the reference's. CR9 (section 6), wanted and not a prerequisite of version 1, cuts its cost instead, changing, by estimate, no outcome on the corpus; its figures change: `dependency_peak`, the `inputs` rows' `ms` and the head's `vm_hwm`. For each of the 686 inputs (685 on build B) crossing the larger block, the crossed member's share of its `dependency_peak` falls from about 145 MB to about 9 MB (its 8.1 MB file, read whole, plus under 1 MB of prefix and transient, a cut-sized dictionary assumed), and its figure from about its view's staged member bytes (a median 13.5 MB, at most 17.3 MB) plus 137 MB (135 MB) to about those bytes plus 1 MB, the four a build crossing only the 2.1 MB block not measured for room; the head's figure falls from about 154 MB (152 MB) to about 18 MB by `m_Dependencies` views, or to 25 to 31 MB by the manifests' dependency lists, all estimates (section 6).

## 9. Before it is built

- This plan's review rounds, run until a round finds no MEDIUM or HIGH problem: done, through design round 21 (2026-11-10). Still to do before the build: the design's PR (design round 38, round 37's review folded as wording, has no review round of its own), and publish time to stores in two regions (section 8, with the owner's stores). The texture path's export peak and work are measured (section 8). Validation time and full export-work measurements are recorded in section 8; `max_export_work` is final at `2 × M`.
- CR1 to CR5 and CR7 answered. CR1, CR5 and CR8 have landed (core 4.1's *a failed dependency load publishes nothing*; core 3.15's provider cache bound; core 4.1's *CR8's counters cost a build without the feature nothing*), so their interims no longer apply: X13, X10's attempt-level `refuse` cases and X11's count of registration's reads can be built. None of the others is needed for correctness; each has its interim. CR6 waits for the planned cache. CR9 is wanted, not a prerequisite.
- `extract.md`'s gate (its section 5) agreed. It holds the engine's tests named here: D (2.10), budgets and charging, the deadlock guard and scans (section 4), the stop path (3.15), and the memory rows (5.8).
